- `2026-10-27` [[readmemd-faircslmtrainbench-at-main]] — README.md · FAIRC/SLMTrainBench at main — SLMTrainBench (FAIRC, HuggingFace, v1.0.0, 2026) is the measurement dataset behind 'When Peak Floating-Point Throughput Misleads: Utilization and Cost Frontiers for Small Language Model Pretraining'. It reports batch-saturated single-GPU steady-state training-step throughput (AdamW state zeroing + BF16-autocast forward + shifted-token cross-entropy + backward + FP32 AdamW update; eager PyTorch 2.8.0 + OLM, torch.compile, no activation checkpointing, no gradient accumulation) for nine dense decoder-only models (150M/250M/350M/500M/700M/1B/2B/4B/8B) across ten GPUs and context lengths 512-32,768: 2,963 configurations, seeds 11/22 as timing replicates. THE LOAD-BEARING RESULT for this run, computed from benchmark_catalog.csv: on A100 80GB PCIe the 500M model does 26,297 tok/s at 4k (MFU 39.88%) and 11,990 tok/s at 32k (MFU 63.88%) - a measured per-token cost ratio of 2.19x against a FLOPs ratio of 3.51x, so a 1.60x MFU uplift absorbs 40% of the quadratic penalty. The pattern is systematic across sizes on A100: 150M 2.21x (MFU 32.9->62.4%), 250M 2.27x (35.1->62.7%), 350M 2.23x (37.0->63.2%), 500M 2.19x (39.9->63.9%); on H100 80GB HBM3 the same models give 2.31x/2.36x/2.35x/2.33x with MFU 21-27% -> 38-41%. The 1B model OOMs at 32k on A100 PCIe but gives 4k=15,757 tok/s (42.61%) and 16k=10,920 (55.42%), a 1.44x ratio for 4x sequence length. Fitting a two-component MFU model (attention vs non-attention) to the 500M A100 points gives attention at ~73% of peak and the rest of the step at ~21%, which reproduces the measured curve to within a point. Caveats: single-GPU, no context parallelism, no activation checkpointing, and the best batch-saturated config at each length (bs=8 at 4k, bs=1 at 32k), so this isolates the compute-scaling component of the long-context penalty rather than a distributed-training measurement.
- `2026-10-27` [[effective-learning-rate-governs-loss-dynamics-in-language-model-pretraining]] — Effective Learning Rate Governs Loss Dynamics in Language Model Pretraining — Measured run-to-run loss variation used as an internal calibration. 'Repeating the same configuration while changing only the initialization seed or data-order seed yields variations of 1.11x10^-2 and 1.62x10^-2' - pairwise mean absolute trajectory discrepancy for Llama-124M on FineWeb with AdamW, batch 128, sequence 1024. That is 0.011-0.016 nats of seed-induced loss variation at 124M, which the paper contrasts with ELR-collapse errors of 1.8-2.6e-3 and with 'the 10^-2-scale improvements regarded as meaningful in LLM optimizer studies'. It also states plainly that 'There is no configuration-independent threshold for when two pretraining loss trajectories differ meaningfully', while noting optimizer studies routinely use 1e-2-scale loss differences. This is a second, independent measured small-scale noise floor in the 0.01-nat range - 6-10x below a 3.5%-relative (0.1-nat) floor.
- `2026-10-27` [[published-as-a-conference-paper-at-iclr-2025-2]] — Published as a conference paper at ICLR 2025 — PolyPythias (ICLR 2025; arXiv 2503.09543): 9 new seeds x 5 Pythia sizes (14M/31M/70M/160M/410M), ~7k checkpoints, varying parameter-initialisation seed and batch composition. Finding: 'per-size accuracy is generally consistent across seeds', with only two outlier model-seed combinations in the whole suite - 410M seed 3 and seed 4, which also show training loss spikes (Fig. 5). So at 410M, 9 seeds produce a stable distribution with 2/9 outliers, and the outlier mechanism is loss spikes rather than ordinary seed noise. The extracted text does not print a final-loss SD; its stability metrics are accuracy, inter-seed agreement (Cohen's kappa) and validation-loss z-scores. Useful as (a) a 410M-scale multi-seed receipt and (b) evidence that the tail risk at small scale is spikes, not a wide Gaussian noise floor.
- `2026-10-27` [[github-quixiaiopen-gpu-kernel-modules-p2p-support-for-nvidia-consumer-cards-gith]] — GitHub - QuixiAI/open-gpu-kernel-modules: P2P support for nvidia consumer cards · GitHub — QuixiAI/open-gpu-kernel-modules (3,609 words) — a fork that enables BAR1 P2P on NVIDIA consumer cards (RTX 3090 GA102, 4090 AD102, 5090) by forcing the GH100 BAR1-P2P HALs on, resizing BAR1 to cover the framebuffer, and defaulting RMPcieP2PType to BAR1. [T2 community measurement + code, 8x RTX 3090, PCIe 4.0 x16.] This is the strongest counter-evidence to cc-6's 'P2P is a latency lever, not a bandwidth lever': on 8x RTX 3090 (EPYC Rome, four root complexes, PCIe 4.0 x16) direct peer copies run at 23-26 GB/s versus ~2 GB/s per GPU through host staging, and 'NCCL all-reduce bus bandwidth reaches 24.7 GB/s versus 2.7 GB/s on NCCL's no-P2P SHM transport — a 9x collective-fabric jump'. So when BAR1 covers the whole framebuffer AND the link is wide, P2P IS a bandwidth lever. The gating mechanism is the same one the CMP 170HX hits: kbusIsPcieBar1P2PMappingSupported_GH100 requires static BAR1 spanning the framebuffer, so BAR1 must be resized (32 GB on a 3090; 64 GiB on an unlocked 170HX). It also documents that the stock 610 driver already ships native BAR1 P2P for supported chips, and that no userspace changes are needed (stock 610.57.04 userspace). Caveat: GA102/AD102 at Gen4 x16, not GA100 at Gen2 x4 — the 9x ratio does not transfer to a 2 GB/s link, and the club-170hx 4-card receipt measures the CMP case directly (2 GPU +31%, 3 GPU -9%, TP4 -25 to -44%).
- `2026-10-27` [[i-tested-the-cmp170hx]] — I tested the CMP170HX — bittide.aicompass.dev 'I tested the CMP170HX' (1,055 words) — a 4x CMP 170HX (GA100, 70 SM, 64 GB each, 150 W) llama.cpp serving report on PCIe Gen2 x4 with no NVLink, cards mounted through a single x16-to-4x4 M2 bifurcation adapter. [T2 community measurement on this exact hardware at this exact width.] The load-bearing line for the fabric question: 'the transfer rate is 800MB/s at Gen 1 and 1.6GB/s at Gen 2' — i.e. the measured Gen2 x4 link rate on these cards is ~1.6 GB/s, which is the hard ceiling any 5-rank ring all-reduce must live under (busbw <= link rate; algbw <= 0.8 x link rate at N=5, so algbw <= ~1.0 GB/s). Serving numbers: 1 card gpt-oss-120B MXFP4 78 tg / 1,244 pp; 2 cards 85 tg / 1,870 pp; 3 cards DeepSeek V4-Flash 0731 Q4_K_XL ~29 tg / ~365 pp at 1M ctx; 4 cards same model 29 tg / ~450 pp; dense >30B is a 'dead end'. No NCCL/all-reduce measurement — this is a link-rate datapoint, not a collective receipt, so it bounds the fabric but does not by itself settle NCCL efficiency at 5 ranks.
- `2026-10-27` [[quantifying-variance-in-evaluation-benchmarks]] — Quantifying Variance in Evaluation Benchmarks — 10 Llama-2-7B-architecture models trained from scratch on 210B tokens, identical except the initialisation seed, plus ~280 models/checkpoints. Defines benchmark seed variance as the SD of a metric across seeds and compares it to bootstrapped 95% CIs and across-setup variance. The focus is downstream benchmark variance (MMLU etc.), not final training loss; it finds benchmark-level seed variance is often smaller than choice-of-setup variance and that framing choice tasks as completion tasks reduces variance. Relevance here is indirect: it establishes that at 7B/210B the seed-induced spread on downstream metrics is measurable but modest, and that single-seed evaluation is unreliable at benchmark level.
- `2026-10-27` [[github-pzhao-engflashmla-github]] — GitHub - pzhao-eng/FlashMLA · GitHub — Community Ampere port of FlashMLA (branch `a100_replace`). Adapts the Hopper FlashMLA to Ampere with a warp-specialized SM80 kernel (`warp_spec=True`, 8 warps split 4 consumer QK^T+softmax / 4 producer loads, double-buffered K/V, raw PTX barriers for SM80). Scope is decode only: the only entry point is `flash_mla_with_kvcache(q, kvcache, block_table, cache_seqlens, dv, tile_scheduler_metadata, num_splits, causal=True)`, i.e. paged-KV decode, with no training/backward API. Performance is poor by the author's own admission ('performance of Ampere is currently poor due to register overflow'): up to 464 GB/s memory-bound and 59 TFLOPS compute-bound on A100 SXM vs 2,039 GB/s and 312 TFLOPS peak. This is the strongest candidate for an sm_80 sparse-MLA path and it is inference-only — it does not overturn cc-4(a).
- `2026-10-27` [[missing-sparse-backward-kernel-issue-107-deepseek-aiflashmla-github]] — Missing sparse backward kernel. · Issue #107 · deepseek-ai/FlashMLA · GitHub — deepseek-ai/FlashMLA issue #107 'Missing sparse backward kernel' (opened 2025-10-09, still open). The reporter states the library 'provides a complete forward and backward pass for dense attention, but the sparse attention implementation appears to be forward-only' and asks whether a sparse backward will be released. No maintainer answer; a community reply argues a sparse backward 'would take MUCH MUCH more amount of time to implement than using dense backwards' because it needs atomic accumulation over misaligned K/V. Confirms from the upstream project's own tracker that no sparse-MLA backward exists in FlashMLA — the only sm_80-adjacent DSA forward has no training counterpart.
- `2026-10-27` [[quantifying-pretraining-variance-data-initialization-and-floating-point-arithmet]] — Quantifying Pretraining Variance: Data, Initialization, and Floating Point Arithmetic — Spencer Frei — Measured variance decomposition for LM pretraining at 184M parameters. 23 runs of a 12-layer, d=1024, 16-head nanodo transformer on C4 for 28,055 steps (~3.7B tokens, 20 tokens/param, Chinchilla-optimal), bf16, on TPUs with bitwise determinism verified across data centres. One factor varied at a time: 5 init seeds, 5 data seeds, optimizer perturbations, FP-arithmetic/sharding variants. THE LOAD-BEARING NUMBER: 'At the final step, the 23 runs span the range [2.941, 2.953]-a maximum deviation of 0.4% from the overall mean of 2.946. Within-group standard deviations are <=0.004 for every category; FP arithmetic is even tighter at 0.001.' So the measured seed-to-seed SD of FINAL VALIDATION LOSS at 184M/3.7B is <=0.004 nats, i.e. <=0.14% relative - roughly 25x below the 3.5%-relative floor the pipeline borrowed from the scaling-law-estimation literature. Function-space divergence is large (per-token symmetric KL ~0.19-0.23 nats between seeds) even though aggregate loss is nearly identical, and FP-arithmetic-order variance is within 20% of data/init-seed variance. Caveat: single model family, single dataset, 3.7B tokens, personal blog not peer-reviewed; the loss spread is measured at one budget, not across budgets.
- `2026-10-27` [[opt2024-16th-annual-workshop-on-optimization-for-machine-learning]] — OPT2024: 16th Annual Workshop on Optimization for Machine Learning — Scaling Collapse (Minder et al., OPT 2024 workshop). Trains a 3-block next-pixel-prediction transformer ladder on CIFAR-5M, D=768-2048, 12M-79M params, 5 seeds per size. Defines cross-seed fluctuation sigma_D(t) as the SD of the reducible loss curve as a fraction of its average final value, and reports 'we can only predict the reducible loss curve of any one model to a relative error of 1%'. So the measured cross-seed relative fluctuation at 12-79M params is ~1% - below the 3.5% floor but above the 0.14% measured at 184M on a language task. Caveat: pixel-regression task, not LM; tiny models; the 1% is a trajectory-level relative error, not a final-loss SD.
- `2026-10-27` [[a-13-hour-training-experiment-on-a-pc-workstation-why-optimization-begins-with-m]] — A 13-Hour Training Experiment on a PC Workstation: Why Optimization Begins with Measurement — Second-hand write-up of Zach Mueller's AI Council 2026 talk 'Optimizing Model Training End-to-End: A Tiny MoE Case Study': a 4x RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell Max-Q workstation on PCIe 4 with no NVLink and no InfiniBand, training a Qwen3-style MoE with 500M total / ~330M active params on ~10B tokens at ~100k tokens per microbatch and gradient_accumulation=10, i.e. an effective global batch of ~1M tokens — the same batch regime as the CMP 170HX program. Measured: 4-GPU DDP gave only ~2.6x over single-GPU (not 4x); on the PCIe 4 bus 'gradient communication accounted for 50% of the single-step time, while matrix multiplication took up only about 20%'; a single All-Reduce took ~0.08 s and ~101,000 microbatches accumulated ~2.26 hours of gradient sync. Applying DDP.no_sync() so the all-reduce fires only on the 10th accumulation step cut wall-clock from <18 h to 13.2 h. This is a direct measured demonstration that at a 1M-token effective batch, DDP's per-microbatch 50% communication share collapses to roughly 5% of total step time via gradient accumulation — supporting the pipeline's 'DDP overhead at 1M tokens is small' position — on a no-NVLink PCIe fabric, albeit PCIe 4 (~25 GB/s) and a 500M model. Tier: T2 community report, second-hand from a talk.
- `2026-10-27` [[how-to-train-long-context-language-models-effectively]] — How to Train Long-Context Language Models (Effectively) — ProLong (arXiv 2410.02660). Recipe for long-context pretraining: Llama-3-8B base, 64K context, 5B tokens for ablations; full run 20B tokens stage 1 (2.2K H100 hours) + 20B tokens stage 2 (12.2K H100 hours). Table 15 gives measured throughput per device on 8xH100 with FSDP at 64K: 64K full attention = 2,770 tok/s/GPU; variable-length attention 2,780 (+0.4%); + minibatch reordering 3,095 (+11.7%). At 2,770 tok/s/GPU for an 8B model at 64K the implied FLOPs rate is 4.18e14/s = 418 TFLOPS = 42% MFU of H100 bf16 peak - consistent with the FLOPs model; no 4k baseline is reported. Also documents that variable-length attention's speedup is negligible under FSDP unless minibatches are reordered by sum of squared document lengths.

- `2026-10-27` [[ep-to-tp-rewriting-blackwell-gpu-wiki]] — EP-to-TP rewriting - Blackwell GPU Wiki — Community wiki (Blackwell GPU Wiki) on parallelism planning for no-NVLink workstation GPUs. States the mechanism cc-3 needs for EP: expert parallelism 'demands a token all-to-all every layer. On a system without NVLink and without P2P atomics, that all-to-all goes through the host bridge and tanks performance', while TP needs only per-layer all-reduce and 'NCCL ring all-reduce works tolerably over PCIe and the host bridge, especially with NCCL_P2P_LEVEL=PIX'. Claims rewriting an EP plan as a TP plan 'often eliminates 80%+ of communication overhead with no kernel-level work required'. Bandwidth arithmetic at H=8192, N=4, top-k=8: EP ~16x8x8192 ~= 1 MB per layer of cross-GPU traffic vs TP ~12 KB per layer, a ~80x difference. Scope: inference/serving engines (vLLM, sglang, TRT-LLM), not training; no measured numbers, no hardware receipt. Tier: T3 community guidance, not a measurement.
- `2026-10-27` [[pcie-110-gpu-스케일링]] — PCIe 1~10 GPU 스케일링 — Vendor whitepaper measuring DDP vs DeepSpeed ZeRO-2 scaling on a 10x RTX 3090 host (Supermicro X12DPG-OA6, 24 GB each, 2 NUMA nodes, GPU 0-4 on NUMA0 / 5-9 on NUMA1) where `nvidia-smi topo -p2p r` reports GPU0-GPU9 = CNS (Chipset Not Supported), i.e. no P2P, and all traffic crosses CPU/system memory with ~3 GB/s of PCIe shared by all 10 GPUs. This is the only DDP-vs-ZeRO-2 A/B on a no-P2P PCIe fabric found in this gap-fill, and it points AGAINST the cc-3 concern: ZeRO-2 is faster than DDP at every GPU count — 1 GPU 27.5 vs 27.4 samples/s, 2 GPUs 31.4 vs 29.3, 4 GPUs 36.3 vs 28.6 (+27%), 8 GPUs 59.4 vs 39.6 (+50%), 10 GPUs 52.6 vs 47.9 (+10%). Scaling efficiency is poor for both (10 GPUs = 1.91x ideal, 19%), and the DDP column is non-monotonic (27.4 -> 29.3 -> 28.6 -> 39.6 -> 47.9), consistent with a small model where batch effects dominate. Model size and sequence length are NOT stated anywhere in the report, so the >=1B/32k bar is not met. Multi-node: 1 node 10 GPU DDP 47.9 s/s, 2 nodes 20 GPU 73.3 (1.53x); DeepSpeed 51.6 -> 82.8 (1.73x).
- `2026-10-27` [[github-deepaksatnadistributed-llm-training-benchmark-framework-production-ready]] — GitHub - deepaksatna/Distributed-LLM-Training-Benchmark-Framework: Production-Ready Performance Analysis for PyTorch DDP, FSDP, and DeepSpeed on Oracle Cloud Infrastructure · GitHub — DDP/FSDP/DeepSpeed ZeRO benchmark framework run on Oracle Cloud Infrastructure with 4x NVIDIA A10 24GB where each GPU is on a SEPARATE NODE (1 GPU per node) and the interconnect is standard Ethernet (eth0) for A10, RDMA/InfiniBand only for A100/H100. DeepSpeed ZeRO-2 at 4 GPUs reaches 18,147 tok/s, +48% vs DDP, while ZeRO-2 at 2 GPUs is -24% vs DDP and ZeRO-3 at 4 GPUs is -31% vs DDP; FSDP scales 1.39x from 2 to 4 GPUs. Because there is no intra-node PCIe GPU-to-GPU path at all, this measures a network fabric, not a host-staged PCIe one, and is therefore SILENT on cc-3's fabric question — but it is a second independent datapoint that ZeRO-2 can beat DDP rather than pay a communication penalty.
- `2026-10-27` [[readme_enmd]] — README_EN.md — liumorrisclaw/cmp170hx-glm53-lab README_EN.md (3,396 words) — a 4x CMP 170HX rig that negotiates Gen2 x4 on all four slots (confirmed across cold power cycles), no P2P by default, and later gets P2P working (108/108 content check) via cmpunlocker patches. [T2 community measurement on this exact hardware at the query's actual link width.] Three load-bearing results: (1) the quantified x4-vs-x16 gap on identical software (1.6.0, both TP4, host-shm all-reduce, P2P off): c1 structured 284.2 vs 396.0 tok/s (-28%), coding 229.1 vs 306.2 (-25%), prose 136.4 vs 192.5 (-29%), 8-user aggregate 447.2 vs 798.6 (-44%) — 'the aggregate gap widens to 44% as all-reduce payloads grow with batch size'; KV pools differ by 0.9% (1,081,579 vs 1,072,150), ruling out software differences. (2) At Gen2 x4 P2P is a pessimization: TP4 gains +3-6% single-stream but loses -4.4% aggregate; PP4 turns negative even single-stream (-19.1% aggregate); c8 aggregate -13.6% with P2P; 'at Gen2-x4 bandwidth no row shows a strict win' and 'P2P gains are bandwidth-dependent: +10% on x16, negative on narrow links'. (3) The x4 link is not software-solvable: 0.72 (link) x 0.78 (layout) ~= 0.56, matching the 45% total gap to the x16 reference rig. Also: ECC unlocked in cmpunlocker v0.5 at <=0.3% perf cost (integration-level, no fault injection); clock locking is a no-op (vBIOS voltage curve); PP4 keeps its prefill/aggregate advantage on narrow links (x4, even x1) while TP4 needs all-x16 to reach published numbers.

- `2026-10-27` [[why-is-the-bandwidth-utilization-of-7-gpus-much-lower-than-that-of-6-8-gpus-for]] — Why is the bandwidth utilization of 7 GPUs much lower than that of 6, 8 GPUs for tasks with a specific total parameter amount? · Issue #97 · NVIDIA/nccl-tests · GitHub — NVIDIA/nccl-tests issue #97 (Nov 2021) — the only authoritative statement found that odd GPU counts are pathological, and the mechanism behind it. [T2 expert-opinion from an NCCL maintainer.] Reporter measured all-gather at a fixed total parameter count (10,660,224) across 4, 5, 6, 7 and 8 GPUs and found 'the bandwidth utilization of 5, 7 GPUs will be much lower than others'. NCCL maintainer sjeaugey: 'With 5 or 7 GPUs the alignment of the offsets is probably not great (e.g. 4 or 8 bytes instead of 16 bytes, or even down to 1 byte if using an int8 datatype).' This corroborates the Tartan/TPDS warning that 5-GPU PCIe rings are pathological, but supplies NO GB/s figure — it is a mechanism confirmation, not a measurement, and it does not distinguish a 5-rank penalty of 1.6x (ring-load factor) from the 3-5x the cc-2 falsifier requires. It also implies the penalty is payload-shape dependent: a 1M-token global batch's gradient bucket sizes are chosen by the framework, so alignment can be engineered away (pad buckets to 16-byte multiples) in a way that a fixed-total-parameter all-gather cannot.
- `2026-10-27` [[inconsistent-p2p-bandwidth-and-nccl-allreduce-bus-bandwidth-issue-814-nvidianccl]] — Inconsistent P2P bandwidth and NCCL allreduce bus bandwidth · Issue #814 · NVIDIA/nccl · GitHub — NVIDIA/nccl issue #814 (comaniac, Mar-Apr 2023) — a 6x RTX 4090 host-staged all-reduce receipt, the closest committed artifact to a 5-rank no-P2P measurement. [T2 community measurement, 6 ranks.] Command: NCCL_P2P_DISABLE=1 mpirun -np 1 all_reduce_perf -d half -b 1M -e 64M -f 2 -g 6 on a 6x4090 box whose topology mixes PHB and SYS links; the thread never states the link generation, but its p2pBandwidthLatencyTest P2P-disabled matrix shows 20-22 GB/s unidirectional per pair, i.e. PCIe 4.0 x16-class links (inference, not a quoted spec). Results (out-of-place algbw/busbw): 1 MiB 2.04/3.39; 2 MiB 1.70/2.83; 4 MiB 1.64/2.74; 8 MiB 1.54/2.56; 16 MiB 1.54/2.57; 32 MiB 1.48/2.47; 64 MiB 1.45/2.42; 'Avg bus bandwidth: 2.70867'. So a 6-rank host-staged all-reduce on a fast PCIe fabric lands at ~2.4-2.7 GB/s busbw / ~1.45-1.5 GB/s algbw — 14-27x above the cc-2 falsifier of 0.1 GB/s algbw. The reporter also posts an all-pairs all_reduce_perf matrix (busbw 2.61-4.50 GB/s between pairs) and p2pBandwidthLatencyTest matrices with and without --sm_copy (P2P writes 9.6-10.2 GB/s, P2P disabled 20-22 GB/s, device-to-self 911-922 GB/s). NCCL maintainer sjeaugey's diagnosis: 'That's unfortunately a common problem, in particular with AMD CPUs... NCCL needs to communicate in both directions; it's not just a copy. This tends to decrease performance, even more than 2x... With 6 GPUs and an allreduce you have 6 flows interleaving on the CPU' — i.e. the CPU, not the link, is the binding resource for host-staged collectives at N>=6. Caveat: PCIe 4.0 x16-class with P2P disabled, not Gen2 x4; the CPU-bound regime does not transfer directly to a 2 GB/s link, where the link binds instead.
- `2026-10-27` [[exampledeepseek-v32-adaptive-threads-for-sparse-mla-backward-by-butterfingrz-pul]] — [Example][DeepSeek-V3.2] Adaptive threads for sparse MLA backward by Butterfingrz · Pull Request #2592 · tile-ai/tilelang · GitHub — TileLang PR #2592 (merged 2026-07-22), adaptive thread count for the sparse MLA backward kernel. Identifies this kernel as 'the sole hotspot of the GLM-5 DSA (DeepSeek Sparse Attention) training backward path (`SparseMLA.autograd.Function`), computing sparse MLA gradients for the latent geometry (DQKV=576, DV=512, topk=2048)'. Profiling is on H200 (sm_90) — `ncu` shows L1/TEX ~=81-83% SOL, DRAM <1%, occupancy ~=12.2%, memory-pipe bound; the fix raises in-flight cp.async bytes via 8 warps and yields ~3.6x kernel-only bwd speedup on sm_90 Hopper. No sm_80/A100 path, no sm_80 benchmark. Confirms the only open-source DSA training backward is TileLang-on-Hopper.
- `2026-10-27` [[nemo_automodelcomponentsmodelsdeepseek_v4kernelstilelang_sparse_mla_bwd-nvidia-n]] — nemo_automodel.components.models.deepseek_v4.kernels.tilelang_sparse_mla_bwd | NVIDIA NeMo AutoModel — NVIDIA NeMo AutoModel API docs for `tilelang_sparse_mla_bwd` — the DeepSeek-V4 sparse MLA backward, implemented in TileLang, with `preprocess` (row-wise delta), `bwd` (B, S, S_kv, H, D, topk, block_size=32), `postprocess`, and a `sparse_mqa_bwd_interface(q, kv, attn_sink, o, do, topk_idxs, lse, ...)` taking int32 top-k indices and fp32 LSE from the forward. Confirms a production-grade DSA-family training backward exists in NVIDIA's stack, but it is TileLang (wgmma/SM90-class) and carries no sm_80 claim. Relevant to cc-4 as a 'the backward exists, but not on Ampere' datapoint.
- `2026-10-27` [[published-as-a-conference-paper-at-iclr-2026]] — Published as a conference paper at ICLR 2026 — FSA: An Alternative Efficient Implementation of Native Sparse Attention Kernel (Yan, Jiang, Chen, Mai, Chen, Yuan; ICLR 2026; arXiv 2508.18224). This is the measured end-to-end training comparison cc-4 said did not exist. Setup: NVIDIA H20 (148 TFLOPS, 4 TB/s) and H200 (989 TFLOPS, 4.8 TB/s) interconnected by NVLink at 450 GB/s; BF16 training; Llama3-8B, Qwen3-14B, Qwen2.5-32B at 32K and 64K sequence lengths; baselines are the Triton NSA kernel (fla-org) and 'an efficient Triton-based Flash Attention kernel (Triton, 2024)' — i.e. Triton FA, not CUDA FlashAttention-2. Measured end-to-end TRAINING: FSA up to 1.25x / on average 1.09x vs NSA, and up to 2.47x / on average 1.86x vs full attention. Dividing the two averages implies NSA ~= 1.71x full attention end-to-end on average — inside cc-4's 2x threshold. Kernel level: FSA up to 3.5x (avg 1.8x) vs NSA on H20 and up to 2.9x (avg 1.4x) on H200; FSA up to 6.4x (avg 2.4x) vs full attention on H20 and up to 4.9x (avg 2.3x) on H200 — so vanilla NSA's own kernel advantage over full attention is only ~1.3-1.6x on average. The decisive sentence: 'vanilla NSA lags behind full attention in many tested cases, even with its sparse attention mechanism. For example, when the sequence length is 32K, one GQA group contains one query head, NSA consistently falls short of full attention, while FSA demonstrates superior performance than full attention.' Caveats: H20/H200, not A100; Triton FA baseline (slower than CUDA FA2, so NSA-vs-FA2 would be even smaller); authors are the FSA authors, a competing implementation; peer-reviewed at ICLR 2026.
- `2026-10-27` [[feature-add-an-opt-in-sm80-fallback-for-deepseek-sparse-attention-dsa-on-nvidia]] — [Feature] Add an opt-in SM80 fallback for DeepSeek Sparse Attention (DSA) on NVIDIA A100 · Issue #35420 · sgl-project/sglang · GitHub — SGLang issue #35420 (opened 2026-08-19, closed inactive) requesting an opt-in SM80 fallback for DeepSeek Sparse Attention on A100. Establishes that 'SGLang currently has no end-to-end CUDA path for running DeepSeek Sparse Attention (DSA), including DeepSeek-V3.2, on NVIDIA A100 / SM80' with two independent blockers: (1) the DSA indexer's `DSAPagedMQALogitsBackend.AUTO` selects DeepGEMM on CUDA, and the paged-MQA logits implementation is unavailable on SM80; (2) the BF16 DSA decode path selects FA3 on pre-Blackwell GPUs, and SM80 FA3 does not support DSA's asymmetric head dims (QK 576 = 512 nope + 64 RoPE, V 512), failing with 'RuntimeError: Only Hopper supports different V headdim'. The proposed fix is explicitly inference-only — a chunked Torch sparse-MLA reference for correctness, then SM80 Triton paged-MQA logits and a BF16 Triton sparse-MLA DECODE kernel with online softmax. No backward, no training path. Confirms cc-4's 'no sm_80 DSA training path' as of Aug 2026.
- `2026-10-27` [[github-relaxed-system-labflash-sparse-attention-efficient-implementations-of-nat]] — GitHub - Relaxed-System-Lab/Flash-Sparse-Attention: 🚀🚀 Efficient implementations of Native Sparse Attention · GitHub — Flash-Sparse-Attention (FSA), the official implementation of arXiv 2508.18224 (ICLR 2026), an optimized Triton NSA kernel. Explicitly tested on 'NVIDIA Ampere or Hopper GPUs (e.g., A100 SXM, H20, H100 PCIe, H100 NVL, H100 SXM, H200 SXM)', fp16/bf16, head dim <=256, GQA group sizes 1-16, and 'Training and inference (prefill)' — the README's usage example calls `loss.backward()` through a 65,536+32,768-token variable-length batch, so this is a runnable sm_80 NSA-family TRAINING path (requires flash-attn==2.6.3). This answers cc-4's third falsifier: 'run NSA instead' is NOT itself a Hopper-only porting project. Its kernel claim is that NSA's selected-attention kernel wastes work padding GQA groups below 8, so FSA reorders the loop nest (KV blocks outer, queries inner) into main+reduction+online-softmax kernels.
- `2026-10-27` [[github-fla-orgnative-sparse-attention-efficient-triton-implementations-for-nativ]] — GitHub - fla-org/native-sparse-attention: 🐳 Efficient Triton implementations for "Native Sparse Attention: Hardware-Aligned and Natively Trainable Sparse Attention" · GitHub — fla-org's Triton implementation of NSA (the reference implementation the NSA paper's own efficiency section is built on), with full NSA training support via `flame` (2025-03-15), an online top-k selection kernel that avoids materializing the attention matrix, a fused selected+sliding Triton kernel, and variable-block-count support. Ships `parallel_nsa` with autograd (q/k/v require_grad) and `benchmarks/benchmark_nsa.py`. Its published benchmark table is the independent check on the paper's 6.3x/3.4x at 32k: at T=16,384, nsa 20.165 ms vs flash 53.634 ms = 2.66x forward, nsa_bwd 116.298 ms vs flash_bwd 204.354 ms = 1.76x backward; at T=8,192, 9.954 vs 13.465 = 1.35x fwd and 49.575 vs 52.567 = 1.06x bwd; at T=4,096, 4.870 vs 3.520 = 0.72x fwd (NSA SLOWER) and 23.083 vs 14.193 = 0.61x bwd (NSA SLOWER). So this reimplementation reproduces a shrinking advantage well below the paper's headline ratios at every length it reports, and is a net loss below ~8k. Triton, so arch-portable to sm_80 in principle; the repo does not state a tested A100 receipt.
- `2026-10-27` [[simple-linear-attention-language-models-balance-the-recall-throughput-tradeoff]] — Simple linear attention language models balance the recall-throughput tradeoff — Based / simple linear attention (arXiv 2402.18668). Measures INFERENCE throughput (prefill and generation) against recall quality for 360M-1.3B models; reports up to 24x higher generation throughput and faster prefill than FlashAttention-2 at 4k. Contains no training-step throughput measurement, so it is off-topic for the long-context TRAINING cost question this gap asks about.
- `2026-10-27` [[readmemd-3]] — README.md — PixelML/club-170hx results/2026-10-01-cmp170hx-4card-bar1-p2p-plx/README.md (606 words) — the prose receipt for static-BAR1 P2P on 4x CMP 170HX behind two PLX switches, and the companion to the notebook that carries the NCCL tables. [T2 community measurement on this exact hardware.] Verdict: direct GPU-to-GPU access works on all 12 ordered pairs (every byte verified, 5.79 GB/s each, 16 us at 4 KiB), requiring the memory unlock's 64 GiB BAR1 on every card plus a hand-programmed PCIe layout applied before driver load (the BIOS placed switch A's 64-bit window at the top of CPU2's 1 TiB aperture at 224 MiB, so the second card on a switch got no BAR1 and static BAR1 refused to start it with -EIO; fix is to program every BAR and bridge window by hand with each BAR3 directly below its 64 GiB-aligned BAR1, then kexec). P2P cuts small-copy latency by up to 1.77x and lifts Qwen3.8-27B TP2 single-user decode 11-14%, but SLOWS GLM-5.3-Flash TP4 decode 25-44%: 'the host-staged all-reduce vLLM picks without P2P is faster on this PLX + Broadwell path.' Receipts listed include receipts/nccl/ (nccl-* = SHM, nccl-p2p-* = P2P/CUMEM) — but those log files are NOT present in the repo tree, so the notebook's rendered tables are the only committed NCCL numbers. Limits: only the CPU2 switch pair tested (the CPU1 switch board's link was down); GLM numbers use Morrowmake's default DFlash2 drafter; the layout script hard-codes this board's bus numbers.
- `2026-10-27` [[transformers-are-ssms-generalized-models-and-efficient-algorithms]] — Transformers are SSMs: Generalized Models and Efficient Algorithms — Mamba-2 / SSD (arXiv 2405.21060). Establishes state-space duality and a block-decomposition algorithm for structured state-space models. Relevant to this run only at kernel level: SSD 'is faster than FlashAttention-2 starting at sequence length 2K' and the paper reports attention-vs-SSD crossover curves, but it contains no full-training-step throughput-vs-sequence-length table, so it cannot supply a per-token training cost ratio. Kept as background for the attention-vs-linear-architecture axis, not as evidence on long-context training cost.
- `2026-10-27` [[2026-10-01-cmp170hx-4card-bar1-p2p-plx-cudaipynb]] — 2026-10-01-cmp170hx-4card-bar1-p2p-plx-cuda.ipynb — PixelML/club-170hx notebook 2026-10-01-cmp170hx-4card-bar1-p2p-plx-cuda.ipynb — the primary receipt for NCCL collectives running over BAR1 P2P on four CMP 170HX (GA100, 64 GiB BAR1 each after the memory unlock, Gen2 x16 per card, two PLX PEX 8747 switches, dual Xeon E5-2686 v4, 180 W, driver 615.71.09 + upstream cmpunlocker @88e39ce + rebar-serialize.patch + the four BAR1-P2P patches from admunch888/cmpunlocker, RMForceStaticBar1=1;RMPcieP2PType=1). [T2 community measurement on this exact hardware.] NCCL all-reduce (bf16 sum, torch 2.13.0+cu130 / NCCL 2.29.7, busbw = algbw x 2(n-1)/n) at 256 MiB: 2 GPU SHM 3.69 GB/s busbw (72,736 us) vs 2 GPU P2P/CUMEM 4.83 GB/s (55,536 us) = +31%; 3 GPU SHM 4.24 GB/s (84,389 us) vs 3 GPU P2P 3.86 GB/s (92,732 us) = -9%, i.e. P2P is WORSE at three ranks. At 1 MiB: 2 GPU SHM 2.67 vs P2P 3.31 GB/s; 3 GPU SHM 3.05 vs P2P 2.90. Copy bandwidth inverts above 16 MiB: 5.79 GB/s P2P vs 6.27 GB/s host-staged at 256 MiB; latency wins only at small sizes (15.8 vs 22.0 us at 4 KiB, 194 vs 343 us at 1 MiB, 0.92x at 256 MiB). Workload level: Qwen3.8-27B W4A16 TP2 single-user decode 71.8 -> 80.0 tok/s (+11%, cross-switch) and 71.5 -> 76.8 (+8%, same-switch), but GLM-5.3-Flash TP4 decode LOSES 25-44%: 19.4 ms/step with P2P off vs 26.1 ms with P2P on, because TP4 issues about a hundred small collectives per step. THIS IS THE DECISIVE cc-6 ARTIFACT: a working NCCL all-reduce over BAR1 P2P at 4.83 GB/s busbw exists on a CMP 170HX, so the '64 MB BAR1 makes BAR1 P2P impossible' reading is superseded once the unlock supplies a 64 GiB BAR1; but the gain is rank-count- and size-dependent and reverses at N>=3 and in real TP workloads, consistent with 'P2P is a latency lever, not a bandwidth lever'. Caveats: Gen2 x16, not x4; 2-3 ranks, not 5; the referenced receipts/nccl/ log files are NOT committed to the repo (the tables above are the notebook's rendered outputs); one run per cell, no error bars.
- `2026-10-27` [[skyladder-better-and-faster-pretraining-via]] — SkyLadder: Better and Faster Pretraining via — SkyLadder (arXiv 2503.15450, NeurIPS 2025). Context-window scheduling for pretraining: 1B models up to 32K context and 3B models at 8K, 100B tokens. Table 8 is the load-bearing table: for 1B models, Random(8K) = 11.6e20 FLOPs and Random(32K) = 25.5e20 FLOPs (a 2.20x FLOPs ratio, exactly what 6N + 12LHQT predicts for a TinyLlama-shaped 1.1B/22L/2048 model), and the measured training-time saving from SkyLadder tracks the FLOPs saving within ~5% (32K: 77.8% of baseline time for 73.7% of baseline FLOPs; 8K: 86.9% time for 85.3% FLOPs). That is a measured confirmation that for a 1B model at 8K-32K, wall-clock scales with the PaLM FLOPs formula at roughly constant MFU.
- `2026-10-27` [[distributed-trainingreadmemd-at-main-cortwavedistributed-training-github]] — distributed-training/README.md at main · cortwave/distributed-training · GitHub — Reproducible DDP/FSDP/DeepSpeed benchmark harness run on a no-P2P PCIe fabric: 4x RTX 4090 24GB on Vast.ai, PCIe 4.0 x16, topology PHB/NODE, and `check_p2p.py` reports all 12 GPU pairs P2P DISABLED (GPU0->GPU1 247 ms/32 Gbps, GPU1->GPU0 76 ms/105 Gbps asymmetric, GPU0->GPU2 233 ms/34 Gbps cross-NUMA). The FSDP comparison at batch 1/GPU is the closest thing found to a ZeRO-2-vs-DDP A/B on a host-staged fabric: DDP 6.90 samples/s (fwd 0.033 s, bwd 0.423 s, opt 0.128 s; 21.1 GB/GPU), FSDP SHARD_GRAD_OP ~= ZeRO-2 4.13 samples/s (fwd 0.323 s, bwd 0.643 s, opt 0.009 s; 15.8 GB), FULL_SHARD ~= ZeRO-3 2.88, NO_SHARD 3.88. Step-time ratio DDP -> SHARD_GRAD_OP is 1.67x (+67% overhead), which exceeds cc-3's 50% overturn threshold. But the bar is not met: model and sequence length are NOT stated (21.1 GB DDP residency implies ~1.3B params at 16 bytes/param, but that is inference, not a stated figure), batch is 1/GPU so DDP's 0.033 s forward is launch-bound, and SHARD_GRAD_OP's forward is 10x DDP's (0.323 s) — the signature of a small-model all-gather-dominated regime, not a 32k/1B one. Also measures a separate 'bad topology' DDP run where 2 GPUs give 0.29x and 4 GPUs 0.91x of single-GPU throughput.
- `2026-10-27` [[usp-a-unified-sequence-parallelism-approach-for]] — USP: A Unified Sequence Parallelism Approach for — USP (arXiv 2405.07719). Unified sequence parallelism combining ring and Ulysses; 47% MFU on two 8xA800 nodes for Llama3-8B. Table 3 gives attention-module-only (fwd-only) throughput in iters/sec on 8xL20 PCIe for Llama3-8B shapes at 8K/32K/128K: best config 8K=454.93, 32K=62.75, 128K=5.48 iters/s (ulysses-degree 2, lb-ring), i.e. per-token attention cost rises 7.25x from 8K to 32K and 11.5x from 32K to 128K. Because this is the attention module alone (not a full training step), the ratio exceeds the full-step ratio: the non-attention FLOPs do not grow with sequence length. Table 4 repeats the benchmark on 8xA100-SXM4 NVLink.
- `2026-10-27` [[loongtrain-efficient-training-of-long-sequence-llms]] — LoongTrain: Efficient Training of Long-Sequence LLMs — LoongTrain (arXiv 2406.18485). Head-context parallelism (2D-attention) for long-sequence training; improves MFU by up to 2.88x vs Megatron-CP and 1.49-1.53x vs DS-Ulysses. End-to-end MFU of 7B-MHA and 7B-GQA on 64 GPUs (Table 3) ranges 0.360-0.518 across 128K-1M sequence lengths; Ring-Attention's MFU is 'less than 10%' at 128K for MHA and reaches 19.6% (GQA) / 40.6% at 1M. So at 128K-1M on 64 A100s, long-context MFU for the best system is 36-52% - comparable to short-context MFU - which again implies the quadratic FLOPs penalty is paid in wall-clock rather than amortized away. No 4k baseline is reported.
- `2026-10-27` [[deepspeed-ulysses-system-optimizations-for-enabling]] — DEEPSPEED ULYSSES: SYSTEM OPTIMIZATIONS FOR ENABLING — DeepSpeed Ulysses (arXiv 2309.14509). Sequence parallelism via all-to-all on the head dimension: 'Communication reduction of over 10x compared to existing systems, resulting in throughput improvements of up to 2.5x, and sustained throughput of over 175 TFlops/GPU (over 54% of hardware peak)'. Measured on up to 256 A100s with 7B and 30B GPT models. Table 3 (weak scaling, GPT-7B dense, global batch 8): 65,536 tokens on 64 GPUs = 9,676.76 ms at 161.36 TFLOPs/GPU; 131,072 on 128 GPUs = 17,052.5 ms at 157.41 TFLOPs/GPU; 262,144 on 256 GPUs = 33,486.5 ms at 147.4 TFLOPs/GPU. Per-GPU TFLOPs is therefore nearly FLAT (161->157->147, -9%) as sequence length doubles twice, i.e. the quadratic attention FLOPs convert to wall-clock at roughly constant efficiency for a 7B model. Also reports a 1.3B GPT convergence run at 32K on 8 A100s with SP degree 4.
- `2026-10-27` [[ring-attention-with-blockwise]] — Ring Attention with Blockwise — Ring Attention (arXiv 2310.01889). Blockwise transformer ring-passing over K/V blocks across devices, enabling context length to scale linearly with device count (7B at 256k on 8xA100, 30B at 2048k on TPUv4-1024, 65B at 1024k). Evaluates MFU across model size and context length with FSDP/TP and 2M-token batches on 8/32xA100 and 4M on TPUv4-256, arguing MFU is maintained as context grows because communication is overlapped with blockwise computation. IMPORTANT CAVEAT: the extracted Table 4 (MFU vs model size/compute/context) lost its numeric cells in text extraction, so this note cannot supply a numeric MFU-vs-sequence-length curve; it supplies only the architectural claim that per-token cost stays flat per device when devices scale with context.
- `2026-10-27` [[perf-29-throughput-by-switching-from-deepspeed-to-ddp-issue-177-tensorautoopen-2]] — perf: ~2.9× throughput by switching from DeepSpeed to DDP · Issue #177 · TensorAuto/OpenTau — On-box A/B of DeepSpeed ZeRO-2 vs plain DDP on 8x A100 80GB (NV12 NVSwitch), pi05/PaliGemma+Gemma-expert ~3.4B params, accelerate harness. ZeRO-2 28.7 samples/s (step 3,909 ms) vs DDP 83.1 samples/s (step 1,155 ms) = 2.90x for DDP; after a bf16-Adam-state bug was fixed (PR #182) the same-hardware 4-GPU ratio is 1.96x (DDP fp32 30.2 s/s at 794 ms vs ZeRO-2 15.4 s/s at 3,648 ms). The mechanism is decisive for the cc-3 question: the gap is NOT bandwidth. `nvidia-smi topo -m` shows NV12/NVSwitch, NCCL init confirms P2P/CUMEM/read on 24 channels, an 8 GB bf16 reduce-scatter should take ~25 ms, and every communication-overlap knob (overlap_comm, contiguous_gradients, reduce_bucket_size 5e8, allgather_bucket_size 5e8, round_robin_gradients) produced zero improvement (3,071 -> 3,093 ms). Single-GPU backward is 641 ms vs 3,071 ms on 4-rank ZeRO-2; the ~2.4 s delta is CPU-side Python execution of DeepSpeed's per-parameter post-accumulation hooks stalling the next kernel launch. ZeRO-2 backward got worse from 4 to 8 GPUs (3,071 -> 3,442 ms). DDP installs bucket-granular hooks so host overhead is amortized. Implication for cc-3: ZeRO-2's 'exactly 2-Psi' byte identity does not imply DDP-identical wall-clock — but the failure mode here is host-side per-parameter hooks (worst on models with hundreds of small tensors, e.g. MoE experts), not the wire, so it is fabric-independent and would bite on a CMP 170HX box too.

- `2026-10-27` [[deepspeed-zero-vs-pytorch-fsdp2-measured-18x-on-nvlink-barely-finishing-on-pci-2]] — DeepSpeed ZeRO vs PyTorch FSDP2, Measured: 1.8x on NVLink, Barely Finishing on PCIe (Plus How to Actually Get GPUs on GKE) | Implicit None — Measured single-harness A/B of DeepSpeed ZeRO stages vs PyTorch FSDP2 on two interconnects — 4x A100 40GB (NVLink) and 4x L4 24GB (PCIe, no NVLink) — Qwen2.5-3B bf16, micro-batch 1, seq 2048, 4 ranks, 15 steps median. The PCIe run is the closest published analogue to a host-staged fabric: NCCL's share of kernel time rises from 17.3% to 50.2% for ZeRO-2, 26.1% to 46.7% for FSDP2 no-reshard, and 27.7% to 51.1% for FSDP2 reshard — communication becomes roughly half of kernel time once the wire is PCIe. Throughput on L4: ZeRO-2 1,923 tok/s (step 4,260.5 ms), FSDP2 no-reshard 1,761 (4,651.6), FSDP2 reshard 1,218 (6,723.9), ZeRO-3 2,290 (3,577.2), ZeRO-3+offload 1,405; on A100 ZeRO-2 17,124 and FSDP2 no-reshard 17,449. The ranking flips: on PCIe ZeRO-3 beats ZeRO-2 by ~19%, the reverse of NVLink. There is NO DDP baseline in the matrix (ZeRO-0 OOMs even on 40 GB at 30.01 GB resident before activations), so this cannot yield a ZeRO-2-vs-DDP step-time ratio. Caveats: L4 is PCIe Gen4 x16 (~25 GB/s), ~25x faster than a Gen2 x4 CMP 170HX link; the author states the NCCL kernel-duration share 'does not map one-to-one onto pure communication-wait time'; n=1; defaults-vs-defaults.

- `2026-10-27` [[interim-report-csa2-at-32k-sm80]] — Interim report — csa2-at-32k-sm80 — CSA2 at 32k on sm_80 is a kernel-porting project, not an experiment: the indexer's 64-128 GiB score tensor is a 32x-reducible artifact (StreamIndex measures 0.40 GB at 32k), but every training-capable DSA kernel is Hopper-or-newer (cuDNN SM90/SM100, FlashMLA now sm_100a/103a+Ascend with Hopper dropped, TileLang H800/H200, StreamIndex tested sm_90a only) and the only sm_80 path is vLLM's inference-only TRITON_MLA_SPARSE with no backward. LongCat's 0.83x speedup ratio at 32K means CSA2 would be a +10.7-14.7% step-time penalty even if it ran; NSA's measured 6.3x fwd / 3.4x bwd at 32k on 8xA100 implies a 4.02x attention-time reduction = -39.1% to -54.1% step time. Run NSA (MoBA as flash-attn-2.6.3 fallback), drop CSA2.
- `2026-10-27` [[interim-report-topology-cost-at-1m-tokens]] — Interim report — topology-cost-at-1m-tokens — DDP is not the only viable topology: for the repo's own 7B-total/1B-active MoE, DDP needs 112 GB/card (does not fit) and its all-reduce costs 252% of compute at 1M tokens (36% x total/active = 7x), FSDP costs 378% (1.5x DDP's bytes, not 2x), while PP=5 fits at ~25.8 GB/card and pays only a 4-14% bubble because its 134 MB/micro-batch activation hand-off is hidden inside a 243 ms compute window. EP is ruled out because no EP library runs without NVLink/P2P/RDMA (ThunderEP), not because of the repo's 72x estimate, which is ~6x once aggregate volume is divided by GPU count. TP is bandwidth-bound at training batch sizes (~28x), latency-bound only at decode.
- `2026-10-27` [[interim-report-sustained-throughput-at-32k]] — Interim report — sustained-throughput-at-32k — The six per-card TFLOPS figures are a ladder across lock state, power cap and duration, not a dispute: defensible sustained training denominator is ~90-100 TFLOPS/card (450-500 aggregate, 45-50% MFU), not 162/810; 6ND is invalid at 32k where attention is 88% of step FLOPs and total FLOPs are 8.5x 6ND, so the repo's 80-120k tok/s is 2-3x above the 39.6k hardware ceiling and realistic is ~9-22k tok/s (5-12 days for 9B tokens, not 25 h).

- `2026-10-27` [[interim-report-allreduce-ceiling-and-definition]] — Interim report — allreduce-ceiling-and-definition — 0.75 GiB/s is the nccl-tests busbw column, not algbw: at N=5 busbw = 1.6x algbw by construction, and 0.805 GB/s sits at 75-96% of the only physically available Gen2-x4 host-staged band (0.84-1.07 GB/s) while as algbw it would demand 77% of the one-way link rate on a double-traversal non-pipelined ring (best ever measured: 64%). The repo's 36% DDP overhead at 1M tokens is really 54-58% (constant 5.4e5-5.8e5, not 3.6e5), and its 2.0 GB payload already assumes bf16_compress_hook. BAR1 P2P is a latency lever (+30% on 2-GPU NCCL at x16, -47% forced across a host bridge, hangs collectives on 64 MB BAR1), not a bandwidth fix; the 24-capacitor x16 mod is the only order-of-magnitude lever at 3.9-4.0x busbw, saturating near 4.3 GB/s.

- `2026-10-27` [[p2pmd]] — Peer-to-peer and multi-GPU — Consensus-Protocol/cmp170hx technical wiki — Consensus-Protocol/cmp170hx 'Peer-to-peer and multi-GPU' page (docs/frontier/p2p.md, 28,895 bytes) — the primary source for the run's no-P2P premise, and the page that most sharply constrains the training-topology verdict. Short answer: peer-to-peer is absent on this card by default and no shipping code enables it. Measured baseline: torch.cuda.can_device_access_peer(i,j) returns False for ALL 56 ordered pairs on 8 unlocked cards (P2P-capable pairs: 0 of 56), including pairs within a PIX group; a ggml -lv 5 log shows zero peer/p2p/rpc occurrences; the only MIG profile offered (1g.64gb, 63.00 GiB, 70 SMs) reports 'P2P No'; and the active link during the sweep was Gen1 x4 at ~1.0 GB/s, which did not ramp under inference load even though the device maximum reported Gen2 x16. The absence is also visible in the source tree: a grep for 'p2p' and 'peer' across shipping master and all twelve unreleased branches returns exactly two kinds of hit — the stock nvidia-peermem.ko filename in build.sh's module list, and one line of unmodified context (nv_uvm_resume_P2P) inside the Gen2 branch's probe-retrain patch. No branch contains any P2P enablement. The page explicitly warns that nvidia-peermem.ko is NOT GPU-to-GPU peer access — it is the stock peer-memory client that lets third-party RDMA hardware reach GPU memory, and seeing it built and loaded is not evidence of peer access.

THE MEASURED COST OF NO P2P, and the TP dead end. With '-sm layer' split on an 8-card, 80-layer model (10 layers per GPU), every generated token makes 7 GPU-to-CPU-RAM-to-GPU hops, one of which crosses a NUMA/socket boundary at the layer 49-to-50 transition. The result is a concurrency ceiling: 1 user 17.3 tok/s, 2 users 21.6 aggregate, 4 users 25.7, 8 users 28.1, 16 users 38.9 — only 2.25x aggregate scaling from 1 to 16 users, attributed to the absence of P2P/NVLink, the cross-NUMA hop and the link together. Tensor parallelism, the strategy that would benefit most from peer access, is a measured dead end on these links: Qwen2.5-72B dense AWQ under vLLM at Gen1 x4 gives 1 card prefill 839/1,092/960 t/s at 1k/4k/16k with decode 27.3 t/s; PP2 829/1,084/1,167 with decode 29.1; TP2 316/420/416 with decode 33.7. So TP is 2.3-2.8x WORSE at prefill for only +23% decode — a direct measurement supporting the repo's claim that TP is ruled out by the fabric, and one that does not depend on the wiki's judgement call.

THE P2P PATCH, AND WHY IT WOULD BREAK DDP RATHER THAN HELP IT. github.com/aikitoria/open-gpu-kernel-modules (fork of tinygrad's, default branch 610.43.03-p2p) layers a three-commit P2P diff (+83/-28 across 8 files) on top of cmpunlocker; the mechanism is BAR1 peer-to-peer on GPUs where NVLink is absent. One builder reports it half-works on GA100: peer DATA MOVEMENT runs at 6.25 GB/s against 5.70 GB/s for the same copy staged through host memory, cross-process CUDA IPC handle sharing works, but peer SYNCHRONISATION does not work at all — any NCCL collective HANGS at transport connect (no error, no timeout, both GPUs pinned at 100%) and vLLM's custom all-reduce hangs the same way. The offered mechanism: kbusIsPcieBar1P2PMappingSupported_GH100 requires STATIC BAR1 on both GPUs spanning the whole framebuffer at a 512 MB-aligned offset, but on the 170HX BAR1 is 64 MB, so the check cannot pass; the mailbox fallback then fails its own alignment assertions in kern_bus.c and kern_bus_gm200.c. The practical consequence is narrow but decisive for this run: hand-written multi-GPU code that moves buffers and leaves coordination to the host could use peer DMA, while every collective library — and therefore tensor parallelism in every mainstream server — could not. The reporter's working multi-card vLLM configuration is NCCL_P2P_DISABLE=1 plus --disable-custom-all-reduce, which is exactly the configuration that works with NO P2P patch at all, so on that rig the patch bought nothing. This is an UNVERIFIED single-builder claim (logs posted, never independently reproduced) and the page flags it as a lead, not a result — but if it holds, applying the P2P patch to the box would hang DDP's NCCL all-reduce, i.e. it would break the one topology the repo says is viable.

EVIDENCE QUALITY: there are almost none, and the page says so — 'the gap is the single most important thing on this page'. p2pBandwidthLatencyTest has NOT been run on any 170HX, with or without the patch; nobody posted a matrix. P2P pairs capable unpatched: 0 of 56 (high confidence). Patch builds and loads on cmpunlocker: yes, one tester 2026-07-23 (medium). Effect on 170HX-only pairs: reported none, one tester, no test output (low). The Blackwell reference numbers (42.69-43.91 GB/s P2P-disabled, 55.59-56.58 GB/s P2P-enabled, 1,611-1,665 GB/s device-to-self) do NOT transfer — a 170HX at Gen1 or Gen2 x4 moves roughly one thirtieth to one sixtieth of those figures, and that driver branch lists only 3090/4090/5090 as supported. Open problem: does the patch do anything on a 170HX-only host? Two same-day reports conflict superficially — one 'p2p + cmpunlock working' in a rig that also contained two RTX 3090s, another that it 'doesn't seem to take effect on the 170HX ... only has an effect on them if there are other models of GPUs on the same machine' — but they may agree, since the successful rig is precisely the mixed-model case. What would settle it: the connectivity matrix from a 170HX-only two-card host, with and without the patch.

IOMMU/ACS constraints: BAR1 peer DMA writes a raw physical address at the other device, which only works if the IOMMU is in passthrough mode (iommu=pt), a configuration the fork's own README calls 'very dangerous if you run untrusted software or devices'. ACS on the root ports forces GPU-to-GPU traffic up through the CPU root complex, destroying the bandwidth the patch exists to provide; remedies are disabling ACS in BIOS, booting with pcie_acs_override=downstream,multifunction, or an ACS override kernel patch — all of which break IOMMU group isolation. Notably, the Gen2 code already configures exactly what the P2P patch requires (commit 6a85e6c appends intel_iommu=on iommu=pt or amd_iommu=on iommu=pt and verifies at runtime), making Gen2-plus-P2P the closest thing to a pre-configured stack anyone could assemble today — and nobody has assembled it. 4,462 words.
- `2026-10-27` [[moba-mixture-of-block-attention-for]] — MoBA: Mixture of Block Attention for Long-Context LLMs — MoBA: Mixture of Block Attention for Long-Context LLMs (Moonshot AI + Tsinghua + Zhejiang Lab, arXiv 2502.13189, technical report) — the TRAINED block-level sparse-attention baseline that HISA compares against and whose forced first/last-block rule HISA adopts, and the most directly comparable 32K training datapoint in the corpus. Mechanism: partition the context of length N into n blocks of size B = N/n; apply MoE-style top-k gating where the affinity score is s_i = <q, mean pool(K[I_i])> — the inner product between the query and the MEAN-POOLED keys of block i — and g_i = 1 iff s_i is in the top-k; attention is computed only over blocks with g_i > 0, with a causal constraint forbidding future blocks. This is exactly the pooled-block scoring HISA reuses for its Stage 1. MoBA adds no parameters and removes none, so it is a drop-in substitute for full attention, and the paper shows sliding-window attention and attention sink are special cases of MoBA under particular gating networks.

THE 32K RESULT THAT MATTERS: the scaling-law experiment trains five models (568M/10.8B tokens, 822M/15.3B, 1.1B/20.6B, 1.5B/27.4B, 2.1B/36.9B) at sequence length 8K with block size 512 and top-k 3 (sparsity up to 81.25%). At 8K, MoBA and full attention track within 1e-3 LM loss (fitted curves 2.625*C^-0.063 vs 2.622*C^-0.063). But when the maximum sequence length is raised from 8K to 32K — making the pattern 95.31% sparse — MoBA shows a marginally higher trailing-token LM loss than full attention in ALL FIVE experiments (fitted 1.546*C^-0.108 vs 1.464*C^-0.097), with the gap progressively narrowing as compute grows. So block-level sparsity trained at 8K extrapolates WORSE to 32K than full attention at these scales, which is the strongest published evidence for the repo's suspicion that CSA-style sparsity disappoints at 32k.

THE FIX, and the recipe the box should copy: a MoBA/full HYBRID. Three 1.5B models trained on 30B tokens at 32K context with block size 2048 and top-k 3: (a) MoBA/full hybrid — MoBA for 90% of tokens, then full attention for the last 10%; (b) full attention throughout; (c) MoBA throughout. The MoBA-only recipe gives higher position-wise losses for trailing tokens, while the 90/10 hybrid 'reaches a loss nearly identical to that of full attention', with no significant loss spikes at the switch. A layer-wise hybrid is also proposed for SFT: switching the LAST several Transformer layers from MoBA to full attention significantly reduces SFT loss, motivated by the observation that MoBA is sometimes suboptimal during SFT because prompt tokens are masked out of the loss, creating a sparse-gradient problem for sparse attention. Block granularity also matters: at 1.5B/32K, holding sparsity at 75%, there is a 1e-2 LM-loss difference between the coarsest setting (2 of 8 blocks) and finer settings (4/16, 8/32, 16/64, 32/128), so fine-grained segmentation is a general win for MoE-family attention.

Downstream and efficiency: Llama-8B-1M-MoBA (continual pre-training from Llama 3.1 8B Base, 128K -> 256K -> 512K -> 1M with position interpolation) matches Llama-8B-1M-Full within noise across AGIEval (0.5144/0.5146), BBH (0.6573/0.6589), CEval (0.6273/0.6165), GSM8K (0.7278/0.7142), HellaSWAG (0.8262/0.8279), Loogle (0.4209/0.4016), Competition Math (0.4254/0.4324), MBPP (0.5380/0.5320), MBPP Sanitized (0.6926/0.6615), MMLU (0.4903/0.4904), MMLU Pro (0.4295/0.4328), HumanEval (0.6951/0.7012), SimpleQA (0.0465/0.0492), TriviaQA (0.5673/0.5667) and LongBench@32K (0.4828/0.4821). Efficiency: up to 6.5x speedup when prefilling 1M tokens, and 16x reduction in attention computation time at 10M tokens. MoBA is deployed to support Kimi's long-context requests.

WHY THIS MATTERS FOR THE BOX, versus HISA: MoBA's gate is LEARNED — it is part of the model, trained end-to-end, with the same parameter count as full attention. So adopting MoBA on the CMP 170HX means training the block router, which is a real training-time cost and a real architecture change. HISA's hierarchical indexer, by contrast, is training-free and drop-in. The two are complementary: HISA gives the inference-time indexer speedup without retraining, while MoBA gives a trainable sparse-attention architecture whose 90/10 hybrid recipe is the only published recipe that matches full attention at 32K. The repo's CSA2 experiment should therefore specify which of the two it is testing. 8,376 words — LONG SOURCE, flag for source-analyst delegation.
- `2026-10-27` [[representation-drift-in-continual-learning]] — Representation Drift in Continual Learning: LoRA versus N-gram Memory — Representation Drift in Continual Learning: LoRA versus N-gram Memory (Hao Wu, Illinois Institute of Technology, arXiv 2610.14921v1, 10 Oct 2026) — the prior work Delta N-gram builds on, and the primary source for the claim that writing into a pretrained N-gram table disturbs the base model least. Setup: a 48-layer mixture-of-experts model (512 routed experts, top-10 routing plus shared experts, four hyper-connection residual streams) carrying a native, jointly pretrained N-gram memory of 51.2B parameters — a table of 320,001,536 rows of width 160, read at layer 2, with each token reading one row from each of 8 bigram and 8 trigram hash heads (16 rows, 2,560 values); addresses depend only on raw token IDs with history reset after EOS; the model runs in BF16. Three update channels on identical data and budget (125 optimizer steps x global batch 40 = 5,000 presentations per task, max length 128, Adam beta (0.9,0.95), peak lr 1e-4 cosine to zero, gradient clipping 1, optimizer and scheduler reset at every task boundary): (1) N-gram memory only — only the table rows addressed by the current task's inputs are trainable, everything else frozen; (2) Shared LoRA rank 16 alpha 64 across six projection families; (3) Merged LoRA, a fresh adapter per task merged into the backbone. Seven-task chain of SIGMORPHON 2023 morphological inflection (Northern Sami, Navajo, Armenian, Gulf Arabic, Albanian, Macedonian, Amharic), 5,000 examples each.

THE LOAD-BEARING RESULT FOR THIS RUN: updating only the N-gram memory changes the base model's representations of general math and code inputs by 26%/15% relative RMS, against 64%/35% for merged LoRA and 97%/39% for shared LoRA. Critically, this drift DOES NOT GROW with the number of tasks — it reaches 25% after the first task and stays there for all seven (21-26% on math), while shared LoRA grows almost monotonically (45% -> 97%) and merged LoRA grows to 64% with a 76% peak. Generic behaviour is essentially unchanged (no code problem lost, math NLL -0.01), and every change is confined to inspectable, reversible table rows. The mechanism is exact: an input whose addressed rows were never rewritten is bit-for-bit unchanged at every layer and in every logit, because the addresses are fixed by raw tokens and everything upstream of the read is frozen.

THE COST, which the run must not elide: N-gram memory acquires new tasks more weakly and its stored tasks interfere with one another, losing as much old-task NLL as shared LoRA. Forgetting is governed by drift on the learned tasks' OWN inputs, measured from the checkpoint at which each was learned, not by generic drift (Spearman rho = 0.85; rho = 0.71 on Llama-3-8B). With a frozen head, every pairwise logit margin changes by the projection of drift onto a head-difference direction. The design principle the paper draws — 'write new knowledge only into the memory table and keep the backbone fixed' — leaves the remaining problem, interference between stored tasks, inside the memory.

THE FEASIBILITY NUMBERS THAT MATTER ON A 64 GB CARD: the table is 51.2B parameters, i.e. ~102 GB in BF16 — the table ALONE exceeds a single CMP 170HX card by ~1.6x, before the 48-layer MoE backbone. So the frozen-table literature's actual scale is far beyond the repo's '7B total / 6B frozen' design; the repo's design is at the small end of this design space and cannot inherit these results without scaling the table down by roughly an order of magnitude. On the positive side for memory math: the N-gram arm's trainable set is only 51,018-149,809 rows per task (8-24M scalars), all seven tasks together touch 666,747 rows = 0.21% of the table, and each task shares 1,277-4,911 rows with earlier tasks. Because untouched rows have identically zero gradient and optimizer state, 'Adam is exactly equivalent to full-table Adam' on the touched rows — so optimizer state scales with touched rows, not table size, which is precisely the property the repo's 16 B/trainable-param vs 2 B/frozen-param arithmetic relies on.

TOPOLOGY DEPENDENCY: training used Megatron-SWIFT on 4xH200 with TP2/PP2/EP2 — tensor, pipeline AND expert parallelism simultaneously, none of which the CMP 170HX's no-P2P Gen2 x4 fabric supports. So this paper's training recipe, like Delta N-gram's EP4/DP4, is not reproducible on the box without re-deriving it under pure DDP. 11,284 words — LONG SOURCE, flag for source-analyst delegation.
- `2026-10-27` [[performancemd]] — Measured performance — Consensus-Protocol/cmp170hx technical wiki — Consensus-Protocol/cmp170hx 'Measured performance' page (docs/operations/performance.md, 28,339 bytes) — the page the PCIe subsystem page defers to for what link width/speed actually do to throughput. It is the strongest measured-compute source in the corpus and it directly calibrates the repo's hardware table. Compute, locked vs unlocked (all measured on compute-unlocked cards at stock clocks): FP32 non-tensor 0.30-0.41 -> 12.58 TFLOPS (28.4x-30.7x); FP32 SGEMM 393 Gflop/s -> 12,233-12,256 Gflop/s (31x); FP32 rented-cohort mean 0.39 -> 12.6 TFLOPS (32.3x, 8 cards, driver 610.43.02); FP64 non-tensor 0.20 -> 6.223-6.31 TFLOPS (exactly 1/2 of FP32); FP64 tensor 197/191 Gflop/s -> 11.6-11.96 TFLOPS; TF32 tensor 2.96-3.21 -> 79-94 TFLOPS (widest spread of any datatype); FP16 tensor 6.01 -> 158.7-190 TFLOPS; BF16 tensor 6.41 -> 164.4-192.7 TFLOPS with a 202.1 TFLOPS ceiling; INT8 tensor MMA 1.60 -> 335.0-335.6 TOPS; INT4 tensor MMA 320.2 TOPS. FP4/FP6/FP8 MMA: 'not supported ... expected for sm_80' — independent confirmation of the repo's claim that GA100 has no FP8 tensor cores. The compute unlock is a register write, not a clock change (SS0/SS1 writes under PLM at 0x00823804), and it does not touch power limit or PCIe link speed, so every figure is a stock-clock figure.

Calibration against the repo's hardware table: the repo states bf16 ~162 TFLOPS, fp16 ~178, TF32 ~83, fp32 ~13 (measured). This page's ranges are BF16 164.4-192.7, FP16 158.7-190, TF32 79-94, FP32 12.58 — so the repo's numbers sit inside the measured bands, but its bf16 162 is at the LOW end and the measured ceiling is 202.1 TFLOPS. That matters for the run's headline '~810 TFLOPS bf16' for 5 cards: 5 x 162 = 810 is the conservative figure; 5 x 192.7 = 963 TFLOPS is what the same measurements support at the top of the band. HBM: theoretical peak 1555.2 GB/s (1215 MHz DDR x 5120-bit) = 1448.4 GiB/s; measured range 1305.86-1600 GB/s with no methodology reconciling it; stock-vs-modded on the same card 1592 -> 1599 GB/s (1.0x, the unlock control row); 8 rented 64 GB cards 1600 GB/s identical across all eight; OpenCL coalesced read/write 1305.86/1521.62 GB/s; misaligned read/write 789.82/161.76 GB/s. The page explicitly warns there is no single canonical HBM number and that '1493 GB/s datasheet' (A100 40 GB PCIe) and '1555 GB/s theoretical at full boost' are different quantities routinely conflated.

PCIe bandwidth by link configuration (the table the run's communication model should cite): Gen1 x4 stock ~0.85 GB/s (clpeak, high confidence) or send 0.80 / receive 0.84 / bidirectional 0.81 GB/s (OpenCL-Benchmark, medium); Gen1 x4 under inference load ~1.0 GB/s and did not ramp (8-card rig, high confidence); Gen1 x16 cap-mod-only 2.88 GB/s (nominal ~4); Gen2 x4 branch-only send 1.68 / receive 1.71 GB/s; Gen1->Gen2 on a cap-modded card that negotiated x8 1.67 -> 3.24 GB/s (explicitly NOT a Gen2 x4 figure, since 3.24 exceeds the ~2.0 GB/s Gen2 x4 ceiling); Gen2 x4 vendor claim '2 GB/s' (low confidence, not achievable on shipping master); Gen2 x16 branch + full 24-capacitor mod 6.63-6.67 GB/s (~83% of the 8 GB/s line rate) with nvtop TX 7.061 GiB/s. Two rigs have published Gen2 x16 captures — 6.63-6.67 GB/s on one card on 2026-07-26 and 5.97 GB/s on each of four cards with zero AER errors over 90 minutes — but nobody has published a long burn-in.

How much PCIe actually costs, and the honest limits of that evidence: single card llama.cpp x4->x16 moves pp 439->448 and tg 81.91->85.75 (~+2%); three cards llama.cpp x4->x16 pp 441->461, tg 86->89; three cards GLM-5.2 with almost all of the model on CPU, Gen1 x4 -> 'gen2 x4 attempt', pp2048 33.44 -> 48.22 t/s (+44.2%), tg512 5.90 -> 6.39, TTFR 61,253.57 -> 42,510.25 ms (-30.6%) — but the page flags that the post-Gen2 run used a Q4-labelled quant where the pre-Gen2 run was unlabelled, so the prompt-processing delta may be overstated; single card Qwen3.6-27B-MTP-UD-Q8_K_XL fully VRAM-resident, Gen1 x4 -> Gen2 x4, pp2048 328.81 -> 449.41 t/s, pp8192 363.25 -> 493.86, tg128 38.15 -> 41.52, tg512 37.69 -> 40.12 (same tester, five days apart, and neither the tester nor the channel could explain why link speed moved a fully-resident model); model load ~30 s one-time at Gen1 x4; graphics BeamNG.drive 15 fps at Gen1 x16 vs 5 fps at x4. The reconciliation: single-card work is largely bandwidth-local (weights resident, only model load crosses the link) while multi-card and CPU-offload prefill is link-bound. Stated open problem: 'nobody has measured Gen2 x16 on a multi-card LLM rig' — the Gen2 x16 bandwidth figure and the Gen1-to-Gen2 inference runs were measured on different systems and never combined; the 2x2 matrix of {Gen1,Gen2} x {x4,x16} on one rig with one model would settle both this and the width-versus-generation dispute.

CRITICAL GAP THIS PAGE EXPOSES FOR THE RUN: it contains NO training, fine-tuning, pretraining, all-reduce, NCCL, DDP or gradient-communication measurement at all — every number is inference or microbenchmark. So the repo's DDP overhead model (~20% at 1M-token global batches, ~36% at theoretical peak, ~0.75 GiB/s all-reduce) has no external corroboration from this wiki; the only training datapoint in the corpus is the repo's own 494M-model, ~140k tok/s, 91-100 TFLOPS/GPU run, which is tier-1 self-measured but unverified by any third party. 4,874 words.
- `2026-10-27` [[physical-modsmd]] — Physical modifications — AC-coupling capacitor mod — Consensus-Protocol/cmp170hx — Consensus-Protocol/cmp170hx 'Physical modifications' page (docs/operations/physical-mods.md, 39,791 bytes) — the procedure page the PCIe subsystem page defers to, and the primary source for the run's link-width question. Headline: the CMP 170HX trains at PCIe x4 because 12 of its 16 lanes ship with their AC-coupling capacitors depopulated from the factory; hand-soldering the 24 missing 0402 220 nF X7R capacitors restores a full x16 link with NO software patch of any kind. The mod changes LINK WIDTH ONLY and never PCIe generation: a cap-modded card with no unlocker installed reports x16 at PCIe 1.0, while an unlocker with no cap mod reports Gen2 x4. The four-state verification table is the cleanest artifact in the whole corpus — stock: LnkSta 'Speed 2.5GT/s, Width x4 (downgraded)', nvidia-smi 1,1,4; cap mod only: 'Speed 2.5GT/s, Width x16', 1,1,16; unlocker only: 'Speed 5GT/s, Width x4 (downgraded)', 2,2,4; cap mod + unlocker: 'Speed 5GT/s, Width x16', 2,2,16. The '(downgraded)' suffix disappearing from the width field once all 24 parts are in place is the single clearest before/after signal; '(overdriven)' on the speed field is normal and not an error.

Procedure and risk. Parts: 24 x 0402, 220 nF, X7R, 6.3 V or higher; confirmed part Taiyo Yuden MAASJ105SB7224KFCA01 (AEC-Q200), value read off the NVIDIA A100 GA100-883 reference schematic P1001-B02 page 3. Tooling: fine-point iron at ~380 C, wick away all factory lead-free solder first, gel flux, tin one pad, tack the part, flow the other side; hot air with a correctly sized nozzle is faster for batches; Kapton-tape the surroundings; no preheat. Time: ~20 minutes per card by hand for an experienced modder, medium confidence. Difficulty: routine 0402 rework on labelled, uncramped pads on the back of the board beside the gold fingers — no BGA, no reball, no stencil, no firmware step; experienced modders call it 'probably the easiest card to do PCIE mod'. The dominant beginner failure mode is preheating the whole board (an IR stove plus hot air), whose documented consequence is a bent PCB, broken internal traces and cooked ICs. An imperfect result is non-destructive: it under-trains the link rather than damaging the card, and the card keeps working at whatever width it negotiated while the joints are fixed — no attempt limit, no state to reset. Population-to-width mapping: 12 of 24 correctly populated trains x8; 12-23 of 24, or any bridged/cold joints, trains x8; 24 of 24 trains x16. An x8 result means incomplete or bridged solder work, not a distinct hardware limit; the remedy is mechanical — reflow and inspect all 24 joints under magnification for tombstoned parts, solder bridges between the two pads of a pair, and joints that merely rest on the pad without wetting it.

FIELD REPORT — FOUR CARDS AT GEN2 x16. A community member modified four CMP 170HX cards with twenty-four Taiyo Yuden MAASJ105SB7224KFCA01 each and supplied photographs and logs. All four train PCIe 2.0 x16 and have run continuous four-card LLM inference with ZERO PCIe errors. This is the second independent rig to reach Gen2 x16 after the single 2026-07-26 observation, and the first with more than one card, more than one board revision, or any error telemetry at all — which materially upgrades the stability verdict from the PCIe subsystem page's 'unestablished'. Only the capacitors were fitted: no VBIOS was flashed (all four still report the stock 92.00.6D.00.0A), no VRM phases populated, no strap resistors moved, no backup flash chip added, no other footprint touched. The circulating a100-unlock.pdf bill of materials lists a Winbond flash chip and other parts; none of them were needed for x16.

Measured bandwidth by configuration (the numbers the run's communication model should use): Gen1 x4 stock ~0.85 GB/s (send 0.80, receive 0.84); Gen1 x16 cap-mod-only 2.88 GB/s flat and error-free (nominal ~4 GB/s, shortfall attributed to PCIe 1.1 signalling overhead); Gen2 x4 software-only 1.68 GB/s send / 1.71 GB/s receive; Gen2 x16 both 6.63-6.67 GB/s via ocl_pcie_bw with nvtop showing TX 7.061 GiB/s at 'PCIe GEN 2@16x'; Gen2 x16 second rig 5.97 GB/s host-to-device, identical on all four cards, CUDA H2D with MaxPayload 256 bytes. So a cap-modded + unlocked box would move ~4x the bytes of the repo's assumed Gen2 x4 fabric, which is exactly the ~4x swing the repo's open question #1 anticipated. Caveat on Gen3: a purely software Gen3 x4 unlock would need no soldering and is the community's next target, but the arithmetic does not support the claim that it is speed-equivalent to Gen2 x16 — Gen3 x4 is about 3.9 GB/s against Gen2 x16's 8 GB/s, landing nearer Gen1 x16; 'lanes are lanes' for anyone packing many cards into one host.

Two further load-bearing points. (a) The page repeats, independently of the PCIe subsystem page, that 'tensor and expert parallelism were judged unworkable even at PCIe 2.0 x16', and that pipeline-parallel inter-card traffic is negligible (a 5120-hidden-dimension model moves 10,240 bytes per token per hop, so roughly 25,000 tokens/s would be needed to saturate a single PCIe 1.0 lane). (b) Sourcing correction: a widely repeated claim states the distributed unlock README documents the x4 limitation as missing AC-coupling capacitors on lanes 4-15 — the shipping README.md contains no such text, and neither does any branch or any point in the git history. The technical claim is true and well evidenced; only the attribution to the shipped README is wrong, and it most likely originates in a third-party guide. The software tree genuinely contains nothing about the mod: no file in shipping master or any of the 12 unreleased branches contains 'capacitor', 'AC coupling', 'solder' or any lane-width register, and the shipping 0007-pcie-gen2.patch manipulates link speed registers only. 6,424 words — LONG SOURCE, flag for source-analyst delegation.
- `2026-10-27` [[published-as-a-conference-paper-at-colm-2026]] — HISA: Efficient Hierarchical Indexing for Fine-Grained Sparse Attention (COLM 2026) — HISA: Efficient Hierarchical Indexing for Fine-Grained Sparse Attention (Xu, Meng et al., Peking University + Xiaohongshu, arXiv 2603.28458v4, 10 Sep 2026, COLM 2026) — the hierarchical/pooled-block indexer prior work behind DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash's CSA2, and the paper that makes the run's 32k CSA2 question answerable. Problem statement: token-level sparse attention (DSA, DeepSeek-V3.2, also GLM-5) scores EVERY historical key per query through a lightweight indexer before running Sparse MLA on the top-k; the downstream attention is cheap but the indexer still scans the full prefix, so per-layer indexing cost is O(L^2) — the same asymptotic scaling as dense attention — and it 'can transition from a negligible overhead into the dominant cost component' as context grows. Measured confirmation: at 64K context with top-k=2048, the Sparse MLA operator costs a roughly constant ~1.6 ms while the indexer reaches 5.6 ms, i.e. the indexer is ~3.5x the sparse-attention cost and is 'the main performance bottleneck in DSA'.

HISA's fix is a two-stage rewrite of the search path that preserves the search result: (1) block-level coarse filtering — partition the prefix into M = ceil(L/B) causally valid blocks, mean-pool each block's indexing keys into one representative key, score those pooled keys with the SAME indexer queries and gating weights as DSA (J_t,b = sum_j w_j * ReLU(q_j . k_pooled_b)), and take the top-m blocks (first and last blocks always forced in, following MoBA, to keep the attention sink and local context); (2) token-level refinement — run the original DSA scoring only over the union of tokens in the selected blocks, then take the final top-k. Per-query cost drops from O(L) to O(L/B + mB), with the feasibility constraint mB >= k. Crucially it is a plug-and-play replacement that preserves the token-level top-k index interface consumed by Sparse MLA and leaves the KV cache layout unchanged; pooled summaries are maintained incrementally alongside the KV cache.

THE BOUNDARY BEHAVIOUR IS THE LOAD-BEARING PART FOR THIS RUN. Three regimes: when t <= k, all prefix tokens are selected and HISA is EQUIVALENT TO DENSE ATTENTION; when k < t <= mB, the coarse filter selects all blocks (m >= M) and Stage 2 reduces to k tokens, so HISA is EQUIVALENT TO THE ORIGINAL DSA INDEXER with no hierarchical advantage; only when t > mB does non-trivial block pruning activate. So the hierarchical win is a step function of the candidate budget mB, not a smooth function of context. With the paper's configuration (B=128, m=64 -> mB=8192) the cap binds at 32k (32768 > 8192) and pruning is active; with a 16,384 cap (the figure the repo attributes to CSA2) 32k still exceeds it by 2x, so the cap DOES bind at 32k — the repo's phrasing that it 'barely binds' understates the pruning but its qualitative conclusion (a smaller win than the published 1M-context numbers) holds.

Measured results. Kernel-level, ALL ON AN NVIDIA A100 (SM80 — the same architecture class as the CMP 170HX's GA100), both indexers in TileLang, query len 1024, top-k 2048, B=128: at 64K context HISA gives ~2.16x speedup at a 4:1 first-stage compression ratio (16K candidate budget) and up to 3.75x under a fixed 8K budget; the paper's abstract cites 2-4x kernel-level speedup at 64K. End-to-end in SGLang on DeepSeek-V3.2 (8xH200, TP=8, 128K input / 512 output): DSA official 5868.43 tok/s, E2E 143764.47 ms, TTFT 106449.81 ms, TPOT 73.02 ms; HISA (B=128, m=64) 9712.21 tok/s, 86718.06 ms, 62722.91 ms, 46.96 ms -> 1.65x throughput, 1.66x E2E, 1.70x TTFT, 1.55x TPOT, achieved with TileLang/Triton operators that are less mature than DeepSeek's hand-tuned CUDA, so the speedups understate HISA at matched kernel maturity.

Quality: HISA is TRAINING-FREE — applied at inference time by replacing the indexer module, with no fine-tuning and no architectural modification. NIAH (8K-648K contexts, 0-100% needle depth): HISA closely matches DSA's near-perfect retrieval with only marginal degradation at extreme lengths/depths, while the block-sparse baseline degrades badly in the middle of the context. LongBench averages: DeepSeek-V3.2 DSA 51.05, HISA 50.78 (-0.27), Block-Sparse 49.54; GLM-5 DSA 46.01, HISA 46.32 (+0.31), Block-Sparse 42.67. So hierarchical indexing costs ~0.3 LongBench points and buys 1.65x serving throughput.

Two corrections this forces on the run's CSA2 reasoning. (a) The repo says CSA2's indexer 'adds extra code and an extra loss term' to training; HISA shows the HIERARCHICAL part is training-free and drop-in, so only the base DSA indexer needs the distillation loss — the hierarchical layer adds zero training cost. (b) The repo's premise that no DSA path runs on SM80 is too strong: the cuDNN DSA kernels are SM90/SM100-only, but HISA's indexer kernels are TileLang and were benchmarked on A100, so the dominant cost component (the indexer) has an SM80-runnable implementation; the caveat is that HISA retains the official flashmla sparse path downstream, which is Hopper-oriented. Open directions the authors name: overlapping/adaptive blocks or max pooling to reduce coarse-filter information loss, and 'training-aware HISA' (jointly training the block-scoring stage) which they explicitly have NOT done. 8,581 words — LONG SOURCE, flag for source-analyst delegation.
- `2026-10-27` [[pcie-subsystemmd]] — PCIe subsystem — Consensus-Protocol/cmp170hx technical wiki — Consensus-Protocol/cmp170hx 'PCIe subsystem' page (docs/hardware/pcie-subsystem.md, 27,761 bytes) — the page that answers the run's open question #1 ('actual negotiated link width: Gen2 x4 vs x16 changes every communication number ~4x'). PATH CORRECTION: the assigned URL docs/hardware/pcie.md is a 404; the real page is docs/hardware/pcie-subsystem.md. Headline: the CMP 170HX leaves the factory with TWO INDEPENDENT restrictions that coexist on the same board and defeating either does nothing for the other. (1) Speed cap: stock trains at PCIe Gen1 2.5 GT/s, enforced by OTP fuses plus a signed DevInit table in firmware; defeated only by a driver patch on unreleased branches, Gen2 reached in software 2026-07-24 and NOT shipped, Gen3/Gen4 unreached. (2) Width cap: x4 trained, x16 advertised, because 12 of the 16 PCIe data lanes ship with their AC-coupling capacitors physically omitted from the PCB — two capacitors per differential pair, so 24 missing 0402 parts; lanes 0-3 populated, lanes 4-15 not. Defeated only by hand-soldering those 24 capacitors, reproduced by multiple independent modders since April 2026. The independence proof: a Gen2-patched unmodded card is Gen2 x4; a fully modded unpatched card is Gen1 x16. A stock card reports LnkCap Speed 5GT/s Width x16 while LnkSta reads Speed 5GT/s Width x4 (downgraded) — 'Nothing in software can close that gap, because there is no electrical path on 12 of the lanes.'

DIRECT ANSWER TO THE GAP: yes, width beyond x4 requires the AC-coupling capacitor mod, and it is PCB rework, not software. Three independent lines of evidence rule out every software explanation: (a) no lane fuse is set — OPT_PCIE_LANE_DISABLE 0x00820394, CTRL_OPT_PCIE_LANE 0x0082082C and STATUS_OPT_PCIE_LANE 0x00820C2C all read 0x00000000, so the x16 electrical width is intact in silicon; (b) no code touches width — an exhaustive audit of every PCIe-related write in the Gen2 code found LINK_CAP[9:4] Max Link Width never read or written, and LNKSTA masked with CLS and DLLLA but never NLW; a grep for 'capacitor'/'AC coupling'/'solder' across master and twelve unreleased branches returns nothing; (c) a known-good x16 host port still trains x4 (measured 2026-07-26, sysfs cur 4 / max 16 on both cards).
