The parts: 24 x 0402, 220 nF (0.22 uF), X7R dielectric (often mistyped XR7), >=6.3 V; reference designators C1100-C1350 range; confirmed manufacturer part Taiyo Yuden MAASJ105SB7224KFCA01, Samsung CL05B224KO5NNNC a reported-working substitute; value read off the NVIDIA A100 GA100-883 reference schematic P1001-B02 page 3 'IO: PCIe CONNECTOR'. Measured before/after: LnkSta Speed 2.5GT/s Width x4 (downgraded) -> Speed 2.5GT/s Width x16, speed field unchanged as expected. Partial work negotiates down through the legal widths 16/8/4/1: 12-23 of 24 capacitors correctly populated trains at x8, and one modder's progression across three cards was x4 then x8 then x16 as technique improved — an x8 result means incomplete or bridged joints, not a hardware limit.

Measured bandwidth by configuration (the numbers the run's communication model should use): Gen1 x4 = 0.85 GB/s write / 0.84 GB/s read (clpeak, high confidence); Gen2 x4 = 1.68 GB/s send / 1.71 GB/s receive (OpenCL-Benchmark, one archived screenshot, medium); Gen1 x16 cap-mod-no-Gen2 = 2.88 GB/s flat (medium); Gen1 x8 -> Gen2 x8 = 1.67 -> 3.24 GB/s (medium, a width result, not a Gen2 x4 figure); Gen2 x16 = 6.63-6.67 GB/s (ocl_pcie_bw) with nvtop showing PCIe GEN 2@16x TX 7.061 GiB/s, a second rig reporting 5.97 GB/s on each of four cards (medium). WARNING: Gen2 x16 has been observed ONCE, on 2026-07-26, on one rig, in one screenshot, on a card with a complete 24-capacitor mod; no lspci capture bridges it to the earlier all-x4 Gen2 survey, no burn-in, no AER counters, no second rig — treat 6.63-6.67 GB/s as medium confidence and Gen2 x16 STABILITY as unestablished.

Two further load-bearing claims for the run. First, on parallelism: the page states plainly that 'Tensor and expert parallelism are judged unworkable even at Gen2 x16' — i.e. the repo's ruling-out of EP/TP is not an artifact of the x4 link; it survives a 4x bandwidth increase. Pipeline-parallel decode is the opposite case: the link is nearly irrelevant (a 5120-hidden model moves 10,240 bytes per token per hop, so saturating a single PCIe 1.0 lane would need ~25,000 tokens/s). Second, on P2P: 'Peer-to-peer is absent on this card and no branch contains any P2P enablement', and a PCIe switch fan-out does not help because cards behind a switch cannot bypass the uplink without P2P. Platform confounds that are NOT the cause: NV_PTOP_FS4 0x0002241c, board straps (copying A100 straps made the card undetected at boot), device-ID spoofing (FUSE_DEVID_SW_OVR_DIS reads 1), flashing an A100 80GB VBIOS (tested and failed), a PCIe redriver (only a retimer could forge TS1/TS2 Rate-ID), and ASPM (the card advertises 'ASPM not supported'). Thunderbolt 3 eGPU breaks the unlock entirely (GSP boot failure); VM passthrough advertises Gen2 but never trains. Diagnostic rules: read LnkSta never LnkCap; do not trust sysfs max_link_speed (reported cur 5.0 GT/s / max 2.5 GT/s on three cards); do not read nvidia-smi 'PCIe Generation Max' as evidence; the three honest fields are lspci LnkSta, /sys/bus/pci/devices/<bdf>/current_link_speed, and nvidia-smi --query-gpu=pcie.link.gen.gpucurrent. Also: BAR1 stays 64 MiB regardless of framebuffer size, so full-VRAM host mapping is unavailable and ReBAR is advertised but functionally inert; slot power limit 75 W, MaxPayload 256 B, MaxReadReq 512 B.
- `2026-10-27` [[dsa_referencepy]] — cuDNN Frontend DSA reference implementation (dsa_reference.py) — NVIDIA/cudnn-frontend — NVIDIA cuDNN Frontend DSA reference implementation (Apache-2.0, Copyright 2026 NVIDIA), 980 lines of pure PyTorch — the ONLY DSA path that runs on GA100/SM80, because it contains no CuTe-DSL, no cutlass and no CUDA extension: just torch.einsum, torch.gather and torch.autograd. PATH CORRECTION: the assigned URL github.com/NVIDIA/cudnn-frontend/blob/main/python/dsa_reference.py is a 404 ('File not found · GitHub'); the real file is test/python/deepseek_sparse_attention/cutedsl/dsa_reference.py (37,804 bytes), fetched via raw.githubusercontent.com. Its own docstring frames it as 'Reference implementations for DSA tests ... for numerical verification of the cudnn-frontend DSA kernels' and calls the forward 'Gather-based PyTorch reference DSA forward (uncompiled, slow)' — so it is a correctness oracle, not a training kernel.

What it implements (all CPU/GPU-portable PyTorch): ref_sparse_attention_forward (slot/gather semantics — duplicate top-k indices retain their multiplicity, negative and out-of-bounds indices are masked before gather; lse is KV-only and excludes the sink; empty rows use the kernel sentinels out=0, max_logits=-inf, lse=+inf; MLA path uses all 576 dims for QK and the first 512 for V, while the 512-wide path keeps K=V identical); ref_sparse_attention_forward_chunked (query_chunk_size=64, bounds query intermediates); check_ref_dsa_sparse_attention_backward (chunked autograd producing dq/dkv/d_sink); ref_indexer_forward (dense scores S = sum_h ReLU(Q_h·K_h^T)·W_h with a ratio-causal mask col_limit = floor((q+1)/ratio), optional per-batch q_causal_offsets, returns (B,S_q,S_k) FP32); ref_indexer_top_k (emulates the radix kernel's score ordering with bit-twiddled keys plus a stable argsort, tie_break modes 0/1/2, INT32 indices with -1 padding); check_ref_compressed_topk (validates local compressed top-k ids/logits against dense scores); sparse and dense indexer/attention score recompute; ref_indexer_backward and ref_dense_indexer_backward.

The indexer backward is the decisive confirmation of the run's 'separate distillation loss' claim: it forms a clipped-log KL gradient from the supplied FP32 index_score exactly as the kernel's _score_grad_inplace stage does — eps = exp(-100.0) (kernel CLIP_LOG_MIN), g = -max(target,eps)*[predict>=eps], grad_signal = g - predict*sum(g), masked where topk_indices<0 — and backpropagates through scores recomputed from (index_q, weights, index_k, topk_indices). The comment notes that autograd through log(predict.clamp(min=eps)) is NOT equivalent (the log backward's 1/predict overflows fp32 at eps=exp(-100)), and that a naive construction measures ~0.55 rms_rel against the kernel where the analytic one measures <=0.005. So top-k remains non-differentiable and the indexer is trained against main-attention scores, not end-to-end.

Feasibility verdict for the box: runnable, but not at 32k training context as written. ref_indexer_forward materializes scores_per_head of shape (B, S_q, H_q, S_k) FP32 and has NO chunked variant; at S=32768, B=1, H_q=16 that is 32768*16*32768*4 B = 68.7 GB — already over a 64 GB CMP 170HX card before any other allocation (H_q=32 doubles it to 137 GB). ref_sparse_attention_forward materializes gathered_kv of shape (t, topk, d) FP32: at t=32768, topk=2048, d=576 that is ~154.6 GB, so only the chunked wrapper (64-query chunks, ~302 MB) is usable. Arithmetic from the box's measured 1355 GB/s HBM bandwidth (clpeak) puts the gather traffic alone at ~115 ms per layer-forward per 32k sequence, i.e. multiple seconds per fwd+bwd step per sequence — orders of magnitude below the repo's ~140k tok/s dense baseline. Practical conclusion: this reference can validate DSA numerics on SM80 and can run a reduced-topk / reduced-context CSA2 experiment, but a 32k CSA2 training run on this box requires rewriting the indexer query-chunked and replacing the gather with a tiled kernel; the reference itself is not the training path.
- `2026-10-27` [[delta-n-gram-forward-only-continual-writing]] — Delta N-gram: Forward-Only Continual Writing — Delta N-gram (Hao Wu, Illinois Institute of Technology, arXiv 2610.31125v1, 22 Oct 2026) — the strongest counter-datapoint to the run's frozen-table optimism, and the paper that makes the Memory Grafting design space precise. Setup: a hashed N-gram table T = T0 + Δ, where T0 is the pretrained table and Δ is a sparse patch nonzero only on written rows; every row with Δj = 0 reads exactly its pretrained value, and clearing Δ returns the original model. On Qwen3.8-Flash-Next (48 layers, 512 routed experts, 10 active/token) the native table is read through H = 16 hash heads (8 bigram + 8 trigram), row width d = 160, concatenated to R^2560 and injected before layer-2 attention. Backbone, read-out and T0 are ALL frozen; the only trained component is a 4,075,681-parameter writer. Writes at use time are forward-only: no backpropagation, no optimizer state, no labels; the state stores only written row ids, patches Δj and write counts cj — explicitly 'It carries no optimizer state.'

That is the direct support for the repo's memory math: a frozen table costs its bf16 rows and nothing else (no fp32 master, no m/v, no grad), and the paper demonstrates a working design whose trainable budget is 4.1M params against a 48-layer 512-expert backbone. But the paper's own training run contradicts the box's fabric assumptions: the writer was trained on four H200 GPUs with expert parallelism 4 and data parallelism 4, 12 trajectories per update, AdamW lr 3e-4, 100M target tokens over 4,172 updates. EP4 is exactly the topology the CMP 170HX's no-P2P Gen2 x4 fabric rules out, so reproducing this recipe on the box would require re-deriving the writer training under pure DDP.

The capability results are the counter-datapoint. Frozen memory is NOT free capacity: on a 0.6B prototype the forward writer keeps 16/16 facts after a second write where online gradient writes keep 2-4/16, but the binding is tied to the trained question form — a paraphrased question stays at the empty-memory 2/16 level, and the rows such queries read overlap the written rows in only ~6% of positions. Longer chains erode: four writes leave 60/64 facts answerable, sixteen writes leave 46/64 right after their own write but only 19 at the end. On 64 held-out late-2025 news events the plain writer lowers continuation NLL by 0.068 nats/token against empty memory and 0.024 against another document's memory (0.063 and 0.013 after 16 intervening articles); the delta writer is more document-specific and retains 70% vs 54% of that specificity under distraction, at a smaller total gain.

Long-chain stability is real: 35,000 consecutive writes on two hardware stacks with no numerical failure, and learned-task accuracy drifts only -0.26pp (plain) / -0.52pp (delta) over the rest of the chain versus -7.29pp for the Adam table updates of the prior Representation Drift work. But net acquisition on tasks the writer never saw is NEGATIVE (-12/448) and recall of written examples is only 23-25%. General-input big-K drift is 32.8%, 1.6x the Adam baseline's 20.75%, concentrated in eight format bigram rows that every example writes and whose patches reach 4.4-11.7x their T0 norm; zeroing those eight rows removes over 98% of the layer-2 drift on math inputs — and also removes their NLL gain. Optional decay pulls Δ toward zero (table toward T0), giving reversible forgetting.

Training data mix (relevant because it overlaps the run's own pipeline): Stack v2 Edu 20%, arXiv 25%, Project Gutenberg 20% (Common Pile), FineWeb-Edu 15%, CC-News 17.5%, same-event CC-News streams 2.5%; 38,566 training documents. Prototype used a zero-initialised external table of 16,384 rows each for bigrams and trigrams, row width 1024, 1,190,465 writer params. 16,791 words — LONG SOURCE, flag for source-analyst delegation.
- `2026-10-27` [[huggingfacetbstack-edu-datasets-at-hugging-face]] — HuggingFaceTB/stack-edu · Datasets at Hugging Face — Dataset card for HuggingFaceTB/stack-edu (the page the run's data-pipeline.md cites as the 15% code slice). Confirms the headline number: Stack-Edu is a 125B-token educational-code corpus filtered from The Stack v2 — specifically the StarCoder2Data corpus used to train StarCoder2 — across 15 languages, with per-language token counts under the SmolLM2 tokenizer: Java 42.1B, Python 21.8B, Cpp 16.0B, Markdown 14.0B, C 11.1B, JavaScript 11.1B, SQL 9.62B, PHP 9.07B, C-Sharp 8.87B, Shell 3.13B, TypeScript 3.03B, Swift 1.83B, Go 1.80B, Rust 1.75B, Ruby 1.61B. Curation: 15 language-specific StarEncoder classifiers trained on synthetic annotations generated by Llama3-70B-Instruct, filtering threshold 3-of-5 except Java at 2; the card reports consistent MultiPL-E improvement over StarCoder2Data.

The load-bearing operational constraint for this box is delivery format, not size: the repo holds only SWHIDs (blob_id), NOT file contents — 167,063,359 rows / 17.5 GB of metadata. Actual code must be pulled from Software Heritage's public S3 bucket (bucket 'softwareheritage', key content/<blob_id>, gzip), and the card's own script takes ~6 hours on a 16-core AWS us-east-1 instance to download. So the 1.35B-token Stack-Edu slice in the run's 9B-token mix is not a one-command download; it is an S3 hydration job whose wall time and disk footprint (full 125B-token corpus is far larger than the 2.2 TB free on the box) must be budgeted. Two further caveats: the 125B figure is SmolLM2-tokenizer tokens, not Qwen3-tokenizer tokens as the run's pipeline assumes, and the card's licence pointer defers to the-stack-v2-train-full-ids rather than stating a licence itself.
- `2026-10-27` [[fullyshardeddataparallel-pytorch-213-documentation]] — FullyShardedDataParallel — PyTorch 2.13 documentation — PyTorch 2.13 FullyShardedDataParallel API documentation (torch.distributed.fsdp) — the primary reference for exactly what FSDP moves over the wire and how it treats frozen parameters. Sharding strategies: FULL_SHARD all-gathers parameters before the forward, reshards after it, all-gathers again before backward, reshards after backward, and reduce-scatters gradients after backward, with optimizer states updated locally per rank; SHARD_GRAD_OP unshards before forward but does not reshard until after backward (so parameters stay gathered between passes); NO_SHARD is DDP-equivalent and all-reduces gradients; HYBRID_SHARD does FULL_SHARD within a node and replicates across nodes, cutting communication volume because the expensive all-gathers and reduce-scatters stay intra-node. Memory/overlap knobs: backward_prefetch=BACKWARD_PRE holds current params, next params and current gradients at peak (most overlap, most memory) while BACKWARD_POST frees current params before allocating the next set; limit_all_gathers (default True) is a rate limiter that caps GPU memory to two consecutive FSDP instances' worth of gathered parameters. Frozen-parameter constraint, directly relevant to a frozen-Engram plan: with use_orig_params=False each FSDP instance must manage parameters that are all frozen or all non-frozen; with use_orig_params=True FSDP supports mixing frozen and non-frozen parameters but recommends against it because of higher-than-expected gradient memory. Also: FSDP cannot run the forward of a submodule contained in an FSDP instance (its params are sharded but no all-gather happens), and mixed precision can be disabled per module at the cost of separate all-gathers/reduce-scatters. Relevance: this is the authoritative source for the claim that FSDP's per-layer all-gather/reduce-scatter pattern is what makes it expensive on a slow fabric, and it shows the frozen-table plan needs its own all-frozen FSDP unit rather than a mixed unit.
- `2026-10-27` [[parallelism-methods-hugging-face]] — Parallelism methods · Hugging Face — Hugging Face Transformers documentation page 'Parallelism methods' (main branch, v5.x) — a short (~1.4k word) strategy-selection guide, no benchmarks or numbers. It gives the decision table this run's topology question needs: single node / fits on one GPU -> DistributedDataParallel or ZeRO; doesn't fit -> PipelineParallel, ZeRO or TensorParallel; largest layer doesn't fit -> TensorParallel or ZeRO; multi-node with fast inter-node connectivity (NVLink/NVSwitch) -> ZeRO or 3D parallelism; multi-node with slow inter-node connectivity -> ZeRO or 3D parallelism. It defines DP vs DDP (DDP recommended because it reduces communication overhead and scales past one machine), the three ZeRO stages (shard optimizer states / gradients / parameters), and notes that model, pipeline and tensor parallelism all add communication overhead and can become the bottleneck. Relevance: it is a ground-truth doc for the claim that DDP is the default when a model fits replicated, and that sharding is the fallback when it does not — but it says nothing about how a PCIe-only, no-P2P fabric changes those recommendations, so it cannot support the repo's stronger claim that DDP is the *only* viable topology on this box.
- `2026-10-27` [[the-pitfalls-of-kv-cache-compression-acl-anthology]] — The Pitfalls of KV Cache Compression - ACL Anthology — The Pitfalls of KV Cache Compression (Chen, Geh, Grover, Van den Broeck & Israel, ACL 2026 Long Papers, pp. 41530-41553; DOI 10.18653/v1/2026.acl-long.1926) — an evaluation-methodology paper, not a training study. It evaluates five inference-time KV compression methods (StreamingLLM, SnapKV, TOVA, H2O, K-Norm) on Llama3.1 8B and Qwen2.5 14B under multi-instruction prompting with IFEval, and shows that aggregate benchmark scores hide method-specific failures: certain instructions degrade much faster under compression and end up completely ignored by the model. It documents system-prompt leakage as a case study and attributes it to compression method, instruction order and KV eviction bias, then proposes simple eviction-policy changes that reduce the effect. Relevance to this run: it is the primary source behind the periodic-weak-spots claim that average scores conceal compression failures, and it supplies a concrete evaluation protocol (multi-instruction prompting, per-instruction breakdown, leakage probes) that a CSA2-at-32k experiment should adopt. Scope caveat: these are eviction-based inference KV compressors, not DeepSeek's chunked training-time CSA/CSA2 — the failure mechanism (eviction bias) is different from phase sensitivity, so it corroborates the evaluation principle rather than the specific CSA2 claim. NOTE: this note is the ACL Anthology landing page (abstract + BibTeX, ~1.4k words); the PDF is at aclanthology.org/2026.acl-long.1926.pdf.
- `2026-10-27` [[deepseek-v3-technical-report]] — DeepSeek-V3 Technical Report — DeepSeek-V3 technical report (DeepSeek-AI, arXiv 2412.19437, Dec 2024) — the canonical primary source for what expert parallelism actually costs and what it takes to hide that cost. Model: 671B total / 37B active MoE with MLA and DeepSeekMoE, 8 routed experts plus 1 shared, trained on 14.8T tokens on 2048 H800 GPUs for 2.664M GPU hours (~180K H800 GPU-hours per trillion tokens; 2.788M GPU hours / $5.576M total). The load-bearing admission for this run: 'the communication overhead introduced by cross-node expert parallelism results in an inefficient computation-to-communication ratio of approximately 1:1' — i.e. on their fabric, EP all-to-all cost roughly as much time as the compute it fed, before overlap. Their fix is DualPipe, which splits each chunk into attention / all-to-all dispatch / MLP / all-to-all combine and rearranges forward and backward chunks so that both all-to-all and PP communication are fully hidden, reaching 'near-zero all-to-all communication overhead' as long as the compute-to-communication ratio is held constant. That overlap is built on a two-tier fabric: intra-node NVLink at 160 GB/s versus inter-node IB at 50 GB/s (3.2x), with each token dispatched to at most 4 nodes (averaging 3.2 experts per node, so up to 13 experts at the same communication cost) and only 20 SMs dedicated to saturating both links. Training topology: EP32 with no tensor parallelism at all — they explicitly say the memory work 'enabl[es] us to train DeepSeek-V3 without using costly Tensor Parallelism (TP)'. Inference uses TP4+DP8+EP32 for prefill and EP320 for decode. Relevance: this is the strongest published evidence that (a) EP all-to-all is a first-order cost even on NVLink+IB, (b) TP was avoided for training by the lab with the most reason to use it, and (c) the standard mitigation (overlap) presupposes a fast intra-node tier and a separate scale-out network — neither of which exists on a flat Gen2 x4 no-P2P box.
- `2026-10-27` [[behind-the-scenes-of-distributed-training-and-why-your-gpu-wiring-matters-as-muc]] — Behind the Scenes of Distributed Training and Why Your GPU Wiring Matters as Much as Your Strategy | Towards Data Science — Measured practitioner study (Towards Data Science, 2026) of how the GPU fabric, not the strategy, sets distributed-training throughput. Part 1 (four A100 80GB, HuggingFace Accelerate, BF16): Mistral-7B with Adam needs 87 GB of model state per GPU (14 GB bf16 params + 14 GB grads + ~58 GB fp32 Adam momentum/variance) before any activation, so DDP OOMs on an 80 GB card while FSDP shards it to ~22 GB estimated and ~29 GB actual peak. Measured peak memory for the same 7B job on 4 GPUs across the dial: DDP 87 GB, ZeRO-1 55, ZeRO-2 51, ZeRO-3 40, FSDP 37 — the floor is activations, not model state, so sharding never reaches 87/4. On a fast wire ZeRO-1/2 give up ~20% of DDP throughput and FSDP claws nearly all of it back at the same memory; on a slow wire the whole dial flattens toward the same low throughput. Part 2 (H200 machines, same GPUs wired two ways): NVLink vs forced PCIe on identical silicon is worth ~10-11x on the same collectives; all-reduce bandwidth is 330 GB/s at 2 GPUs rising to 467 at 8 on NVSwitch, 114 GB/s at 2 GPUs and 322 GB/s at 4 inside an NVLink-bridged quad (NV6 links per pair), but only 28/35/39 GB/s at 2/4/8 GPUs when a job must cross between quads over PCIe — the same four cards run 322 GB/s inside a quad vs ~35 GB/s across two, a ~9x difference decided purely by placement. End-to-end, the cross-quad job loses 3-5x throughput, and the loss is not uniform: FSDP falls hardest (it communicates every layer, and per-layer transfers cannot hide behind compute on a slow wire) while DDP holds up best (one all-reduce per step). The operational rule: run nvidia-smi topo -m, and on a slow wire use plain DDP if the model fits replicated; if it does not, pick the least-sharding stage that fits and accept the wire's price. Caveat for this run: the slow wire here is PCIe Gen5 x16 across NVLink quads (~35 GB/s all-reduce), still ~45x faster than the CMP 170HX box's measured ~0.75 GiB/s, so the direction transfers but the magnitudes do not.
- `2026-10-27` [[flashattention-fast-and-memory-eﬃcient-exact-attention]] — FlashAttention: Fast and Memory-Eﬃcient Exact Attention — Dao, Fu, Ermon, Rudra and Re (Stanford / SUNY Buffalo, arXiv 2205.14135, 2022) - IO-aware exact attention: tile the QK^T, softmax and V computation so the N x N score matrix is never materialized in HBM, cutting HBM reads/writes and making the algorithm IO-optimal over a range of SRAM sizes; the backward pass is also computed with linear memory. Reports 15% end-to-end wall-clock speedup on BERT-large (seq 512) against the MLPerf 1.1 record, 3x on GPT-2 (seq 1K), 2.4x on long-range arena (seq 1K-4K), 0.7 better perplexity on GPT-2 and 6.4 points on long-document classification, plus first better-than-chance results on Path-X (16K) and Path-256 (64K); block-sparse FlashAttention is faster than prior approximate methods. Key line for this run: FlashAttention and block-sparse FlashAttention have the same memory footprint, which grows linearly with sequence length - so it removes the quadratic activation-memory term that otherwise dominates a 32k-context step, and any CSA2-versus-full-attention comparison at 32k must hold FlashAttention constant on both arms or the memory comparison is meaningless.
- `2026-10-27` [[an-empirical-model-of-large-batch-training]] — An Empirical Model of Large-Batch Training — McCandlish, Kaplan, Amodei and the OpenAI Dota Team (arXiv 1812.06162, 2018) - introduces the gradient noise scale (the signal-to-noise ratio of the gradient across training examples) as a predictor of the largest useful batch size. Theory: training parallelizes almost linearly up to a batch size equal to the noise scale, then switches smoothly but rapidly to a regime where further parallelism buys almost nothing; the noise scale rises as loss falls, is larger for harder tasks, and depends on model size mainly through improved model performance rather than size per se. Verified across 8 tasks spanning supervised learning (MNIST, SVHN, CIFAR-10, ImageNet, Billion Word), RL (Atari, Dota) and generative autoencoders on SVHN, with a compute-efficiency versus time-efficiency tradeoff model and an analysis of dynamically varying batch size. This is the framework the repo's large-global-batches rule implicitly assumes, and the paper Merrill et al. (2025) test and reject as a proxy under Adam; its evidence base is small-scale and pre-LLM.
- `2026-10-27` [[published-as-a-conference-paper-at-iclr-2018]] — Mixed Precision Training (ICLR 2018) — Micikevicius et al. (NVIDIA with Baidu, ICLR 2018, arXiv 1710.03740) - the standard mixed-precision training recipe: store weights, activations and gradients in IEEE fp16, keep an fp32 master copy of the weights that accumulates the optimizer updates and is rounded to fp16 for forward and backward, use loss scaling to stop small-magnitude gradients flushing to zero, and accumulate fp16 products into fp32 outputs. Nearly halves memory versus fp32 and exploits 2x-8x higher half-precision arithmetic throughput on tensor-core GPUs, with no changes to models or hyperparameters, validated across image classification, generation, detection, language modeling, translation and speech. This is the source of the +2 bytes/parameter master-copy term in every memory budget in this run: bf16 weights (2) + bf16 grads (2) + fp32 master (4) + Adam m and v (8) = 16 bytes per parameter for a fully trainable model, which is what makes 7B fully trainable cost ~112 GB.
- `2026-10-27` [[accurate-large-minibatch-sgd]] — Accurate, Large Minibatch SGD: Training ImageNet in 1 Hour — Goyal et al. (Facebook AI, arXiv 1706.02677, 2017) - the canonical large-minibatch recipe. Shows that on ImageNet large minibatches cause optimization difficulty rather than poor generalization, and that once addressed there is no loss of accuracy up to minibatch 8,192. The two ingredients are a hyperparameter-free linear learning-rate scaling rule (LR proportional to minibatch size) and a gradual warmup over the first epochs that overcomes early optimization problems. Result: ResNet-50 at minibatch 8,192 on 256 GPUs in one hour, matching small-minibatch accuracy, with about 90% scaling efficiency from 8 to 256 GPUs; the rule also transfers to object detection and instance segmentation via Mask R-CNN. This is the mechanism the repo relies on when it says large global batches are the only lever, and it also states the precondition the repo omits: warmup is mandatory, and the rule is derived for SGD with momentum on vision, not for Adam on LLM pretraining (Merrill et al. 2025 argue Adam needs square-root scaling instead).
- `2026-10-27` [[adafactor-adaptive-learning-rates-with-sublinear-memory-cost]] — Adafactor: Adaptive Learning Rates with Sublinear Memory Cost — Shazeer and Stern (Google, arXiv 1804.04235, 2018) - Adafactor replaces Adam's full per-parameter second-moment matrix with a factored approximation: keep only the per-row and per-column sums of the moving average of squared gradients and reconstruct the per-parameter estimate from them, which is exact for rank-1 matrices and requires space proportional to n + m rather than nm. Adds update clipping and a gradually increasing decay rate to stop adaptive methods producing oversized updates, and scales updates relative to parameter scale; drops momentum. Matches the published Adam regime on WMT14 English-German Transformer training while using very little auxiliary optimizer storage. Frames the baseline as Adam's two auxiliary parameters per model parameter tripling memory requirements. For this run: Adafactor state is at most ~4 bytes per parameter (versus 8 for Adam m + v) and far less for large matrices, so it is the cheapest way to keep a 7B model fully trainable inside 64 GB without sharding - though it changes the optimizer and therefore the comparability of any run against the repo's Adam-based baseline.
- `2026-10-27` [[published-as-a-conference-paper-at-iclr-2022]] — 8-Bit Optimizers via Block-Wise Quantization (ICLR 2022) — Dettmers, Lewis, Shleifer and Zettlemoyer (Facebook AI / UW, ICLR 2022, arXiv 2110.02861) - first 8-bit optimizer states that match 32-bit performance, via block-wise dynamic quantization (independent 2,048-element blocks with non-linear mapping, so outliers do not consume the quantization range) plus a stable embedding layer to damp gradient variance from skewed token distributions. States that optimizer state is 33-75% of total training memory and that Adam and Momentum use 8 and 4 bytes per parameter - 8 GB and 4 GB for a 1B model - cut to 2 GB and 1 GB at 8 bits, a 4x reduction on the state term. Validated on 1.5B and 355M language-model pretraining, GLUE, ImageNet, WMT14, MoCo v2 and RoBERTa with no hyperparameter changes; drop-in two-line code change. For this run it is the load-bearing counter-example to the repo's memory arithmetic: 8-bit Adam would take a 7B fully-trainable model's optimizer state from 84 GB (fp32 m + v + master) to roughly 14 GB, which changes the DDP-fits-on-64-GB verdict without freezing any Engram tables.
- `2026-10-27` [[critical-batch-size-revisited-a-simple-empirical]] — Critical Batch Size Revisited: A Simple Empirical Approach to Large-Batch Language Model Training — Merrill, Arora, Groeneveld and Hajishirzi (Allen Institute for AI, NeurIPS 2025; assigned DOI 10.52202/085713-3898, read via arXiv 2505.23971v3) - measures the critical batch size (CBS) directly by branched training from checkpoints instead of via the gradient-noise-scale proxy, which they show rests on two assumptions LLM pretraining violates (SGD optimizer, well-conditioned Hessian) and find unreliable under Adam. On OLMo 1B and 7B with 4,096-token documents, CBS starts near 0 at initialization, rises rapidly and plateaus around 4,096 documents (~16.8M tokens); the trend is the same at 1B and 7B, so CBS measured on small runs transfers upward. They turn this into batch-size warmup: CBS reaches 2,048 documents (~8.4M tokens) by 168B tokens and 4,096 (~16.8M tokens) by 503B tokens, doubling the batch at each point. OLMo 1B trained this way beats the original run by 0.0166 loss after pretraining (0.0053 after mid-training) with 43% fewer gradient steps, while a fixed 4,096-document batch from step 0 uses 75% fewer steps but ends with worse loss than the small-batch control. Scaling rule used is square-root, not linear, because Adam. Implication for this run: a 1M-token global batch is roughly 17x below the measured CBS at plateau, so token efficiency is safe late in training, but CBS is near 0 at initialization - starting at 1M tokens without batch-size warmup or LR compensation is the real risk, and the repo's plan does not mention warmup.
- `2026-10-27` [[arxiv250607280v2-cscv-10-jun-2025]] — arXiv:2506.07280v2  [cs.CV]  10 Jun 2025 — arXiv:2506.07280v2  [cs.CV]  10 Jun 2025
- `2026-10-27` [[zero-3-vs-fsdp-memory-efficiency-for-llm-training-lyceum]] — ZeRO-3 vs FSDP: Memory Efficiency for LLM Training | Lyceum — Vendor explainer (Lyceum, a European GPU cloud) comparing DeepSpeed ZeRO-3 and PyTorch FSDP on memory and communication. The usable content is the standard mixed-precision accounting it repeats: a 7B model holds ~14 GB of bf16/fp16 weights, Adam adds 12 bytes/param (4 fp32 master copy + 4 momentum + 4 variance), fp16 gradients add 2 bytes/param, so model states alone are ~16 bytes/param and a 7B model needs >100 GB of VRAM — more than an A100 40GB. DDP replicates all of that on every GPU (7/8 of aggregate memory wasted at 8 GPUs); ZeRO-3 and FSDP both shard params+grads+optimizer states to O(M/N) and both raise total communication volume by roughly 1.5x versus plain DDP, because they add an all-gather before each forward/backward and a reduce-scatter of gradients. Qualitative claims: ZeRO-3 wins in over-subscription and has richer CPU/NVMe offload (ZeRO-Infinity), FSDP tends to higher TFLOPS/GPU when the model already fits, FSDP2 (torch.distributed.fsdp.fully_shard) shards each parameter on dim 0 as a DTensor and relaxes constraints around frozen parameters and sharded state dicts, and CPU offload costs 2-10x throughput. Relevance: this is the arithmetic behind the repo's '7B fully trainable needs ~112 GB/card' and 'FSDP costs ~1.5x more communication than DDP' claims. Tier caveat: it is a marketing-adjacent blog with zero measurements of its own — the 16 bytes/param figure traces to the ZeRO paper (arXiv 1910.02054) and the 1.5x figure to the FSDP PVLDB paper, both already in the vault; cite those, not this.
- `2026-10-27` [[deepseek-aideepseek-v41-flash-hugging-face]] — deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash · Hugging Face — Official Hugging Face model card for deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash (DeepSeek-AI, 2026, MIT licence) — the compact public statement of the architecture the repo's CSA2 and Engram claims rest on. Model: 552B backbone-parameter multimodal MoE, 1M-token context, Causal Encoder-Decoder of 20 causal-encoder + 20 decoder layers; the decoder's global KV is projected from the final encoder hidden states, so only 8B params activate per token in prefill and 16B in decode. MoE shape: 1 shared expert + 384 routed experts per layer, 6 routed experts active per token. CSA2: each attention layer is statically assigned one of three modes — Full, Reindex, Reuse — sharing main KV and indexer K across layers and reusing Top-K sparse-attention indices; in the decoder a Hierarchical Sparse Indexer restricts later indexing layers to a candidate pool built by the first Full-mode layer, bounding deeper indexer cost independently of context length; FP4 main KV (E2M1 with one E4M3 scale per 16 channels) brings the global KV footprint to 890 bytes/token, ~1/4 of DeepSeek-V4-Flash (and ~437x below V4/V1 per their figure). Other components: SWA Bounded Replay (reconstruct missing sliding-window KV by replaying the most recent n_win tokens, cutting persistent KV to ~1/8 of V4-Flash), Engram conditional memory at 196B parameters sparsely accessed by token-based lookup, Single-Pass mHC, DSpark speculative decoding. Training: from scratch on 45T multimodal tokens, with sparse attention trained at a sequence length of 64K and context extended to 1M at 34T tokens. Relevance: this is the primary source for two claims under test — CSA2's training sequence length is 64K, not 32k, and Engram tables are a 196B-parameter block inside a 552B model, i.e. frozen memory can dominate total parameter count. Caveat: model card only; no training throughput, no indexer FLOP accounting, no per-layer mode census (that detail is in arXiv 2609.19969, already in the vault).
- `2026-10-27` [[periodic-weak-spots]] — Periodic Weak Spots: Phase Sensitivity from Chunked KV-Cache Compression — Periodic Weak Spots (Zhu, Yi, Cheng, Lv, Liu, Ying, Ma, Dong — ByteDance Seed + Princeton/Stanford/Berkeley, arXiv 2609.36322v1, 28 Sep 2026): chunked KV-cache compression (the CSA family used by DeepSeek-V4) introduces a positional coordinate — a token's phase, its position modulo the compression stride S — and retrieval accuracy varies sharply and periodically with it. Evidence: (1) DeepSeek-V4-Flash-Base completing its own inference code flips its top-1 token with period 4, matching CSA's stride S=4; (2) controlled needle-in-a-haystack at 128K context shows up to a 40-percentage-point retrieval swing across phases in the DeepSeek-V4 family (V4-Flash-Base, V4-Flash-0731, post-trained V4.1-Flash all show it, with periods of 2 or 4); (3) they pretrain from scratch a family of transformers on the Qwen3-0.6B backbone — 100B tokens, sequence length 2,048, global batch 1,024 sequences, BF16, AdamW, 47,518 steps — with KV compression as the only architectural change (reference W8/S8, strides 4/6/8/12, uniform-average gates, no-RoPE, partial-RoPE, plus DeepSeek-V4's CSA kernel on the Qwen backbone). Every compressed variant is phase-sensitive with period equal to its stride; full-attention baselines are flat (Prefix-Padding gaps up to 78pp for compressed vs at most 6.1pp for baselines). The effect is invisible to averages: the 8-KV-head W8/S8 model nearly matches its full-attention baseline on mean accuracy and validation loss, yet its worst residue is 9.9% vs 58.4% for the baseline. Mechanistic work (KV-head knockouts, attention-readout mean replacement) shows phase specialization — different heads/layers serve different phases — and an idealized induction task proves gradient flow drives compression gates to sharp, persistent phase preferences. Prescription: evaluate compressed-attention models by shifting inputs a few tokens, not by average accuracy. Relevance to this run: it is direct evidence that CSA-style compression has a training-time failure mode that only shows up across phases, and it demonstrates the whole effect reproduces at 0.6B params / 100B tokens / 2k context — i.e. inside this box's budget. It says nothing about CSA2's indexer compute cost or about 32k training; the DeepSeek-V4 training length claim has to come from the V4.1 report.
- `2026-10-27` [[deepseek-v4-flash-on-amd-gfx90a]] — DeepSeek-V4-Flash on AMD gfx90a — DeepSeek-V4-Flash inference on AMD MI250 (gfx90a/CDNA2) with SGLang (Siming Huang, HKUST-GZ, arXiv 2609.15627v2, Sep 2026) — an enablement and measurement report, not a training study. Model: 284B total / ~13B active, FP4 routed experts, mostly FP8 non-expert weights, 48 safetensors shards. Host: 8 MI250 GCDs (~64 GiB HBM each) on one Supermicro AS-4124GQ-TNMI, 1 TiB DDR4. Topology: a single TP8/EP1 instance — explicitly 'no routed-expert A2A' — with a 1,048,576-token logical KV pool. Measured (three rounds per group, native autoregressive): resident decode C1 88.63, C32 1041.78, C64 1327.10 output tok/s; C16 prefill 10,413.87 / 10,263.02 / 9,966.84 input tok/s at ~8K / 16K / 32K input length; 8K public-source prefill 7,035-10,431 tok/s across C1-64. Accepted prefill config = reduced indexer replication + mHC reuse + paired-GCD attention + fixed-order expert reduction. Honest limits stated: no filled-1M-context performance or quality certification, dynamic batching and cold-shape compilation untested, component equality does not prove numerical equivalence, and the report covers V4 not V4.1/Engram offload. Relevance to this run: it is the closest published datapoint for running a 284B CSA-family MoE across eight 64 GB cards with tensor parallelism and zero expert all-to-all — i.e. TP8 + EP1 is the topology a serving stack chose when it wanted to avoid routed-expert A2A entirely. Caveat: the interconnect is never named (MI250 GCDs are Infinity-Fabric-linked), so the TP8 result is not evidence that TP8 is viable on Gen2 x4.
- `2026-10-27` [[technical-report]] — Pangu Ultra MoE: How to Train Your Big MoE on Ascend NPUs — Pangu Ultra MoE technical report (Huawei Pangu team, arXiv 2505.04519v1, May 2025): a 718B-parameter sparse MoE (256 experts, topk=8 plus shared experts, chosen via pilot runs on a 20B/1.6B-active model and a throughput simulator) trained on 6K Ascend 910B NPUs at MFU 30.0% and 1.46M tokens/s, versus a 4K-NPU baseline of 18.9% MFU / 0.61M TPS. The system half is the relevant part for a no-P2P PCIe box: it quantifies what expert parallelism costs and what it takes to hide that cost. Table 4 gives per-layer communication volumes — plain AllGather dispatch is Seq x TP x EP inter-node; plain All-to-All is Seq x TopK; their Hierarchical EP converts inter-node All-to-All into an inter-node AllGather of Seq x (EP-1) followed by an intra-node All-to-All of Seq x TopK, cutting total volume and letting the two stages overlap with forward and backward compute. Adaptive Pipe Overlap then overlaps EP A2A with finer-grained compute; Table 5 attributes +28.6% MFU to that overlap alone (baseline 1 -> +15.8% fine-grained recompute/swap -> +28.6% adaptive pipe overlap -> +49.2% host optimisation -> +58.7% fused operators, cumulative 30.0% MFU). Design constraints they hit: an excessively large EP degree introduces large communication overhead and degrades performance, so EP degree is chosen jointly against memory and comms; and large TP on granular experts (2048 intermediate size) shrinks matmuls enough to lose efficiency. Caveat: Ascend 910B has an HCCS intra-node fabric and a dedicated inter-node network — nothing here transfers numerically to Gen2 x4, but the structure of the EP cost (volume x per-link bandwidth, hidden only by overlap) does.
- `2026-10-27` [[simsmoe-toward-efficient-training-mixture-of-experts-via-solving-representationa]] — SimSMoE: Toward Efficient Training Mixture of Experts via Solving Representational Collapse - ACL Anthology — SimSMoE (Do, Le & Tran, Findings of NAACL 2025, pp. 2012-2025; DOI 10.18653/v1/2025.findings-naacl.107) targets representation collapse in sparse mixture-of-experts training — the failure where experts converge to near-identical representations, wasting parameters and hurting quality. It proposes a similarity-based routing algorithm that provably drives experts apart under a fixed FLOPs budget, and evaluates on three LLMs for both pre-training and fine-tuning, reporting gains over existing SMoE routing policies. Code at github.com/giangdip2410/SimSMoE. NOTE: this note is the ACL Anthology landing page only (abstract + BibTeX, ~1.1k words) — no numbers, no training-cost data; the primary PDF is aclanthology.org/2025.findings-naacl.107.pdf. Value here is corroborating that expert collapse / load balancing is a first-order MoE training risk at the scale this box can reach, not providing quantitative evidence.
- `2026-10-27` [[routing-free-mixture-of-experts]] — Routing-Free Mixture-of-Experts — Routing-Free MoE (Liu et al., LMU Munich/UCLA/TUM, arXiv 2604.00801v1, Apr 2026) removes the centralized router entirely — no external router, Softmax, TopK or hard-coded load balancing — and lets each expert self-activate when its own internal low-rank confidence score (||x A_gate,i||_2) clears a global threshold theta, with a unified token-balancing + expert-balancing auxiliary loss that interpolates between the two objectives. Trained from scratch on OpenWebText for one epoch at three scales up to 0.8B params (scale S = 92.44M), iso-FLOP against standard MoE/AoE/ReMoE: better PPL and +0.80pp mean downstream at every scale, and stable where standard MoE diverges at lr 2e-3. The load-bearing part for this run is Appendix B.1, which gives an alpha-beta model of expert parallelism: standard MoE pays 2(M-1)*alpha + 2*K*T*D*b/(M*B) per layer for the two blocking all-to-alls, and Routing-Free MoE replaces the dispatch barrier with a non-blocking all-gather plus async point-to-point combine. Measured EP throughput at scale S (prefill T=1024, decode 128 steps, bf16, batch 1): standard MoE prefill falls 22,346 -> 18,034 tok/s (-19%) going M=1 -> M=2 and does not recover at M=3/4; decode falls 57.90 -> 45.65 tok/s (-21%); Routing-Free MoE keeps >=97.8% of single-device prefill and >=95.6% of decode at M=4 (1.19x prefill, 1.12x decode vs MoE at M=4). Caveat: the accelerator model and interconnect are never stated, and these are inference-stage (prefill/decode) numbers, not training — so the 19-21% M=1->2 penalty is a lower bound for a Gen2 x4 no-P2P box, not a measurement on it.
- `2026-10-27` [[eigenvectors-of-experts-are-training-free-non-collapsing-routers]] — Eigenvectors of Experts are Training-free Non-collapsing Routers — Do, Le and Tran (Deakin University A2I2, ICML 2026, arXiv 2605.30992) - SSMoE, a training-free router for sparse MoE. Instead of a learned gating matrix it takes the top singular vectors of each expert's weight matrices (SVD), builds a spectral embedding per expert, and routes each token by similarity to those directions. The motivating claim is that expert collapse persists even in well-pretrained SMoE models and that existing router fixes require training from scratch or fine-tuning, which is expensive in compute and data. Reports roughly 13% average gain over baselines across eight datasets and about 6% over the original GPT-OSS models while cutting memory roughly 23%, evaluated on GPT-OSS-20B (38.96 GB), GPT-OSS-120B (217.61 GB), Qwen3-30B, Qwen3-Next-80B, DeepSeek-16B, Qwen1.5-MoE and OLMoE-7B. For this run: a frozen, weight-derived router removes router parameters and auxiliary load-balancing losses from the trainable budget and removes router collapse as a failure mode, which matters for short-budget MoE runs on a 5-GPU box where a collapsed router wastes the whole run.
- `2026-10-27` [[when-sparse-reward-meets-dense-distilla]] — When Sparse Reward Meets Dense Distillation: Training Dynamics of On-Policy Distillation — When Sparse Reward Meets Dense Distillation (Peking University, arXiv 2609.34849, Sep 2026, ~21k words) - NTK analysis of hybrid RLVR plus on-policy teacher-KL training. Defines a token-level cross-signal NTK K_DR(n) measuring the alignment between reward and distillation gradients at each position, and identifies two failure modes: magnitude drowning (the reward gradient exceeds the distillation gradient by orders of magnitude, so even weak directional conflict makes the distillation loss rise) and localized directional conflict (K_DR(n) < 0, where the sequence-level advantage and the teacher's position-specific distribution push opposite ways). The gradient-norm ratio kappa = ||grad L_R|| / ||grad L_D|| is stable within a run but varies by roughly an order of magnitude across tasks, with an empirical threshold above which naive scalar mixing collapses persistently (Qwen3-1.7B on GSM8K collapses by step 500). Proposes M3 (magnitude normalization plus an NTK mask, a soft relaxation, or a fast-slow extragradient step) and reports stable training across four backbones and four benchmarks where scalar mixing collapses. Relevance to this run is peripheral: it is a post-training RL result, not a pretraining-feasibility one. Its only bearing is that any distillation-based objective (for example a CSA2 indexer distillation loss) needs explicit gradient-magnitude balancing, and that on-policy distillation keeps a teacher and a policy resident at once, which is a memory problem on 64 GB cards.
- `2026-10-27` [[f43939791a7ceb0b01a4937308317be5]] — FineWeb-Edu Classifier (HuggingFaceTB/fineweb-edu-classifier) model card — Model card (HuggingFaceTB/fineweb-edu-classifier, mirrored as a gist by Quentin Anthony) for the classifier used to build the FineWeb-Edu dataset: a Snowflake-arctic-embed-m encoder with a single regression head, trained for 20 epochs at LR 3e-4 on 450k Llama3-70B-instruct educational-quality annotations (scores 0-5) sampled from FineWeb, with the embedding and encoder layers frozen. Reports F1 82% as a binary classifier at score threshold 3, accuracy 0.71 and macro-F1 0.50 on a 46,867-sample hold-out; per-class recall collapses at the top of the scale (class 5 recall 0.01, class 4 0.35, class 3 0.50), and the card recommends int_score >= 3 as the curation threshold. Relevant to the data-pipeline axis (the repo's 9B-token FineWeb-Edu mix) rather than to topology or memory questions; it is the ground-truth artifact behind any claim about what FineWeb-Edu filtering actually does.
- `2026-10-27` [[memory-efficient-llm-training]] — Memory Efficient LLM Training — Archie Sengupta blog, Memory Efficient LLM Training (~5,100 words) - practitioner survey of the training-memory stack, useful mainly as an arithmetic reference. Per-parameter accounting: fp32 weights 4P, fp32 grads 4P, Adam first plus second moment 8P, bf16 copy 2P; activations grow quadratically with sequence length and linearly with batch. Gradient checkpointing: full checkpointing costs ~30-40% compute overhead, every-K checkpointing gives ~Kx memory for 20-30% overhead. Gradient accumulation: memory stays at micro-batch size while AllReduce frequency falls with accumulation. ZeRO stages: per-GPU memory 2P + 8P/N (stage 1 and 2) and 12P/N (stage 3), with stage 3 costing O(N) AllGathers per layer per forward/backward pass, which is why ZeRO-3 is normally paired with tensor parallelism inside a node. ZeRO-Offload and FSDP CPU offload cost 10-20% to 30-50% slowdown. Its topology table assumes NVLink at 900 GB/s for TP and PP and InfiniBand at 50 GB/s for DP - there is no PCIe-Gen2-only case anywhere in the piece, and its only fabric-relevant statement is that TP must stay within a node (NVLink required). Internal inconsistency worth flagging: it states Adam needs 8 bytes per parameter but its total equation is P x (4+4+4+2) = 14 bytes, i.e. only 4 bytes of optimizer state; the repo's 112 GB / 7B figure uses the correct 12 bytes (fp32 m + fp32 v + fp32 master).
- `2026-10-27` [[revisiting-small-batch-training-for]] — Revisiting Small Batch Training for Deep Neural Networks — Masters and Luschi (Graphcore Research, arXiv 1804.07612, Apr 2018) - systematic CIFAR-10 / CIFAR-100 / ImageNet sweep arguing against thousand-scale minibatches. With a learning rate chosen to hold the average weight update per gradient computation constant, the variance of the weight update grows linearly with minibatch size m, so larger batches progressively shrink the range of learning rates that give stable convergence and acceptable test performance. Best test performance is consistently at m = 2..32, explicitly contrasted with recent work advocating minibatch sizes in the thousands. Also argues that Hoffer et al.'s same-number-of-updates prescription imposes a compute overhead proportional to m, negating the parallelism gain, and that small batches give more up-to-date gradients. This is the counter-position to the repo's large-global-batches rule, but note the scope: it measures generalization and optimization stability for SGD on vision tasks, not communication overhead or Adam-based LLM pretraining.
- `2026-10-27` [[memory-efficient-mixed-precision-optimizers]] — MEMORY EFFICIENT MIXED PRECISION OPTIMIZERS — Lewandowski and Kosson (EPFL, arXiv 2309.12381, Sep 2023) - mixed-precision optimizer that removes the fp32 master copy of the weights by storing only the residual extra bits between the bf16/fp16 value and its fp32 original, and fuses the optimizer step into the backward pass so gradients never persist in memory. Claims up to 25% lower peak memory and 15% faster training at equal accuracy; measured 54% lower peak memory on a synthetic 2B-parameter SGD run (against a stated theoretical maximum of 60%), 11% lower peak memory when fine-tuning T5 on GLUE with no meaningful slowdown, 16% faster on ResNet-18, and 20-25% reduction in realistic training. States the baseline budget explicitly: standard AMP keeps an fp32 weight plus fp16 copy (6 bytes), an fp32 gradient (4 bytes) and Adam state (8 bytes) per parameter, and notes Adafactor and 8-bit Adam cut state to 4 and 2 bytes. Two caveats that matter for this run: round-to-zero on the stored significand makes the compute-side bf16 value less accurate than round-to-nearest mixed precision, and fusing backward with the optimizer step makes gradient accumulation impossible - which rules the technique out for any batch plan built on accumulation to reach a 1M-token global batch.
- `2026-10-27` [[noname-manuscript-no]] — Mesa: A Memory-saving Training Framework for Transformers — Mesa (Monash University, arXiv 2111.11124) - memory-saving training framework for Transformers that keeps the forward pass in exact precision but stores only a low-precision (int8) copy of each activation, dequantizing it on the backward pass to recompute gradients. Two additions make it work: head-wise activation quantization (per-attention-head clipping ranges and offsets instead of per-tensor, because MSA heads have heterogeneous activation distributions) and running estimates (RE) of the quantization parameters so no extra forward pass is needed. Reports up to 50% training-memory reduction at comparable or slightly better accuracy: DeiT-Ti 4,149 MB -> 2,000 MB (RE) with ImageNet Top-1 71.9 -> 72.1; DeiT-B -51%, Swin-Ti -55%; compressing MSA alone -27%, FFN alone -21%. The cost is throughput: DeiT-Ti falls from 1,196 to 431 images/s (about 2.8x slower) with RE, and compressing the whole model halves training speed. Nearest rounding without stochastic rounding fails to converge on ImageNet (Table 3). Under the same memory budget it doubles DeiT-Ti batch size 1,024 -> 2,048 (33.4 GB vs 28.8 GB) for +0.8% Top-1. Crucially, the paper argues the throughput penalty is partly hidden in distributed training because communication dominates, and reports PVT-Ti costs only +15% GPU hours. Vision-only (ImageNet, CIFAR-100, ADE20K): no LLM or long-context experiments, so the 32k-activation-memory case is untested.
- `2026-10-27` [[nvidia-a100-tensor-core-gpu-data-sheet-jun21-1]] — NVIDIA A100 TENSOR CORE GPU  |  DATA SHEET  |  Jun21  |  1 — NVIDIA's official A100 datasheet (Jun21 revision), fetched as the authority behind the arXiv study's SM-count scaling. Gives the reference numbers needed to sanity-check the repo's measured 162 TFLOPS bf16/GPU: BFLOAT16 Tensor Core 312 TFLOPS dense (624 with sparsity) and FP16 Tensor Core 312 TFLOPS dense, TF32 156 TFLOPS, FP32 19.5 TFLOPS, FP64 9.7 TFLOPS (19.5 via FP64 Tensor Core), across both SXM4 and PCIe form factors. The A100 40 GB PCIe card has 1,555 GB/s HBM2 and a 250 W TDP - the same TDP as the CMP 170HX - while the 80 GB PCIe part is 1,935 GB/s at 300 W and the SXM4 80 GB is 2,039 GB/s at 400 W. Interconnect is PCIe Gen4 at 64 GB/s or NVLink at 600 GB/s.

Why it matters here: the A100 has 108 SMs and the CMP 170HX has 70 (74 after the cmpunlocker SM re-enable), so a linear SM scaling puts the CMP ceiling at roughly 202 TFLOPS bf16 dense (214 at 74 SM). The repo's measured 162 TFLOPS/GPU is therefore about 80% of that ceiling - physically plausible for an unlocked card running at a 250 W cap - whereas the stock-card ~42 TFLOPS FP16 in the arXiv study is roughly a fifth of the same ceiling, which is the signature of tensor-core paths not being engaged before the unlock.
- `2026-10-27` [[cmp-170hx-inference-labdocsunlock-and-modsmd-at-main-dl4rcecmp-170hx-inference-l]] — cmp-170hx-inference-lab/docs/UNLOCK-AND-MODS.md at main · dl4rce/cmp-170hx-inference-lab · GitHub — Short (573-word) but load-bearing caveat doc from the dl4rce lab. Its central point is that the two unlocks are orthogonal: the software unlock (patched nvidia-open 610.43.x) gives compute throughput, VRAM geometry (64 GB on the 8 GB SKU, 40 GB on the 10 GB SKU) and PCIe Gen2 *speed*, while PCIe *width* x16 requires populating the missing AC-coupling capacitors on 12 of the 16 unpopulated lanes. Neither delivers PCIe Gen3/Gen4, NVLink, ECC, or native FP8/FP4.

SKU lottery detail that matters for capacity planning: device id 10de:20c2 (8 GB, often Hynix) is the 64 GB unlock; 10de:2082 (10 GB, often Samsung) is stable at 40 GB, and the 80 GB geometry has been reported unstable with GSP/DMA faults, so '80 GB 170HX' listings should not be trusted. The FMA lock is itself a lottery until the unlock is applied and a microbench confirms real FP32/BF16 throughput. Also states plainly that multi-GPU tensor parallel over this PCIe is a trap, citing the public DeepSeek-V4-on-four-170HX write-ups that had to use pipeline parallel instead, and that the cards ship at 250 W with no display output and usually no NVENC.
- `2026-10-27` [[2x-cmp-170hx-for-llm-inference-unlock-cross-root-p2p-serving-vllm-epycromed8-2-2]] — 2x CMP 170HX for LLM inference: unlock, cross-root P2P, serving (vLLM, EPYC/ROMED8-2T) · GitHub — The original primary gist behind the wpnews.pro article in this batch, fetched to verify the reprint. Content is identical in substance - the same prefill table (no-P2P ~2500 tok/s flat; P2P 70 SM 4634/4572/4543/4486 across 8k/32k/65k/128k; 74 SM 4766/4708/4669/4599; +200 MHz offset 5033/4989/4951/4881) and the same P2P recipe (amoghmunikote/cmpunlocker base plus bayley/cmpunlocker patches 0011-p2p-bar1, 0013-skip-mailbox-peer-preinit, 0015-bar1p2p-readcap-override, plus regkey ForceP2P=0x11). So the aggregator did not distort the numbers; cite this gist as the authority and the wpnews note as the convenient copy.

Its distinctive contributions beyond the reprint: the explicit 2026-10-02 revision note that the first P2P setup was not reproducible for some users, the pinning of exact commits (cmpunlocker 6c442ee, bayley 5a7bb4b), the statement that the regkey alone was never verified sufficient, the 74-SM result from re-enabling reserved TPCs, the 300 W VBIOS cross-flash with thermal ceilings (200 W plateaus 77 C, 250 W runs away), and the +200 MHz GVF offset that is stable under real load where +250 hung a card with Xid 175.
- `2026-10-27` [[arxiv230516264v5-cscl-28-jun-2025]] — Scaling Data-Constrained Language Models (arXiv 2305.16264v5) — Scaling Data-Constrained Language Models (Muennighoff et al., arXiv 2305.16264v5, revised 28 Jun 2025) is the primary behind the '4-5 epoch' repetition threshold that SmolLM2 cites when sizing its data mixture. Setup: more than 400 models from 10M to 9B parameters, trained for up to 1500 epochs, with budgets ranging up to 900B training tokens; the headline experiment uses a 4.2B-parameter model. All models and datasets are released as huggingface/datablations.

Core findings: (1) with constrained data at a fixed compute budget, training with up to 4 epochs of repeated data yields negligible loss change versus unique data, and differences in downstream task performance are insignificant up to around 4 epochs (25% of budget) before dropping; (2) beyond that, the value of additional compute decays to zero — the fitted data-constrained scaling law replaces the raw token count D with an effective-token count D' = U_D + U_D*R_D*(1 - e^(-R_D/R*_D)), where R*_D is the 'half-life' of repeated data; (3) in the data-constrained regime, compute should be allocated to both more parameters and more epochs, with epochs scaled slightly faster than Chinchilla would predict.

Mitigations: mixing Python code into natural-language training provides a 2x increase in effective tokens even when evaluated only on natural-language tasks, and filling up to 50% of the data with code (42B tokens) shows no deterioration. On filtering, perplexity filtering is effective but deduplication does not help on their (clean) benchmark; they recommend reserving filtering for noisy datasets.

Why it matters here: it bounds how far a 9B-token mix can be stretched on a fixed box. If the unique-token pool is ~9B, roughly 36B tokens of training are available at the 4-epoch threshold before returns collapse — the real constraint on how long a run this hardware can justify, not the FLOP budget alone.
- `2026-10-27` [[training-compute-optimal-large-language-models]] — Training Compute-Optimal Large Language Models — Training Compute-Optimal Large Language Models (Hoffmann et al., DeepMind, arXiv 2203.15556) is the primary behind the '20 tokens per parameter' rule that FineWeb and SmolLM2 both use to size their ablations. The paper fits three independent approaches — minimum over training curves, IsoFLOP profiles, and parametric loss modelling — on models from 70M to over 16B parameters trained on 5B to over 400B tokens, and finds that model size and training tokens should be scaled in approximately equal proportions as compute grows: exponents a = 0.50 / b = 0.50 (approach 1), 0.49 / 0.51 (approach 2), 0.46 / 0.54 (approach 3), versus Kaplan et al.'s 0.73 / 0.27.

The practical frontier (Table 3, approach 1) gives exact FLOPs and token counts: 400M params -> 1.92e19 FLOPs / 8.0B tokens; 1B -> 1.21e20 FLOPs / 20.2B tokens; 10B -> 1.23e22 FLOPs / 205.1B tokens; 67B -> 5.76e23 FLOPs / 1.5T tokens; 175B -> 3.85e24 / 3.7T; 1T -> 1.27e26 / 21.2T. The FLOPs constraint used throughout is FLOPs(N,D) = 6ND.

Headline result: Chinchilla (70B params, 1.4T tokens) trained on the same compute budget as Gopher (280B) uniformly and significantly outperforms Gopher, GPT-3 (175B) and Jurassic-1 (178B), reaching 67.5% on MMLU, a >7% improvement over Gopher. The paper's central claim is that the models of its era were considerably over-sized relative to their compute budgets.

Why it matters here: 6ND plus the Table 3 frontier converts the box's ~810 TFLOPS bf16 into a concrete answer to 'how big a model and how many tokens can this run afford' — a 1B model at 20.2B tokens costs 1.21e20 FLOPs, a multi-day-to-multi-week run at realistic MFU, while a 10B model at 205B tokens (1.23e22 FLOPs) is out of reach. It also justifies the repo's choice of a ~1B-trainable model rather than a larger one at fixed compute.
- `2026-10-27` [[readmemd]] — README.md — PixelML/club-170hx results/2026-10-01-cmp170hx-3card-p2p-plx/README.md (505 words) — the sanitized evidence receipt behind the NCCL SHM all-reduce numbers cited in BENCHMARKS.md, and therefore the primary measurement underpinning any DDP gradient-reduction arithmetic on this hardware. Setup: 3x CMP 170HX memory-unlocked to 65,536 MiB each, all at Gen2 x16 on the same CPU socket, GPU0/GPU1 behind one PLX PEX 8747 and GPU2 behind a second, ACS at firmware default with request/completion redirect enabled (ACSCtl 0x001d), 250 W cap, dual Xeon E5-2686 v4, Proxmox VE 9, kernel 7.0.2, bare metal, driver 615.71.09 with upstream cmpunlocker @ 88e39ce plus rebar-serialize.patch and explicitly NO BAR1-P2P patches. All-reduce measured with torch 2.13.0+cu130 / NCCL 2.29.7 under torchrun, bf16 sum, 5 warm-ups, median of 20, bus bandwidth = algbw x 2(n-1)/n. Results: canAccessPeer/enablePeerAccess returns 0 / CUDA_ERROR_PEER_ACCESS_UNSUPPORTED (217) on all 6 pairs and nvidia-smi topo -p2p r reports GNS, yet an alias-proof cuMemcpyPeer passes on all 6 pairs because the driver stages through host memory. cuMemcpyPeer at 256 MiB runs 6.21-6.30 GB/s against a single-card D2H reference of 6.68 GB/s; latency 22 us / 40 us / 343 us at 4 KiB / 64 KiB / 1 MiB versus D2H 9.5 / 18.4 / 165 us. Same-switch and cross-switch differ by <=1%, i.e. nothing takes the switch-local path. NCCL all-reduce bus bandwidth at 256 MiB: 3.69 GB/s (same-switch pair), 3.85 GB/s (cross-switch pair), 4.24 GB/s (all three); at 8 KiB the times are 144 / 120 / 134 us. NCCL transport is SHM/direct for every pair. Limitations stated by the authors: true BAR1 P2P was untested here so this is the no-P2P baseline, and there is one probe run per pair with no cross-run error bars.
- `2026-10-27` [[environment-variables-nccl-2323-documentation]] — Environment Variables — NCCL 2.32.3 documentation — NVIDIA's official NCCL 2.32.3 environment-variable reference - the primary source behind the tuning claims in the ClusterBid blog. Load-bearing entries for a no-P2P PCIe box:

NCCL_P2P_DISABLE disables the peer-to-peer transport ("CUDA direct access between GPUs, using NVLink or PCI"). NCCL_P2P_LEVEL sets the maximum topological distance at which P2P is used, with named levels LOC (never), NVL (NVLink), PIX (same PCI switch), PXB (through PCI switches, possibly multiple hops), PHB (same NUMA node, traffic through the CPU) and SYS (across NUMA nodes, potentially crossing QPI/UPI). Integer legacy values 0-4 map to LOC/PIX/PXB/PHB/SYS; NVL has no legacy integer.

NCCL_SHM_DISABLE documents the fallback path that matters most here: "SHM is used between devices when peer-to-peer cannot happen, therefore, host memory is used." That is the primary confirmation that a no-P2P box stages every collective through host RAM, and it names the knob (NCCL_SHM_DISABLE) that would force the network path instead.

NCCL_ALGO accepts Ring, Tree, Collnet, CollnetChain, CollnetDirect, NVLS, NVLSTree and PAT (2.23+), with per-function selection syntax since 2.24 and caret-prefixed exclusion lists. Default is unset, i.e. NCCL picks automatically from node topology and architecture. NCCL_PROTO accepts LL, LL128 and Simple; the docs warn users are "discouraged from setting this variable" and that "enabling LL128 on platforms that don't support it can lead to data corruption" - a direct caution against blindly copying tuning recipes.

Other relevant knobs: NCCL_BUFFSIZE, NCCL_NTHREADS, NCCL_MIN_NCHANNELS / NCCL_MAX_NCHANNELS (channel count trades CUDA blocks for bandwidth), NCCL_NVB_DISABLE (intra-node NVLink via an intermediate GPU), NCCL_PXN_DISABLE, NCCL_NET_GDR_LEVEL, and NCCL_IB_* for RDMA fabrics. Notably the docs do not support the blog's claim that NCCL_ALGO=Ring with NCCL_PROTO=LL is a recommended setting for >256-GPU clusters; that is vendor-blog folklore, not documented guidance.
- `2026-10-27` [[lessonsmd]] — LESSONS.md — PixelML/club-170hx LESSONS.md (5,664 words — LONG, candidate for source-analyst), fetched via the raw-markdown mirror because the github.com blob URL hit a login redirect. This is the per-checkpoint companion to TOPOLOGY-AND-PARALLELISM.md and the richest single source in this batch for the training-topology question. §c states the fabric premise in the corpus's own words: 'The tested CMP 170HX fabric has no NVLink and no P2P over PCIe Gen2 x4 (about 1.0 GB/s measured bus bandwidth between cards). This one fact decides the topology choice.' It then quantifies the TP/PP asymmetry: tensor parallel performs 2 all-reduces per layer, i.e. 86 collectives per forward pass on a 43-layer model, and is communication-bound at every sequence length (TP4 prefill measures flat at ~800 tok/s from 1.5k to 77k tokens); pipeline parallel moves the payload once per stage boundary (3 hand-offs for 4 stages) and overlaps transfer with compute, about 28x less data on the wire than TP. Measured effect on the same DeepSeek-V4-Flash-0731 checkpoint and cards: PP beats TP by 6.6x on prefill (5,321 vs 801 tok/s at 77k context) and roughly 2x on aggregate decode; TTFT at 100k context is PP4 14.6 s vs TP4 87.3 s. §a is an SM80 kernel/format compatibility table: FP8/FP4 storage formats dequantize fine but FP8/FP4 tensor-core compute does not exist on SM80, so DeepGEMM, CuteDSL, Triton fp8e4nv stores, tilelang FP8xFP4 GEMM and NVFP4 native execution all fail; AWQ/GPTQ INT4 via Marlin, W8A16, BF16, flash_attention and DSpark/DFlash2 all work. §f contains the single most important number for reconciling the repo's compute premise: the PWRBRK# power-brake hazard is measured as 'fp16 throughput 39.3 vs 155.7 TFLOPS healthy' — so a healthy unlocked card measures ~155.7 TFLOPS fp16, which corroborates the repo's ~162 TFLOPS/GPU bf16 claim and contradicts both the 170th-Street spec sheet's 49.05 TFLOPS and club-170hx's own 74.6-78.5 TFLOP/s 'tensor-core gate' figure. §d: speculative-decoding acceptance is checkpoint-specific (DSpark k=5 gives 3.03 mean acceptance on DeepSeek-V4-Flash-0731; k=7 measures worse at 60.3 vs 98.1 tok/s because acceptance never extends past ~3 tokens), FP8 KV costs -3.4% decode and -19.5% prefill versus BF16 KV, and DSpark output is not bit-reproducible at temperature 0. §e: host-RAM exhaustion from eager safetensors loading OOM-killed workers silently at 7 of 48 shards on a 94 GiB host; NFS cold loads measured 31.01 MiB/s versus ~500 MiB/s local NVMe. §h: independent reports put field failure around 50% among buyers, with faults appearing only above the 8 GiB region a mining rig would touch — a strict pre-purchase QC gate (memtest_vulkan, vram-gate allocating ~61 GiB) is mandatory, not optional. §g is a failure-mode table (Xid 79 bus drop needs a cold power cycle, Xid 31 MMU fault, Xid 154, NCCL store timeout from storage-read skew, seccomp blocking pidfd_getfd).
- `2026-10-27` [[github-facebookresearchdietgpu-gpu-implementation-of-a-fast-generalized-ans-asym]] — GitHub - facebookresearch/dietgpu: GPU implementation of a fast generalized ANS (asymmetric numeral system) entropy encoder and decoder, with extensions for lossless compression of numerical and other data types in HPC/ML applications. · GitHub — DietGPU (Meta / facebookresearch, author Jeff Johnson) is the reference GPU lossless-compression library that ZipCCL benchmarks against and beats. Two components: a generalized byte-oriented range-based ANS (rANS) entropy encoder/decoder running at ~250-410 GB/s for reasonable data sizes on an A100, and a floating-point codec extending it to fast lossless compression/decompression of unstructured FP data, explicitly aimed at "communicating over local interconnects (PCIe / NVLink) and remote interconnects (Ethernet / InfiniBand)", running at ~250-600 GB/s on an A100. Both C++ (raw device pointers) and Python/PyTorch tensor APIs.

The throughput numbers are the useful datum for this run: compress/decompress at 250-600 GB/s on an A100 is two to three orders of magnitude above the ~0.75 GiB/s collective bandwidth measured on the CMP 170HX box, so on a bandwidth-starved fabric the compression compute is effectively free - the bottleneck is the link, not the codec. That inverts the usual objection that lossless compression costs more than it saves.

Status caveat: the repository was archived by the owner on 14 Jun 2026 and is read-only; the README still labels it "very early alpha preview ... under rapid development". ZipCCL's own measurement is that DietGPU-compressed all-gather was slower than plain NCCL all-gather on their platform, so a naive DietGPU integration is not a drop-in win - kernel/data-layout engineering matters (which is exactly what ZipCCL claims to add).
- `2026-10-27` [[coat-compressing-optimizer-states-and-activation-for-memory-efficient-fp8-traini]] — COAT: Compressing Optimizer states and Activation for Memory-Efficient FP8 Training — COAT (Xi, Cai, Zhu, Lu, Keutzer, Chen, Han; UC Berkeley / NVIDIA / MIT / Tsinghua; arXiv 2410.19313, NVlabs) is the FP8 training framework AGoQ benchmarks against. Two innovations: Dynamic Range Expansion, which shifts optimizer-state distributions to better fill the FP8 representable range and so cuts quantisation error; and Mixed-Granularity Activation Quantization, which combines per-tensor and per-group strategies for activation memory.

Results: 1.54x end-to-end training-memory reduction versus BF16 with nearly lossless quality across LLM pretraining, fine-tuning and VLM training; 1.43x end-to-end training speedup versus BF16, on par with or better than Transformer Engine. It enables full-parameter training of large models on fewer GPUs and "facilitates doubling the batch size in distributed training settings". The worked example is Llama-2-13B on 8x80GB H100 under FSDP.

Relevance here: COAT is the memory-side counterpart to the communication-side FP8 work, and its 1.54x memory reduction is the strongest published lever for fitting a larger trainable parameter count per card. But like FP8-LM it is Hopper-native - the paper explicitly ties FP8 to "the advent of Nvidia's H100 GPU" - so on GA100/sm_80 the memory savings would require emulation. Its "double the batch size" result is directly relevant to the repo's large-global-batch DDP strategy, since batch size is the only lever the repo has for amortising communication.
- `2026-10-27` [[fp8-lm-training-fp8-large-language-models]] — FP8-LM: Training FP8 Large Language Models — FP8-LM (Peng et al., Microsoft Azure/Research; arXiv 2310.18313) is the primary FP8 training framework paper and the baseline GeoFP8/AGoQ build on. It pushes FP8 into weights, gradients, optimizer states and collective communication in three incremental levels, and reports on GPT-175B over Azure NDv5 H100 nodes: 39% reduction in real memory use and 75% faster than the BF16 Megatron-LM baseline, 37% faster than NVIDIA Transformer Engine, with 42% less memory than TE.

The communication result is the load-bearing one for this run: 8-bit gradients for all-reduce give a theoretical 75% reduction in communication cost versus the mainstream 32-bit scheme (BF16 mixed precision computes gradients in 16-bit but still all-reduces in 32-bit), and the observed practical reduction during GPT training is 63% to 65%. Memory reduction versus BF16 is 28% to 39% (29% for GPT-7B, 39% for GPT-175B).

The technical obstacle they solve is overflow/underflow in low-bit all-reduce: pre-scaling divides before summation (underflow), post-scaling sums first then divides (keeps values near the FP8 max). They add distributed and automated scaling with a single shared scalar per gradient tensor, computed as a global minimum across GPUs, so that standard NCCL all-reduce can still be used - NCCL itself "lacks the capability of performing all-reduce operation considering the additional tensor-wise scaling factors".

HARDWARE CAVEAT that decides feasibility on the CMP 170HX: FP8 here is Hopper-native. The paper frames FP8 as arriving "with the release of Nvidia H100 GPU", and the only prior usable framework (NVIDIA Transformer Engine) applies FP8 solely to GEMM. GA100/sm_80 has no FP8 tensor-core path, so the 63-65% communication saving is not directly available on a CMP 170HX without emulation. Training config: 100B tokens, batch size 4M tokens, sequence length 2048, Adam with beta1=0.9/beta2=0.95, weight decay 0.1.
- `2026-10-27` [[evaluating-modern-gpu-interconnect-pcie]] — Evaluating Modern GPU Interconnect: PCIe, — Li, Song, Chen, Li, Liu, Tallent & Barker (PNNL; arXiv 1903.04611, IEEE TPDS) is the canonical primary evaluation of modern GPU interconnects - PCIe, NVLink-V1, NVLink-V2, NV-SLI, NVSwitch and GPUDirect-RDMA - across six platforms: P100-DGX-1, V100-DGX-1, DGX-2, OLCF SummitDev, Summit, and a 2x Turing RTX-2080 SLI system. They measure raw startup latency, sustainable uni/bidirectional bandwidth, topology, routing and NUMA effects under both P2P and collective (NCCL) patterns.

Four new NUMA effects are identified: three triggered by NVLink topology/connectivity/routing, and one by a PCIe chipset design issue they label "anti-locality" - nearby access presenting LOWER performance than remote access, observed only for bandwidth (not latency) and appearing for messages >64 KB. PCIe latency is essentially uniform across pairs (same through one PCIe switch, across a local CPU socket, or across the QPI bridge), i.e. no NUMA effect in PCIe latency; NVLink latency is ~9 us direct and rises ~2x (P100) / ~3x (V100) when routing is required. NV-SLI: local 5 us, NV-SLI 8 us, PCIe 13 us. NVSwitch gives 50 GB/s bidirectional per link, 300 GB/s via a single NVSwitch hop, and 2.4 TB/s baseboard-to-baseboard bisection.

Message-size saturation points differ by an order of magnitude: NCCL collective bandwidth saturates at ~16 MB on PCIe but ~256 MB on NVLink (8 GPUs); on a 2-GPU SLI system both PCIe and NV-SLI saturate at ~1 MB.

THE finding for this run: "the NUMA effects appear quite significant with 5 GPUs, implying that there may be a congestion when forming a NCCL ring among 5 GPUs. One should avoid adopting 5 GPUs in their application setup." The box under assessment has exactly 5 GPUs, so this is a direct, primary-sourced warning that a 5-rank NCCL ring is a pathological configuration on PCIe.

Their discussion adds two structural cautions: NVLink's advantage is bandwidth, not latency, over PCIe; and a faster interconnect alone "can hardly become significant enough to lift the entire application's speedup" unless the CPU-centric master-slave programming model is replaced or communication control moves to the GPU (e.g. NVSHMEM). NVLink also introduces its own overheads (enable/disable peer access, routing, NCCL initialization).
- `2026-10-27` [[github-promisezackrglm53-flash-170hx-pp8-glm-53-flash-320b-moe-served-with-vllm]] — GitHub - promisezackr/glm53-flash-170hx-pp8: GLM-5.3-Flash (320B MoE) served with vLLM on 8x CMP 170HX (SM80, 64 GB, PCIe Gen2 x4): pipeline-parallel 8, NVFP4 weights, DFlash2 speculative decoding, 1M context. Patches, launch config and benchmarks. · GitHub — promisezackr/glm53-flash-170hx-pp8 (9 stars, 1 commit 90ec72e 2026-08-31, Apache-2.0 derivative of vLLM) — the community reference for serving a 320B MoE on the exact fabric the query names: 8x CMP 170HX (GA100, SM80, 64 GB unlocked), PCIe Gen2 x4 each, no P2P, 2x Xeon E5-2690 v4 host with 96 GB DDR4. Directly load-bearing for the topology question because it states the conclusion explicitly: 'TP is not an option on this box (no P2P); PP6 saves only ~6%.' So on a Gen2 x4 no-P2P box, tensor parallelism is ruled out by the fabric in practice, and pipeline parallelism is the topology that works — corroborating the repo's TP claim while refuting the stronger claim that DDP is the only viable topology. Production config: --pipeline-parallel-size 8 with VLLM_PP_LAYER_PARTITION=7,5,7,5,7,5,7,2 (partitioned around the 11 sparse-MLA layers at indices 3,7,...,43 so stages holding two of them get fewer layers and a larger KV budget), --max-model-len 1048576, --gpu-memory-utilization 0.90, --max-num-seqs 8, --max-num-batched-tokens 4096, no prefix caching, DFlash2 drafter with num_speculative_tokens 7, VLLM_PP_MAX_DECODE_REQS_PER_BATCH=2. Benchmarks (single stream, temp 0, including speculation): code 123 tok/s, json 120, counting 114-118, math 59, prose 37 under NVFP4 + DFlash2 k=7; FP8 eager no-spec code was only 6.2 tok/s, so the patch set is worth ~20x. Steady-state aggregate: 201-203 tok/s at 4 streams, 259-267 at 8, 276 at 16. Long context: a 192k-token prompt completes in 53 s and a 960k-token prompt in 208 s end-to-end with the needle retrieved, KV pool 6.62M tokens (6.3 concurrent 1M requests) at utilization 0.90 — i.e. roughly 4,600 tok/s aggregate prefill across 8 cards at 960k, which is the closest thing in the corpus to a measured 1M-token-batch prefill cost. Step-time model from per-stage CUDA events: ~32 ms fixed + 2.1 ms x verified tokens per step. Startup: ~5 min weight load from SATA SSD, ~15 min first warmup, ~12 min later boots. 24 patches on top of vllm/vllm-openai:glm53-flash (65 files, +4.8k/-0.2k), covering SM8x port, glm5next PP intermediate-tensor factories, MTP-under-PP weight loading, DFlash2 under PP8, decode micro-batch cap, int64 KV offsets, drafter sidecar KV re-blocking 16->256, and env-gated stage instrumentation.
- `2026-10-27` [[full-specifications-170th-street]] — Full Specifications | 170th Street — 170th Street gitbook 'Full Specifications' page (381 words, last updated ~2 months before fetch) — the primary spec sheet that the it-still-boots specifications page paraphrases, and the source of the corpus's lowest per-card TFLOPS figures. General: GA100-105F-A1 (8 GB) / GA100-105A-A1 (10 GB), Ampere, TSMC 7 nm N7, 54.2 billion transistors, 826 mm^2, released 2021-09-01 at ~$4,299 MSRP, dual-slot passive, no display outputs. Compute: 70 SM, 4,480 CUDA cores (64/SM), 280 third-generation tensor cores (4/SM), 1,140 MHz base / 1,410 MHz boost, 192 KB L1 per SM, 32,768 KB L2. Memory: HBM2e, 8 GB / 10 GB stock, 64 GB / 40 GB unlocked, 4,096-bit / 5,120-bit bus, 1,458 MHz / 1,215 MHz, 1.49 TB/s / 1.56 TB/s theoretical, ECC disabled, resizable BAR present but limited to 64 MiB. Performance table (Original vs Unlocked): FP64 0.19 -> 6.44 TFLOPS, FP32 0.41 -> 12.99 TFLOPS, FP16 49.05 -> 49.05 TFLOPS (unchanged), INT64 2.52 -> 2.52, INT32 12.99 -> 12.99, INT16 11.94 -> 11.94, INT8 1.64 -> 48.03. Connectivity: physical x16 slot, electrical Gen1 x4 firmware-locked, ~1 GB/s, NVLink connectors present but fuse-disabled, 1x 8-pin CPU power connector via adapter. Power: TDP default 250 W, ~300 W software max, ~30-40 W idle. API: CUDA CC 8.0, OpenCL 3.0, no DirectX/Vulkan/OpenGL/NVENC/NVDEC. Notes section confirms the 8 GB and 10 GB variants are genuine hardware binning variants with different memory buses, not firmware differences. CAVEAT: this is a spec-sheet table, not a measurement — its 49.05 TFLOPS FP16 figure conflicts with the club's measured 155.7 TFLOPS fp16 on a healthy card, so treat it as the weakest tier for compute claims.
