- `2026-10-27` [[github-amoghmunikotecmpunlocker-a-tool-to-unlobotomize-your-nvidia-card-github]] — GitHub - amoghmunikote/cmpunlocker: A tool to unlobotomize your NVIDIA card! · GitHub — amoghmunikote/cmpunlocker — the upstream CMP 170HX unlock tool (808 stars, 55 commits, latest 2d10f89 2026-10-08, release v0.5 2026-10-05), and the primary source for what the community unlock actually restores. README 'What Gets Unlocked' table lists as Working: full SM compute throughput (SS0/SS1), memory geometry (64 GB on 8 GB cards, 40 GB on 10 GB cards), PCIe Gen2 speeds, ECC DRAM and SRAM, full BAR1 size (64 GB), JTAG host-to-JTAG register access, VFIO-based passthrough, GPU profiling, and persistence across reboot via patched modules. Two points matter for this run. First, ECC is listed as Working here, which contradicts club-170hx's 'ECC untested / upstream work in progress' and the Consensus-Protocol wiki's frontier listing — so ECC availability is a live contradiction in the corpus, not a settled fact. Second, P2P is NOT in the upstream feature list at all: BAR1 P2P exists only in forks (bayley/cmpunlocker, admunch888/cmpunlocker), so 'no P2P' is correct for the upstream tool and wrong as a hardware statement. Requirements: Linux x86-64, root, nvidia-open 610.xx.xx+ already installed, matching kernel headers, Secure Boot disabled, network access on first install. Install is sudo ./install.sh with optional --profile=8gb or --profile=10gb to force the memory geometry, then a reboot. Uninstall is ./remove.sh --yes plus reboot. Python 3 is used at build time to select the 8 GB/10 GB geometry.
- `2026-10-27` [[2x-cmp-170hx-for-llm-inference-unlock-cross-root-p2p-serving-vllm-epycromed8-2t]] — 2x CMP 170HX for LLM inference: unlock, cross-root P2P, serving (vLLM, EPYC/ROMED8-2T) — Web Pulse — Aggregated reprint of Roman Puls's gist (published 2026-10-01, revised 2026-10-02) documenting 2x CMP 170HX on an EPYC 7443 / ASRock ROMED8-2T host. The load-bearing finding for this run is that P2P is NOT permanently fused off: by default cudaDeviceCanAccessPeer is 0 and the GSP capabilities report GPU_NOT_SUPPORTED, but three driver patches from bayley/cmpunlocker (0011-p2p-bar1, 0013-skip-mailbox-peer-preinit, 0015-bar1p2p-readcap-override) layered onto amoghmunikote/cmpunlocker plus the regkey ForceP2P=0x11 make it 1 in both directions, verified by cuMemcpyPeer and by NCCL reporting 'via P2P/CUMEM'. With P2P working, vLLM TP=2 prefill is about 1.8-1.9x faster than without, on Gen2. The author is careful that the regkey alone was never verified sufficient.

Other measured facts: after unlock each card reports 64 GB, 74 SM (cmpunlocker master 6c442ee; v0.4 gives 70 SM) and trains at PCIe Gen2 x16; the 250 W card can be cross-flashed to NVIDIA's 300 W VBIOS (92.00.6D.00.0A, memory 1728 MHz); thermal test shows 200 W plateaus at 77 C, 225 W at 83-84 C with no margin, and 250 W runs away at +6 C/min; a +200 MHz GPC VF offset is stable under real vLLM load (~1600-1650 MHz at 200 W) while +250 hung a card with Xid 175. Prefill table: no-P2P ~2500 tok/s flat across 8k-128k, P2P at 70 SM 4634 down to 4486, at 74 SM 4766 down to 4599, and with the +200 offset 5033 down to 4881.

Directly relevant to the Engram memory question: this deployment keeps the model's PLE / n-gram tables in host RAM (~128 GB) via VLLM_PLE_CPU_OFFLOAD=1, which is a working precedent for large lookup tables living outside VRAM.
- `2026-10-27` [[github-dl4rcecmp-170hx-inference-lab-cmp-170hx-llm-inference-lab-vllm-scripts-me]] — GitHub - dl4rce/cmp-170hx-inference-lab: CMP 170HX LLM inference lab: vLLM scripts, measured results, graphs. Not an A100. Unlock/mod caveats. · GitHub — Independent measured lab (2026-08-21) on unlocked 64 GB CMP 170HX cards, with the dual-card experiment that matters most for this run. Confirms P2P is fused off in the CMP SKU: nvidia-smi topo -p2p reports GNS ('GPU not supported'), torch.cuda.can_device_access_peer is False in both directions, device-to-device copies are host-staged at 5.85 GB/s versus 6.17 GB/s for H2D, and vLLM logs 'Custom allreduce is disabled because your platform lacks GPU P2P capability'. The author states explicitly this is not IOMMU or ACS, so nothing in BIOS or the kernel recovers it.

Dual-card numbers (same engine, checkpoint and flags; only the split changes): TP=2 raises single-stream decode 57.5 to 70.9 tok/s (+23%) but drops 16-agent aggregate from 608 to 415 tok/s (-32%) and 48K prefill from 27.1 to 47.4 s (-75%); PP=2 gives 605 tok/s at 16 agents, 895 at 32 agents and 14.8 s at 48K prefill (3.2x faster than TP=2); two independent TP=1 servers behind a load balancer reach 754 tok/s at 16 agents and 1,253 at 32, i.e. 3.0x the TP=2 figure on identical silicon. TP=2's crossover is around 3 agents, and PP=2 matches single-card throughput while holding 2.3x the KV pool.

Direct evidence on the repo's open question about link width: measured PCIe x16 versus x4 gives H2D 6.63 versus 1.7 GB/s, and decode and prefill did not move - so width barely matters for inference, though the repo's training communication estimates do depend on it. Unlock path recorded: cmpunlocker, 8 GB SKU to 64 GB, 10 GB SKU to ~40 GB stable (not 80); Gen2 speed is software while width is a solder mod (24x 0402 AC-coupling capacitors).
- `2026-10-27` [[deepseek-v4-cmp170hxsettingsmd-at-317dac12bbd7f401f0538c76161bc7186086fb65-allov]] — deepseek-v4-cmp170hx/SETTINGS.md at 317dac12bbd7f401f0538c76161bc7186086fb65 · allover326/deepseek-v4-cmp170hx · GitHub — The highest-value hardware-tuning document in this batch: the full annotated vLLM launch configuration for DeepSeek-V4-Flash-0731 on 4x CMP 170HX with --pipeline-parallel-size 4. States PP beats TP by 6.6x on prefill (5,321 vs 801 tok/s at 77k context) and roughly 2x on aggregate decode, with the mechanism spelled out: tensor parallel performs 2 all-reduces per layer x 43 layers = 86 collectives per forward, each moving tokens x hidden bytes, so doubling tokens doubles both compute and communication and a communication-bound setup stays communication-bound at every sequence length - which is why TP prefill measures flat at ~800 tok/s from 1.5k to 77k tokens. PP moves the same payload only 3 times (one hand-off per stage boundary) and overlaps it with compute: ~28x less data on the wire. Characterises the fabric as PCIe Gen2 x4 with no P2P, ~1.0 GB/s measured bus bandwidth, 'close to the worst case for TP'.

Also documents several constraints that bear on training feasibility: fp8 KV cache works correctly on sm_80 because the conversion is software (only fp8 math needs sm_89+); PYTORCH_CUDA_ALLOC_CONF=expandable_segments:True is a hard failure at model load on these cards (cannot map 20 MB with 28.6 GiB free), i.e. CUDA VMM appears broken on GA100 CMP parts; --enforce-eager costs ~12x (8-10 tok/s) so CUDA graphs are mandatory; TP3 is arithmetically impossible for this checkpoint (64 heads and 256 experts do not divide by 3); VLLM_PP_LAYER_PARTITION 12,12,12,7 removes an 8.7 GiB rank imbalance and grows the KV pool ~85%. Honest limits: TTFT at 1M context is 9.2 minutes with decode ~40 tok/s, and retrieval accuracy degrades with depth (~100% at 150k to ~30% at 900k), which the author calls architectural rather than a tuning problem.
- `2026-10-27` [[analysing-the-impact-of-sequence-composition]] — Analysing The Impact of Sequence Composition — Zhao et al., 'Analysing The Impact of Sequence Composition on Language Model Pre-Training' (arXiv 2402.13991; ACL 2024 long paper) — the primary source behind the ACL landing-page note. Setup: LLaMA-architecture 1.3B-parameter models pretrained on 150B tokens sampled from SlimPajama (7 sub-corpora, token proportions preserved), at context windows of 2,048 (2K) and 8,192 (8K). Four sequence-construction / masking variants: MIXChunk (baseline random concatenation), UNIChunk (same-source documents only), BM25Chunk (retrieval-based: for each document, BM25-select related documents from the corpus to fill the chunk), and INTRADoc (intra-document causal masking).

Results: BM25Chunk has the lowest PPL of the three causal-masking variants — -0.62 at 2K and -0.78 at 8K vs MIXChunk; UNIChunk also beats MIXChunk (-0.34 / -0.21). INTRADoc has the lowest PPL of all. GitHub is the most sensitive domain: INTRADoc improves over UNIChunk by -1.3 PPL at 2K and -2.0 at 8K, suggesting code pretraining is disproportionately harmed by unrelated preceding context. Downstream: BM25Chunk improves in-context learning +11.6%, knowledge memorisation +9.8%, context utilisation +7.2%.

Cost: intra-document causal masking costs 4.0% training-efficiency degradation in their implementation, implemented with FlashAttention-2; the authors state they do not fully solve this efficiency issue. This is the number to price against any packing / masking change on the CMP 170HX box: a 4% throughput tax buys double-digit downstream gains, a favourable trade at small scale.
- `2026-10-27` [[huggingfacetbsmollm-corpus-datasets-at-hugging-face]] — HuggingFaceTB/smollm-corpus · Datasets at Hugging Face — Dataset card for HuggingFaceTB/smollm-corpus, the released container for the SmolLM training data: three subsets — cosmopedia-v2 (39.1M rows of synthetic textbooks / blog posts / stories generated by Mixtral-8x7B-Instruct-v0.1), fineweb-edu-dedup (220B tokens of deduplicated FineWeb-Edu, 190M rows), and python-edu (7.68M Python files scoring >=4 on the educational code model, extracted from the-stack-v2-train with contents pulled from Software Heritage's S3 bucket for licence compliance). 236.98M rows / 673 GB total. Fields include token_length (Mistral-7B tokenizer, cosmopedia) and token_count (GPT-2 tokenizer, fineweb-edu-dedup).

Relevance: this is the practical substitute for the Stack-Edu dataset page (which failed to fetch) — it establishes the on-disk footprint and token counts of the exact corpora the repo's 9B-token mix would draw from, and confirms the FineWeb-Edu dedup variant is 220B tokens rather than the full 1.3T. Note that Stack-Edu itself is not a subset here; only python-edu (the Python slice) is, so Stack-Edu's ~125B-token figure has to come from the SmolLM2 paper.
- `2026-10-27` [[the-fineweb-datasets-decanting-the-web-for-the]] — The FineWeb Datasets: Decanting the Web for the — The FineWeb Datasets paper (Penedo et al., NeurIPS 2024 Datasets & Benchmarks; arXiv 2406.17557) is the primary source behind the repo's web-data assumptions. FineWeb is ~15T GPT-2 tokens of Common Crawl (36T tokens before filtering), built with datatrove; FineWeb-Edu is the 1.3T-token educational subset. The method is a chain of data ablation experiments: identical 1.71B-parameter Llama-architecture models, sequence length 2048, global batch ~2M tokens, GPT-2 tokenizer, trained with nanotron on ~28B tokens for filtering ablations (roughly Chinchilla-optimal for that size) and 350B tokens for dedup / cumulative ablations. Two models per data version with different subsets and seeds, averaged. Over 70 models, ~80,000 H100 GPU hours total.

Pipeline findings with numbers: trafilatura extraction from WARC beats WET text; fastText English filter at score >=0.65; MinHash fuzzy dedup with 112 hash functions, 8-hash buckets, 14x8 bands, union-find over connected components; heuristic quality filters tuned by taking the lower tail of each metric's histogram (16 candidate metric-threshold pairs). FineWeb-Edu: Llama3-70B-Instruct scored 500k CC-MAIN-2024-10 pages on a 0-5 additive scale; a BERT-like regression classifier (Snowflake-arctic-embed-m base) fine-tuned on those annotations reached F1 82% at threshold 3; applying it to the 15T-token FineWeb took 6,000 H100 GPU hours; threshold 3 removed 92% of the data leaving 1.3T tokens, threshold 2 leaves 5.4T.

Why it matters here: it fixes the shape of a defensible data-ablation experiment — 1.7B params, 2k context, ~2M-token global batch, 28B tokens — which is almost exactly the scale a 5x CMP 170HX box can reach, and it quantifies the accelerator cost of the curation step separately from the training step. It also documents that the ablation protocol (two seeds, equal token counts, lighteval benchmarks chosen for low variance) is what makes small-scale data comparisons trustworthy.
- `2026-10-27` [[club-170hxdocsclustermd-at-main-pixelmlclub-170hx-github]] — club-170hx/docs/CLUSTER.md at main · PixelML/club-170hx · GitHub — Short (524-word) community design doc for building a low-cost SM80 cluster out of CMP 170HX cards. Its parallelism table is a direct counterpoint to the repo's claim that expert and tensor parallelism are ruled out by the fabric: pipeline parallel is the 'preferred starting point' because it communicates stage boundaries rather than frequent full-tensor collectives; tensor parallel is 'use only after measurement' since all-reduce traffic can erase compute gains on slow PCIe; data parallel is 'good for independent jobs/throughput' but each replica needs its own weights or a suitable sharding design; expert parallel is 'workload-dependent' with routing traffic and runtime support that 'must be measured'. It also notes there is no NVLink in the tested configuration and that PCIe links may negotiate conservatively under virtualization, which changes which parallelism strategy wins. Rest of the doc is operational: storage layout, preflight inventory, scheduling rules and a rule that multi-node claims must publish link speed, transport, topology, batch size and failure behaviour.
- `2026-10-27` [[huggingfacetbfinemath-datasets-at-hugging-face-2]] — HuggingFaceTB/finemath · Datasets at Hugging Face — Dataset card for HuggingFaceTB/finemath: 34B tokens / 21.4M docs in FineMath-3+ and 9.6B tokens / 6.7M docs in FineMath-4+ (a subset of 3+), plus re-scored InfiMM-WebMath text-only portions (InfiMM-WebMath-3+ 20.5B tokens / 13.9M docs; 4+ 8.5B / 6.3M). Combined 3+ release is ~54B tokens. Curation: Llama-3.1-70B-Instruct annotations on a 5-point scale feeding a multilingual-e5-small-based classifier; the first classifier version underperformed OpenWebMath, so the pipeline was revised to (a) keep domains where >=10% of pages scored >=2 and (b) add OWM / InfiMM-WebMath URLs, then re-extract with the OWM pipeline to preserve Markdown/LaTeX, re-score, keep >=3, and dedupe with single-band MinHash-LSH. Decontaminated against GSM8k / MATH / MMLU / ARC with 13-gram overlap removal.

Reported ablations: FineMath-3+ beats base InfiWebMath on GSM8k and MATH; FineMath-4+ beats both 3+ and InfiWebMath-4+; a 50/50 FineMath-3+ / InfiWebMath-3+ mix (~50B tokens) matches FineMath-3+ alone; and deduplicating pages shared between FineMath and InfiWebMath reduces performance versus the non-deduplicated combination — a counter-intuitive result worth flagging against any assumption that dedup is monotonically good. Fields include token_count (Llama tokens), score (float), int_score, language_score. License ODC-By v1.0 + CommonCrawl ToU. 48.3M rows / 149 GB on disk.
- `2026-10-27` [[huggingfacetbfinemath-datasets-at-hugging-face]] — HuggingFaceTB/finemath · Datasets at Hugging Face — Auto-converted to Parquet API Embed  Duplicate Data Studio
- `2026-10-27` [[huggingfacefwfineweb-edu-datasets-at-hugging-face]] — HuggingFaceFW/fineweb-edu · Datasets at Hugging Face — Dataset card for HuggingFaceFW/fineweb-edu: 1.3T GPT-2 tokens of educational web text filtered from the 15T-token FineWeb corpus, plus a sibling FineWeb-Edu-score-2 at 5.4T tokens (threshold 2 instead of 3). Filtering: a Snowflake-arctic-embed-based regression classifier fine-tuned on 500k Llama3-70B-Instruct educational-quality annotations (0-5 additive scale); threshold 3 removed 92% of FineWeb; classifier F1 82% at threshold 3. Classifying the full 15T tokens took 6,000 H100 GPU hours — an anchor for how much accelerator time data curation alone consumes.

Practical shape for a small run: 114 configs (per-CC-MAIN dump plus sample-10BT 9.67M rows, sample-100BT 97.3M rows, sample-350BT 339M rows, nested 10BT within 100BT within 350BT); default has 1.53B rows in the viewer, 5.84 TB on disk. Fields: text, id, dump, url, date, file_path, language, language_score, token_count (35-159k), score (2.52-5.06), int_score. License ODC-By v1.0 plus CommonCrawl ToU. The deduplicated version (220B tokens) ships as the fineweb-edu-dedup subset of SmolLM-Corpus, and the authors report dedup had no measurable effect on a 1.8B model trained on 350B tokens.

Relevance to the repo's 9B-token data pipeline: sample-10BT alone is roughly the right order of magnitude for a 9B-token mix, and the token_count field makes exact token budgeting possible without a tokenizer pass.
- `2026-10-27` [[the-fineweb-datasets-decanting-the-web-for-the-finest-text-data-at-scale-proceed]] — The FineWeb Datasets: Decanting the Web for the Finest Text Data at Scale - proceedings.com — proceedings.com item record for the NeurIPS 37 (2024) proceedings version of 'The FineWeb Datasets: Decanting the Web for the Finest Text Data at Scale' — Penedo, Kydlicek, Allal, Lozhkov, Mitchell, Raffel, von Werra, Wolf; pages 30811-30849; NeurIPS 37 held 10-15 December 2024, Vancouver. Metadata only (169 words): no abstract, no numbers. The substantive content is in the arXiv primary (2406.17557) fetched separately; this note is useful only as the citable NeurIPS-2024 venue record for the FineWeb / FineWeb-Edu paper.
- `2026-10-27` [[analysing-the-impact-of-sequence-composition-on-language-model-pre-training-acl]] — Analysing The Impact of Sequence Composition on Language Model Pre-Training - ACL Anthology — ACL Anthology landing page for Zhao et al., 'Analysing The Impact of Sequence Composition on Language Model Pre-Training' (ACL 2024 Long Papers, pp. 7897-7912, DOI 10.18653/v1/2024.acl-long.427). Abstract only — full text is in the arXiv primary (2402.13991) fetched separately. Core claims: standard fixed-length concatenation with causal masking lets tokens attend to previous documents, injecting distracting context; intra-document causal masking conditions each token only on prior tokens of the same document and significantly improves LM and downstream performance; and the retrieval-based packing method BM25Chunk improves in-context learning (+11.6%), knowledge memorisation (+9.8%) and context utilisation (+7.2%) without sacrificing efficiency. Directly relevant to the repo's data-pipeline packing/determinism claims: packing strategy is a first-order quality variable, not a neutral implementation detail.
- `2026-10-27` [[cramming-training-a-language-model-on-a]] — CRAMMING: TRAINING A LANGUAGE MODEL ON A — Cramming (Geiping & Goldstein, arXiv 2212.14034) is the canonical single-GPU compute-budget study: train a transformer LM from scratch with masked LM on one GPU for 24 hours and measure GLUE. Hardware tested: RTX 2080 Ti (11 GB), RTX A4000 (16 GB), RTX A6000 (48 GB), each paired with 4 CPU cores and 32 GB RAM; the A6000 run reaches BERT-base-class GLUE in one day.

Findings that bear on CMP 170HX feasibility: (1) scaling laws hold in the extreme low-compute regime — per-token training efficiency depends strongly on model size but not on transformer type, so architecture changes only help insofar as they raise throughput at constant parameter count; (2) the authors explicitly find no benefit from CPU/NVMe offloading in this setting; (3) FlashAttention (Dao et al. 2022) is only re-enabled for the final architecture variant after ablations, i.e. it is a throughput lever, not an accuracy lever; (4) the ablation accounting attributes ~2 GLUE points to architecture changes, ~1 point to data changes, and ~0.5 point to training-recipe changes; (5) sequence length 128 with packed sequences gives micro-batches of 64-96 on the 2080 Ti, accumulated into larger batches, and single-epoch training is feasible at this budget.

Relevance: it is the strongest published evidence that a single GPU with no interconnect at all can produce a usable model in a bounded wall-clock budget — the limiting case of the CMP 170HX box (5 cards, no NVLink, no P2P). It also warns that offloading, a plausible workaround for a 64 GB-per-card memory ceiling, bought nothing in their measurements.
- `2026-10-27` [[qwen3-technical-report]] — Qwen3 Technical Report — Qwen3 Technical Report (arXiv 2505.09388, May 2025) documents 6 dense models (0.6B, 1.7B, 4B, 8B, 14B, 32B) and 2 MoE models (30B-A3B, 235B-A22B) trained on 36T tokens. Architecture: GQA, SwiGLU, RoPE, RMSNorm pre-norm, QKV-bias removed, QK-Norm added for training stability; MoE models use fine-grained expert segmentation with 128 total experts / 8 activated per token, no shared experts, plus a global-batch load-balancing loss. Tokenizer is BBPE with a 151,669 vocabulary. Dense context lengths: 0.6B/1.7B = 32K, 4B-32B = 128K; both MoE models = 128K.

Three-stage pretraining: S1 general on >30T tokens at sequence length 4,096; S2 reasoning on ~5T higher-quality tokens, still 4,096; S3 long-context on 'hundreds of billions' of tokens at sequence length 32,768, with a corpus of 75% documents 16,384-32,768 tokens and 25% at 4,096-16,384. RoPE base frequency raised 10,000 -> 1,000,000 via ABF, plus YARN and Dual Chunk Attention for a 4x inference-time extension.

Load-bearing for the CMP 170HX question: 32k is reached by a dedicated long-context stage on hundreds of billions of tokens, not by training at 32k from the start — so a 32k CSA2 experiment on this box is a stage, not a full run. The report also states that with the same pretraining data Qwen3 MoE base models achieve performance similar to Qwen3 dense models, and that MoE models outperform Qwen2.5-MoE with less than 1/2 the activated parameters — the standard MoE-efficiency claim the repo's expert-parallelism analysis has to price against a PCIe Gen2 x4 fabric. No training-infrastructure section (no GPU counts, no parallelism configuration, no throughput numbers) is included, so it cannot corroborate any communication-cost claim.
- `2026-10-27` [[smollm2-when-smol-goes-big]] — SmolLM2: When Smol Goes Big — — SmolLM2 (Hugging Face, arXiv 2502.02737) is the reference recipe for training a ~1.7B model from scratch on a fixed compute budget, and the primary source for the FineMath / Stack-Edu / SmolTalk datasets. Base model: 1.7B params, Llama-2 architecture, 49,152-token tokenizer, ~11T tokens over four stages on 256 H100s with the nanotron framework; total cost stated as ~1e23 FLOPs, i.e. ~$250,000 of GPU compute — cited as the reason the authors rebalance mixtures online instead of running multiple from-scratch runs. Stage 1 (0-6T tokens) is 60% FineWeb-Edu / 40% DCLM plus 10% StarCoder-Data; later stages add OWM/InfiMM-WebMath/FineMath and Stack-Edu at 14-24% code and 4-10% math. Ablation models are 1.7B params, sequence length 2048, global batch ~2M tokens, LR 3e-4 cosine, 350B tokens per ablation.

FineMath: up to 54B tokens of math from Common Crawl, built by re-extracting 7.7B URLs (5.7B classifier-identified + 0.6B OWM + 1.3B InfiMM-WebMath) with the OWM pipeline to preserve LaTeX, then scoring with a Llama-3.1-70B-Instruct-annotated classifier on a 5-point scale. FineMath4+ = 10B tokens / 6.7M docs; FineMath3+ = 34B tokens / 21.4M docs. Reported: FineMath4+ gives a 2x GSM8K and 6x MATH improvement over InfiMM-WebMath; Infi-WebMath4+ plateaus after ~80B tokens (~10 epochs) from repetition, a plateau not seen in FineMath4+.

Stack-Edu: filtered variant of StarCoder2Data restricted to the 15 largest languages (~450B-token subset); 15 language-specific StarEncoder classifiers trained on Llama3-70B-Instruct synthetic annotations (0-5 educational scale, 500k samples each, F1 > 0.7 at threshold 3). Result: ~125B tokens across 15 languages; Python 50.6B -> 21.8B with HumanEval 20.7 -> 25.6, C++ 69.7B -> 16.0B with MultiPL-E 16.7 -> 24.8.

Context extension: 2k -> 8k by continuing from a stage-4 checkpoint with RoPE base 130k and a mixture of 40% long documents (>=8k tokens) from DCLM (10%), FineWeb-Edu (10%) and Dolma books (20%). Relevance to the CMP 170HX run: it establishes that a 1.7B-class model at 2k context with a ~2M-token global batch is a normal, published ablation configuration — the scale this box can plausibly reach — and gives concrete token counts for the three datasets in the repo's data pipeline.
- `2026-10-27` [[allover326deepseek-v4-cmp170hx-deepwiki]] — allover326/deepseek-v4-cmp170hx | DeepWiki — Auto-generated DeepWiki index of allover326/deepseek-v4-cmp170hx, last indexed 2026-08-10 at commit 317dac. Confirms the deployment shape: 4x CMP 170HX with VRAM unlocked to 64 GB per card, PCIe Gen2 x4, 1,047,736-token verified context ceiling, PP4+DSpark decode 98.1 tok/s versus 50.8 tok/s for plain PP4, prefill 5,272 tok/s at 77k context, decode at 100k context 90.0 versus 38.8 tok/s. Names PWRBRK# (pin B30) as a ~4x performance trap on certain motherboards. Thin (661 words) and derivative - it is a navigation index over the underlying repo, so cite the repo's README/RESULTS/SETTINGS files rather than this page.
- `2026-10-27` [[all-gbs-without-flops-nvidia-cmp-170hx-review-performance-lockdown-workaround-te]] — All GB/s without FLOPS - Nvidia CMP 170HX Review, Performance Lockdown Workaround, Teardown, Watercooling, and Repair — Independent teardown + benchmark review of the CMP 170HX (GA100-105F-A1 on a PCB that is near-identical to the A100 40 GB PCIe card). Measured: 1355 GB/s HBM2e (clpeak float8) and 1350 GB/s fp16, i.e. the full memory bus is retained; FP32 FMA throttled to 394 GFLOPS (1/32 of the 12.6 TFLOPS theoretical); FP16 ~42 TFLOPS via mixbench and unaffected by FMA; non-FMA FP32 6250 GFLOPS; FP64 182 GFLOPS with FMA and 95 without; INT32 12.5 TIOPS uncapped. Tensor Cores do not engage usefully through standard FP32 paths (gpu_burn with CUBLAS_TENSOR_OP_MATH reports 6.2 TFLOPS, same as non-FMA FP32).

PCIe is locked down in two independent layers: an on-chip fuse pinning the device to Gen1, plus a board-level x4 limit created by omitting the AC-coupling capacitors on the extra lanes. lspci shows LnkCap 2.5GT/s x16 but LnkSta 2.5GT/s x4 (downgraded); nvidia-smi reports Max Gen2 / Current Gen1 / Device Max Gen1 / Host Max Gen3, so Gen2 is a device-side fuse limit while the host side already supports Gen3. Measured host-device transfer is 0.85 GB/s. No NVLink (the gold fingers exist but all components are unpopulated), Resizable BAR unavailable (largest BAR 64 MiB), stock VRAM 8 GiB.

Power behaviour is workload-class dependent and misleading: FMA-locked FP32/FP64 workloads draw only 60-75 W, while integer-heavy (hashcat) or memory-streaming workloads reach 160-180 W against a 250 W TDP. VBIOS modding is ruled out by digital signature checks. Caveat for this run: the review predates the cmpunlocker VRAM/link unlock, so its 8 GiB and Gen1 findings describe the stock card, not the repo's 64 GB / Gen2 box.
- `2026-10-27` [[quantifying-interconnect-energy-efficiency-on-perlmutter-pjbit-measurements-of-n]] — Quantifying Interconnect Energy Efficiency on Perlmutter: pJ/bit Measurements of NVLink, PCIe, Slingshot NICs, and Rosetta Switches — Zhao, Williams, Antepara, Oliker, Austin & Wright (LBNL, eScholarship qt67z0d2wn, 2026-04-28) measure dynamic energy per bit for the interconnects on Perlmutter (HPE Cray EX, A100 GPUs, NERSC) with a software-centric microbenchmark methodology that correlates controlled stress on one component with job- and rack-level power telemetry, separating dynamic energy from constant-power overhead.

Headline pJ/bit figures: NVLink 70.9 +/- 1.0 pJ/bit, PCIe (host<->GPU) 167.9 +/- 10.5 pJ/bit, NIC+PCIe (host<->Cassini NIC) 4.1 +/- 3.2 pJ/bit, and the GPU DMA engine ~48 pJ/bit at 93.55 GB/s. Moving a bit over PCIe into/out of the GPU costs ~2.4x what it costs over NVLink - the quantitative version of "PCIe is the expensive path".

The mechanism they identify is the "Active Tax": waking an A100's command processor, memory fabric and PCIe PHY from deep idle to P0 costs roughly 50 W of uncore power. Their H2D run shows GPU0 drawing 55.00 W at 24.94 GB/s (of a 32 GB/s PCIe Gen4 peak), and D2H 55.50 W at 24.53 GB/s - the transfer is dominated by the wake-up cost, not the payload. The host->NIC path bypasses the GPU entirely and therefore avoids the tax, which is why NIC+PCIe comes out at 4.1 pJ/bit.

Relevance to the CMP box: every collective on a no-P2P fabric stages through host RAM, so each GPU pays the PCIe path energy (167.9 pJ/bit) AND the ~50 W uncore wake-up tax on every transfer. On a card capped at 250 W, a 50 W uncore tax is 20% of the power budget spent moving bits rather than doing math - a real efficiency argument for large-batch, low-frequency communication (DDP with big global batches and gradient accumulation) and against per-layer all-to-alls. Caveat: measured on A100/PCIe Gen4 with full telemetry; the CMP 170HX has weaker telemetry and a Gen2 x4 link, so absolute pJ/bit will differ, but the direction (PCIe >> NVLink, plus a fixed wake-up tax) is architectural.
- `2026-10-27` [[exploration-of-cryptocurrency-mining-specific-gpus-in-ai]] — Exploration of Cryptocurrency Mining-Specific GPUs in AI — Single-author arXiv preprint (Xing Kangwei, May 2025) that benchmarks a stock CMP 170HX 8 GB over an Oculink x4 link on a Ryzen 7 7840HS mini-PC (driver 570.86.16, CUDA 12.8). Confirms the community FMA workaround: FP32 rises from 0.39 TFLOPS to ~6.2 TFLOPS (15x) when compiled with -fmad=false, while FP16 is unaffected by FMA and reaches ~42 TFLOPS in OpenCL/mixbench. Critically, PyTorch and gpu-burn report only ~6.3 TFLOPS FP16, which the author attributes to the frameworks failing to exploit the card, and Tensor Cores are judged unusable in this configuration: llama.cpp prefill reaches only 14-45% of the A100-scaled theoretical estimate while decode reaches 39-78% (decode is bandwidth-bound and therefore closer to expectation).

Specs recorded: GA100-105F-A1, 70 SM, 4480 CUDA cores, 280 Tensor Cores, 250 W TDP, 8 GB HBM2e at 1493 GB/s, theoretical FP16 50.53 / FP32 12.63 / FP64 6.317 TFLOPS, INT32 12.5 TIOPS uncapped. PCIe is natively 1.1 x4; the author states populating the missing coupling capacitors would give 1.1 x16 but did not validate it. Energy efficiency matches the A100 only where the workload is memory-bandwidth-bound.

Contradiction worth flagging for this run: this paper's ~42 TFLOPS FP16 ceiling and its finding that Tensor Cores do not work conflict with the repo's measured 162 TFLOPS bf16/GPU. The gap is almost certainly the cmpunlocker unlock and patched driver, which this study does not use - so the paper bounds the *stock* card, not the repo's box.
- `2026-10-27` [[overview-cmp-170hx-documentation]] — Overview - CMP 170HX Documentation — it-still-boots.github.io CMP 170HX Documentation — Performance Overview page (1,515 words): a 1,603-point decode sweep across 16 models at 1k-128k prompt depth on ONE unlocked CMP 170HX (64 GiB, ~1.49 TB/s, PCIe Gen2 x4, 150-300 W) in vLLM, llama.cpp and SGLang. Inference-only, but it supplies the two numbers this run most needs about the exact fabric in the query. First, the link: 'PCIe trained at Gen1 x4 (0.84 GB/s) for runs before 2026-09-10, Gen2 x4 (1.69 GB/s) after a reseat' — so Gen2 x4 measures 1.69 GB/s per card per direction, which corroborates bayley/cmpunlocker's 1.68 GB/s BAR1-P2P figure and pins the query's 'Gen2 x4' premise to a real measured box. A matched SGLang ladder on the new link reproduced every depth within 0.3%, i.e. decode is essentially link-insensitive at single-card scale. Second, context-depth scaling, which is the empirical baseline CSA2 has to beat at 32k: median decode retained vs the 1k rate is 99.0% at 4k, 96.2% at 16k, 93.5% at 32k, 88.1% at 64k, 79.4% at 128k (75 measurements per depth to 64k, 56 at 128k). Dense attention therefore costs only ~6.5% of decode throughput at 32k on this card, so a sparse-attention scheme must beat a ~6.5% penalty plus its own indexer overhead to pay off at 32k. Power-limit ladder (210 matched measurements): 250 W keeps 99.3% of the 300 W rate, 200 W 95.7%, 150 W 85.2%. Speculative decoding by depth: DFlash 2.22x at 1k falling to 1.22x at 128k, MTP 1.88x to 0.76x (drafter costs more than it returns at 128k), DSpark 2.06x to 1.40x. Concurrency: Nemotron-3.5-Lightning-30B-A3B W4A16 at 32k reaches 1,306 tok/s aggregate at 16 streams (237 at n=1) with TTFT 3.3 s -> 31.0 s; Ornith-1.5-35B W4A16 on SGLang peaks at 270 tok/s at n=2 then falls as queues grow. Quality (Qwen3.8-27B): W4A16 vs bf16 loses 1.3 points HumanEval pass@1 (0.927 vs 0.939) and 8.7 points GSM8K strict (0.444 vs 0.532).
- `2026-10-27` [[specifications-cmp-170hx-documentation]] — Specifications - CMP 170HX Documentation — it-still-boots.github.io CMP 170HX Documentation — Hardware Specifications page (851 words), a single-page reference paraphrasing the 170th-Street gitbook 'Full Specifications' fact sheet. Compute: 70 SM / 4,480 CUDA / 280 tensor cores, base 1,140 MHz, boost 1,410 MHz, L1 192 KB/SM, L2 32 MB, 54.2 billion transistors, 826 mm^2, TSMC N7. Memory per SKU: 8 GB (0x20C2) unlocks to 64 GB on a 4096-bit bus at ~1.49 TB/s; 10 GB (0x2082) unlocks to 40 GB on a 5120-bit bus at ~1.56 TB/s; ECC disabled; resizable BAR limited to 64 MiB stock. Performance table is the load-bearing part for the query's '~810 TFLOPS bf16' premise and it disagrees with the repo: FP32 ~0.41 TFLOPS stock -> ~12.99 TFLOPS unlocked, FP64 ~0.19 -> ~6.44, FP16 ~49.05 TFLOPS in BOTH stock and unlocked columns, INT8 ~1.64 -> ~48.03. So this source puts per-card FP16/BF16 tensor throughput at ~49 TFLOPS (245 TFLOPS for five cards), roughly a quarter of the repo's claimed 162 TFLOPS/GPU, and it does not model any compute unlock for FP16 at all. Connectivity: mechanical x16, stock electrical Gen1 x4 (~1 GB/s), Gen1 x16 (~4 GB/s) after the capacitor mod, Gen2 after the software unlock with width still set by the capacitors, Gen3 work-in-progress; NVLink connectors present but fuse-disabled. Stock lockdown is two independent layers — firmware Gen lock plus missing AC coupling capacitors on 12 of 16 lanes — and neither mod replaces the other. Power 250 W default, ~300 W software max, 30-40 W idle. No NVENC/NVDEC, no display outputs, CUDA CC 8.0, OpenCL 3.0. Waterblock family Bykski N-TESLA-A100-X-V2.
- `2026-10-27` [[github-consensus-protocolcmp170hx-cmp-170hx-technical-wiki-silicon-firmware-the]] — GitHub - Consensus-Protocol/cmp170hx: CMP 170HX technical wiki: silicon, firmware, the community unlock, procedures and open problems. 55 pages. · GitHub — Consensus-Protocol/cmp170hx (74 stars, 5 commits, current as of 2026-07-31) — README of a 55-page, ~278,000-word CMP 170HX technical wiki; a SOURCE HUB rather than a citable source, whose docs/ tree is the source of truth (sections: start, hardware, unlock, procedures, operations, frontier, history, appendix). Built from two public Discord servers (26,123 messages, 2026-06-22 to 2026-07-28), 1,121 archived attachments, 17 branches of the unlock tool, adjudicated against shipping source code and checked by four independent audit methods; no individuals named, confidence marked throughout. Two claims the wiki insists on, both directly relevant to the query's premises: (1) capacity is per SKU and not interchangeable — the 8 GB card (10de:20c2) unlocks to 64 GB, the 10 GB card (10de:2082) unlocks to 40 GB, and an 80 GB configuration for 10 GB cards was built, tested and rejected as unstable; (2) PCIe link speed and link width are separate problems — Gen1 to Gen2 is a software unlock shipped in cmpunlocker master since 2026-07-29 so any unlocked card runs Gen2, but going beyond x4 width requires hand-soldering 24 AC coupling capacitors, and neither achieves the other. That is the cleanest statement in the corpus of why 'Gen2 x4' is the default unlocked state and why width is a hardware ceiling, not a firmware one. The frontier/ section holds the unsolved problems (PCIe Gen3/Gen4, NVLink, ECC, 80 GB, P2P) — the highest-value pages for this run are docs/hardware/pcie.md, docs/frontier/*, docs/operations/performance.md and docs/appendix/methodology.md, none of which were fetched in this batch.
- `2026-10-27` [[multi-gpu-gemm-algorithm]] — Multi-GPU GEMM Algorithm — Choi & Stegailov (MMST 2022, CCIS 1750, pp. 281-292) benchmark their own multi-GPU SGEMM algorithm on two nodes: 8x A100 80GB over NVLink 3.0 through NVSwitch (cHARISMa), and 4x AMD Radeon RX 6900 XT 16GB over PCIe 4.0 under ROCm 5.2.1. Table 1 gives the parameters that matter: A100 peak FP32 19.5 TFLOPS (real 18.4), FP32 tensor-core peak 156 TFLOPS (real 124), peak GPU-GPU 300 GB/s (real 281); RX 6900 XT peak FP32 22.5 TFLOPS (real 21.4), peak GPU-GPU 32 GB/s (real 25.5).

On the NVLink node the algorithm reaches >80% of peak for 3-8 GPUs at tile size 1024 with N=90000, with strong scaling close to ideal, and beats cuBLAS-XT. Turning tensor cores on raises absolute TFLOPS but drops efficiency below 40% at 8 GPUs. On the PCIe 4.0 node the ported HIP version is much worse: efficiency below 40% for 3 and 4 GPUs, and a single-GPU SGEMM launch only reaches 8 TFLOPS of the 22.5 peak, with "considerable delays between data transfers". The authors conclude the GPU "is a consumer GPU, not a server grade GPU" and struggles "to perform computations and data transfers simultaneously", so "the multi-GPU performance of the algorithm considered can not be transferred easily to the consumer-grade GPU systems."

This is the closest published analogue to a PCIe-attached consumer/mining-class multi-GPU node, and its verdict is a warning: PCIe-attached consumer GPUs lose ~60% of efficiency in multi-GPU GEMM because compute/transfer overlap fails. Note the scale gap - 32 GB/s PCIe 4.0 here versus ~1 GB/s Gen2 x4 on the CMP box - so the effect should be at least as severe, not less.
- `2026-10-27` [[efficient-multi-path-nvlinkpcie-aware-ucx-based-collective-communication-for-dee]] — Efficient Multi-Path NVLink/PCIe-Aware UCX based Collective Communication for Deep Learning — Temucin, Sojoodi, Alizadeh & Afsahi (HOTI 2021, Queen's University) show that a single NVLink/PCIe path badly under-utilises intra-node GPU-GPU bandwidth, and fix it with a multi-path copy that stripes messages across multiple NVLink channels, memory regions and GPU streams. On UCX this yields 1.69x higher bandwidth (72 -> ~122 GB/s) and 1.84x on Open MPI + UCX (up to 134 GB/s), on a 4xV100 POWER9 node with three intra-socket NVLinks.

They then build a 3-stage hierarchical, pipelined MPI_Allreduce: multi-path NVLink for intra-socket stages 1 and 3, and PCIe / X-Bus channels for the inter-socket stage 2, chosen per message size to minimise interference (PCIe Gen4 and X-Bus are both ~32 GB/s, so the X-Bus is the bottleneck on the NVLink-X-Bus-NVLink path). For 512 KB-1 GB messages the hierarchical allreduce beats Spectrum MPI, Open MPI+UCX, Open MPI+HPC-X, MVAPICH2-GDR and NCCL by up to 12.25x, 15.63x, 3.72x, 1.48x and 1.38x respectively; at 64 MB specifically the speedups are 11.47x, 14.33x, 1.09x, 1.26x and 1.25x. It outperforms NCCL for all messages >8 MB.

At the application layer with Horovod+TensorFlow: VGG16 gets up to 2.98x/3.42x/3.22x/1.23x/3.24x over those five libraries, ResNet50 1.50x/1.57x/1.22x/1.24x/1.23x.

Why it matters here: it establishes that NCCL is not a hard ceiling on a PCIe/NVLink intra-node fabric - a hierarchical collective that uses ALL available paths (including the host X-bus staging path a no-P2P box is forced onto anyway) can beat it by ~1.25x at >=8 MB. The mechanism is dated (2021, V100, NCCL 2.5.6) but the structural insight - one path at a time wastes bandwidth - is exactly the situation on a 5-GPU box with no P2P, where every collective round-trips through host RAM.
- `2026-10-27` [[github-bayleycmpunlocker-a-cmpunlocker-fork-with-working-p2p-on-sys-4028gr-trt2]] — GitHub - bayley/cmpunlocker: A cmpunlocker fork with working p2p on SYS-4028GR-TRT2 · GitHub — bayley/cmpunlocker (56 commits, latest 5a7bb4b 2026-08-08, 26 stars) — a fork of the CMP 170HX unlock tool that gets GPU-to-GPU P2P working, and the single most load-bearing source for the query's 'no P2P' premise. Directly falsifies the repo's claim that P2P is impossible: P2P works over BAR1 P2P (not the mailbox), measured 1.68 GB/s each way on PCIe Gen2 x4 with a full 4-GPU mesh (12/12 directed pairs), verified with real transfers rather than cudaDeviceCanAccessPeer(). The mailbox path (P2P_CONNECTIVITY_PCIE_PROPRIETARY) is a documented dead end — driver configures it correctly, both GSP RPCs return NV_OK, transfers move exactly zero bytes with no error and no Xid. BAR1 P2P sidesteps it by rewriting peer PTEs from GMMU_APERTURE_PEER to SYS_COH/SYS_NONCOH pointing at the peer's BAR1 bus address. Requires together: patches 0011 (BAR1 P2P HALs forced on + peer-PTE rewrite), 0013 (skip mailbox peer pre-registration), 0015 (restore BAR1-P2P read cap), kernel-patches for 64 GB BAR1 from a normal boot, and RMForceStaticBar1=1 / RMPcieP2PType=1 in modprobe.d plus update-initramfs. Scaling to eight GPUs across two PLX switches on one Xeon E5 v4 root complex: all 56 directed pairs pass the alias-proof test including all 32 cross-switch pairs, no driver change needed — even though NVIDIA's own _p2pCapsGetHostSystemStatusOverPcieBar1 grants the read cap only for bCommonPciSwitchFound || RYZEN || XEON_SPR, so the gate is conservative rather than descriptive. Steady state: same-switch 18 pairs all 1.68 GB/s; cross-switch 24 pairs 1.07-1.45 GB/s (~78% of same-switch, asymmetric A->B 1.45 vs B->A 1.07-1.31, a root-complex property). ACS redirection must be disabled on every port of both switches — leaving switch A with ReqRedir+/CmpltRedir+ dropped same-switch from 1.68 to 1.20 GB/s (correct but slow, fails silently). Eight 64 GB BAR1s at 128 GB stride span 512 GB across two root ports, needing pci=hpmmioprefsize=2T plus kernel patch 0001. CRITICAL for the query's link-width premise: 'All CMP 170HX report x4 (downgraded) from an x16 capability; that x4 is normal, x2 is not' — one card trained at x2 and ran exactly half speed everywhere (0.84 GB/s same-switch, ~0.58 cross) with zero AER errors, i.e. physical (riser/seating) not signal integrity. Also documents two ways to hang the machine (ForceP2P=0x11 registry dword; live module reload) and an anti-rollback kernel-update rebuild system with pinned NVIDIA packages. Unlock scope: full SM compute, 64 GB on 8 GB cards / 40 GB on 10 GB cards, PCIe Gen2, BAR1 64 MB->64 GB, HBM2e NDIV overclock (N x 27 MHz; NDIV 70 = 1890 MHz works on ~60% of 8 GB cards, 73 usually only on lucky ones).
- `2026-10-27` [[club-170hxdocsbenchmarksmd-at-main-pixelmlclub-170hx-github]] — club-170hx/docs/BENCHMARKS.md at main · PixelML/club-170hx · GitHub — PixelML/club-170hx BENCHMARKS.md (615 lines, 48.8 KB, 7,601 words — LONG, candidate for source-analyst): the club's normalised inference-benchmark index with per-row evidence tiers ('publication-safe' only when the full sanitized receipt chain is merged). - `2026-10-27` [[club-170hxdocscmp170hx-understandingmd-at-main-pixelmlclub-170hx-github]] — club-170hx/docs/CMP170HX-UNDERSTANDING.md at main · PixelML/club-170hx · GitHub — PixelML/club-170hx CMP170HX-UNDERSTANDING.md (172 lines, 19.7 KB, 2026-10-02) — the club's consolidated, supersedes-earlier-pages reference. Compute unlock measured: tensor-core gate 74.6-78.5 TFLOP/s per card. Interconnect table (all measured on the 4-card PLX host, Gen2 x16): no peer access with the plain unlock, copies stage through host memory at 6.21-6.30 GB/s at 256 MiB and 22 us at 4 KiB, same-switch equals cross-switch; static-BAR1 P2P with 4 extra patches gives 12/12 pairs byte-exact at 5.79 GB/s with 16/194 us latency at 4 KiB/1 MiB vs 22/343 us staged; P2P helps two-card TP (Qwen3.8-27B TP2 decode +11%, +14% with MTP k=3) but hurts four-card TP behind PLX on Broadwell (GLM-5.3-Flash TP4 decode -20 to -26% at 1 user, -38 to -48% at 8 users); pipeline-parallel decode is HBM-bound not link-bound (MiMo PP3 74.6 vs 75.5 tok/s at Gen2 vs Gen1 x16). Power table: 140 W default gives 412.7 tok/s 1-user / 814.2 8-user at 537 W for four cards; 165 W adds only 0.1-1.1% over 150 W, so decode waits on memory and four-way synchronisation, not core clock. Driver stack: cmpunlocker @ 88e39ce + 6c442ee + PR #60 HBM-control + rebar-serialize + four BAR1-P2P patches, 17 patches applied cleanly on 615.71.09. §11 explicitly supersedes the older 'Gen1 is the card's maximum' and 'no P2P over PCIe' statements.
- `2026-10-27` [[club-170hxdocstopology-and-parallelismmd-at-main-pixelmlclub-170hx-github-2]] — club-170hx/docs/TOPOLOGY-AND-PARALLELISM.md at main · PixelML/club-170hx · GitHub — PixelML/club-170hx TOPOLOGY-AND-PARALLELISM.md (307 lines, 18.4 KB, commit 9e271ff 2026-10-04): the card/link-level argument that pipeline parallelism beats tensor parallelism on this fabric. Core mechanism: TP does one all-reduce per layer per token, so every layer boundary is a synchronisation point and on a Gen1/no-P2P link the cost is latency-bound (round trip through host memory costs the same for large or small tensors); PP does one hidden-state hand-off per stage boundary instead. Measured on the club's four-card box: GLM-5.3-Flash AWQ W4A16 TP4 + MTP k=3 = 60.4 tok/s c=1 decode (median of 5, peak 77.9), ~38 tok/s aggregate at c=8; PP4 on the unpatched fork collapsed to 3.35 tok/s c=1 (MTP k=5) / 6.1 spec-off, but after a 24-patch port landed 2026-09-05 PP4 reached 87.55 tok/s c=1 (median of 3) and 67.91 (temp 0.7 + ignore_eos) — so PP4 became the recipe of record and TP4 was superseded. Community-reported 2-card bare-metal result (issue #59, Intel X99 root ports, Intel ACS blocks P2P, NCCL_P2P_DISABLE=1, cards at Gen2 x4 after cmpunlocker): PP2 beats TP2+EP on every metric — decode c=1 87.8 vs 69.7 tok/s, prefill 3,610/5,413/5,897 vs 1,388/1,282/1,274 tok/s at ~1.3k/5k/15k, aggregate c=4/c=8 169.8/351.7 vs 110.5/223.6. TP2's prefill is flat at ~1.3k tok/s across prompt sizes — the comm-bound signature the section predicts. Community-reported PP8 cost model from promisezackr/glm53-flash-170hx-pp8 on an 8-card PCIe Gen2 x4 box: step time ~32 ms fixed + 2.1 ms x verified tokens per step, with GLM-5.3-Flash NVFP4 PP8 DFlash2 k=7 delivering 123 tok/s single-stream (code) / 37 tok/s (prose) and 276 tok/s aggregate at 16 streams. PP boot facts measured: VLLM_PP_LAYER_PARTITION honoured exactly (14,12,12,7 on a 45-layer model); --gpu-memory-utilization 0.80 leaves negative KV headroom at 44.5-45.5 GiB/card of weights, 0.92 serves (1,248,304 KV tokens); --enforce-eager PP step time ~160 ms; uncached prefill 1,053 tok/s PP4 vs 818 tok/s TP4. Speculative-decoding sweep (k=0/2/3/5/7, 2026-09-05): acceptance falls 74.0% at k=2 to 37.3% at k=7 while mean accepted length rises only 1.46x, so k=3 is the record — per-verified-token cost is not flat on this box. NVFP4 is not viable on SM80 (no native FP4; checkpoint widens to ~60 GiB resident per card and OOMs during weight load at both 0.90 and 0.85 utilisation).

- `2026-10-27` [[club-170hxdocshardwaremd-at-main-pixelmlclub-170hx-github]] — club-170hx/docs/HARDWARE.md at main · PixelML/club-170hx · GitHub — PixelML/club-170hx HARDWARE.md (81 lines, commit 77e8951, 2026-10-02): the club's four-card CMP 170HX rig. Cards enumerate as PCI 10de:20c2, compute capability 8.0, 65,536 MiB each after the community unlock (256 GiB aggregate). Explicitly 'not equivalent to a supported A100': no NVLink, so every multi-card collective crosses PCIe and 'can dominate runtime'; passive heatsink needs forced front-to-back airflow; unsupported unlock path means version pinning and recovery are normal operation. PCIe section carries two dated measurements that contradict each other by design: 2026-09-02 stock-driver record shows LnkCap 2.5GT/s x16 (Gen1 advertised maximum) with one card training x8 on a bifurcated slot; a 2026-10-02 update states that with the cmpunlocker link patch all four cards on the 4-card PLX host train Gen2 x16, and Gen3 is blocked by the OPT_DISABLE_GEN3_SPEED fuse reading 1 on every card checked. Known-good configuration: four cards enumerated in one Ubuntu guest on 2026-08-30 at 65,536 MiB each; three cards passed CUDA enumeration, a 16 GiB cross-window write/read smoke test, an SM kernel and post-load health checks under Ubuntu 22.04 / kernel 6.8 / driver 610.43.03. Four-card workload performance remains untested in this document.
- `2026-10-27` [[nccl-tuning-guide-ring-tree-and-all-to-all-algorithms-for-optimal-multi-gpu-comm]] — NCCL Tuning Guide: Ring, Tree, and All-to-All Algorithms for Optimal Multi-GPU Communication at Scale | ClusterBid — Vendor blog (ClusterBid, Feb 2026, ~370 words) summarising NCCL's three algorithms: ring all-reduce moves 2(N-1)/N x message size and is bandwidth-optimal for messages >1 MB; tree is O(log N) steps and reportedly 20-30% faster than ring for 128 KB-1 MB messages (and typically faster under 512 KB on H100 DGX); all-to-all has the lowest latency for small messages but needs O(N^2) connections. Claims manual NCCL tuning yields 15-40% improvements, and states a heuristic that communication-to-computation ratio should stay below 15% for efficient scaling at 1,000+ GPUs. Low authority - no measurements, no methodology, no dates on the underlying benchmarks; useful only as a pointer to the ring/tree crossover heuristic, which should be verified against NCCL docs or a primary paper before being cited.
- `2026-10-27` [[zipccl-efficient-lossless-data-compression-of]] — ZipCCL: Efficient Lossless Data Compression of — ZipCCL (Lin, Pan, Fan, Shi, Chu; arXiv 2604.27844) is a lossless compression layer for NCCL collectives in LLM training. Core observation: LLM training tensors (activations, gradients, parameters) are near-Gaussian, so BF16 exponents are highly concentrated - the top-7 most frequent exponents cover >97% of the data. ZipCCL encodes each BF16 exponent in 3 bits (7 codes plus a zero-point escape flag) instead of 8, leaving sign/mantissa untouched, for a theoretical compression ratio up to 11 bits from 16 (~1.45x). It adds GPU-optimised compress/decompress kernels with communication-aware data layout and an adaptive switcher driven by a cost model over measured bandwidth, latency and compression ratio.

Evaluation: 64-GPU cluster of 8x RTX A6000 per node over a 200 Gb/s network, integrated into Megatron-LM (MoE all-to-all) and TorchTitan (FSDP all-gather/reduce-scatter), plus a 16xH800 400 Gb/s cluster. Communication speedups over NCCL: 1.25x on DeepSeek-V3 all-to-all, 1.24x on Qwen3-MoE, 1.35x on Llama3-8B all-gather; averaged across message sizes 1.36x for all-to-all and 1.31x for all-gather. End-to-end training speedups: 1.16x (DeepSeek-V3), 1.16x (Qwen3-MoE), 1.18x (Llama3-8B); on the 16xH800 cluster 1.13x for both Qwen3-MoE and Llama3-8B.

Two findings are load-bearing for this box. First, the benefit is bandwidth-saturation dependent: "When the data volume is small, bandwidth cannot be fully utilized, and our ZipCCL shows very close performance to NCCL. However, as the data volume increases ... and bandwidth is better saturated, the improvement ... becomes significant." On a ~1 GB/s fabric the saturation point arrives at far smaller tensors, so 1.3x is a floor rather than a ceiling. Second, Reduce-Scatter was NOT worth compressing on their platform ("the original Reduce-Scatter implementation was found to be faster"), so a DDP/FSDP gradient path does not automatically inherit the gain - all-gather did, reduce-scatter did not.

Also notable: ZipCCL beats DietGPU (Meta's GPU lossless compressor), where DietGPU-compressed all-gather was slower than plain NCCL all-gather while ZipCCL got 1.35x. The scheme is bit-exact, so unlike FP8/quantisation work it carries no convergence risk; the relevant risk on this box is compress/decompress kernel cost against 5 weak GPUs.
- `2026-10-27` [[agoq-activation-and-gradient-quantization-for]] — AGoQ: Activation and Gradient Quantization for — AGoQ (Lin et al., HIT Shenzhen + Huawei; arXiv 2605.00539, May 2026) combines two techniques for memory-efficient distributed LLM training: a layer-aware activation quantisation that assigns bit-widths per layer type and pipeline stage to reach near-4-bit activation storage (RMSNorm 4U -> 0.5U, SiLU & Multiply 12U -> 2U, attention stays at 5U; overall activation memory drops from 28U in Megatron-LM to 7.75U, an approximately three-fold reduction), and QuanGrad, an 8-bit (FP8) gradient representation used both for local accumulation and for a "precision-preserved FP8 All-Reduce" implemented as quantise -> reduce-scatter -> re-quantise -> all-gather.

Reported results: up to 52% memory reduction and up to 1.34x training-speed improvement versus Megatron-LM (with and without ZeRO), DeepSpeed and COAT, on 8B-32B LLaMA models, with convergence loss on pretraining and comparable downstream accuracy. Gradient memory drops 75% versus COAT; activation memory drops a further 30% versus COAT.

The most transferable result for this box is their commodity-bandwidth experiment: they explicitly re-ran the collective breakdown at 10 Gbps "to simulate commodity constraints" alongside their 200 Gbps primary testbed. At a 32 MB message under 10 Gbps the decomposed 8-bit all-reduce takes 432.36 ms versus 1,603.78 ms for the baseline - a 3.7x speedup (vs 3.4x at 200 Gbps, 39.17 ms vs 131.23 ms). That is the closest published analogue to the CMP box's ~0.75 GiB/s (~6 Gbps) NCCL fabric: the lower the bandwidth, the LARGER the relative win from halving the payload, which is the opposite of the usual "compression overhead dominates on slow links" intuition.

Caveats: the primary testbed is 64x RTX A6000 over 200 Gb/s InfiniBand (PCIe Gen4-class per-GPU links), and the FP8 path is exercised on NVIDIA Pro 6000 GPUs "to support the FP8 format required by COAT" - again flagging that FP8 needs sm_89+/Hopper-class hardware. GA100 (sm_80) has no native FP8, so on the CMP 170HX the 8-bit path would need int8 or emulated FP8, and the paper does not measure that.
- `2026-10-27` [[geofp8-geometry-aware-fp8-gradient]] — GeoFP8: Geometry-Aware FP8 Gradient — GeoFP8 (Wang et al., Rutgers/NCSU; arXiv 2607.07494) attacks low-precision gradient communication from the geometry side rather than the format side. Diagnosis: gradients are highly anisotropic, so axis-aligned (Euclidean) FP8 scaling incurs direction-dependent distortion; transforming gradients into a near-isotropic space before quantisation - using a block-diagonal K-FAC local metric, applied selectively and approximated with a rank-32 input-side low-rank factor - makes the FP8 representation substantially more faithful. GeoFP8 deliberately does not change the optimizer, training recipe, collective, or low-precision format; only the coordinate system used for the low-precision communication.

Numbers: GeoFP8 reduces the error between FP8-averaged and FP32-averaged gradients by up to 67.4% over the Euclidean baseline; it cuts gradient communication volume by 75.0% relative to FP32, yielding a 7.6% end-to-end pretraining-time reduction for Llama-600M on 64 NVIDIA GH200 Superchips. Direct Euclidean FP8 at the same scale gives a 10.79% end-to-end reduction, i.e. GeoFP8 deliberately trades ~3 points of speed for downstream-task preservation. Experiments use 32 GPUs, global batch 512, micro-batch 4, Muon optimizer, lr 5e-4, on TACC Vista GH200 nodes (96 GB HBM3 per GPU, NVLink-coupled Grace-Hopper).

Two caveats matter for the CMP 170HX box. (1) The 7.6% end-to-end figure is measured on a fast NVLink fabric where gradient communication is a minority of step time; on a ~1 GB/s PCIe fabric the same 75% payload cut would translate into a much larger fraction of step time, so the paper's end-to-end number understates the potential gain here. (2) FP8 is not natively supported on GA100/sm_80 - the CMP 170HX is Ampere-generation silicon without FP8 tensor-core paths, so an FP8 gradient path would need software emulation whose cost is not accounted for in the GH200 measurements. Their BF16-vs-FP32 comparison is also relevant to the repo's bf16 gradient plan: BF16 gradient communication "matches FP32 on 7 out of 14 downstream tasks" but with slightly lower absolute values, which is why they kept FP32 as the reference.
- `2026-10-27` [[communication-efficient-distributed-training]] — Communication-Efficient Distributed Training — Munim & Ramamoorthy (arXiv 2609.23898v2, cs.IT, 26 Sep 2026) propose CERAR, a Communication-Efficient Ring All-Reduce protocol that trades exactness for bandwidth: each worker splits its gradient into c chunks, linearly encodes them (Vandermonde, or a condition-number-constrained construction), and runs L = c + N - 2 rounds over an N-worker ring, giving normalized per-worker communication 1 + (N-2)/c instead of ring all-reduce's 2(N-1)/N, at the cost of a bounded approximation error (~1.6-2.9% relative l2 gradient error in their training runs). The Vandermonde route is asymptotically exact as c grows but ill-conditioned; they give an alternate scheme with error O(eps) and condition number O(eps^-r*), r* = ceil(c/N) - 1, plus a constrained optimization for practical designs.

The headline empirical result lands on the fabric that matters for this box: on a single node of 8x A100 PCIe (interconnect characterised as 31.5 GB/s), CERAR cuts raw all-reduce time by 46.0-47.1% versus NCCL - a 1B-entry FP32 tensor goes from 17,954.4 ms (NCCL) to 9,558.6 ms (CERAR); 500M entries 8,611.2 -> 4,651.0 ms; 100M 1,775.9 -> 940.1 ms. On 4x H200 SXM over NVLink (>=300 GB/s) the same scheme is 33.2-33.9% SLOWER than NCCL (24B entries: 392.049 ms NCCL vs 522.366 ms CERAR), so the win is explicitly a low-bandwidth-fabric phenomenon, not a general one.

End-to-end: Pythia-1.4B on 4x A100 PCIe 40GB for 1,800 steps (~117.96M tokens) takes 3.79 h with NCCL vs 3.14 h with CERAR (-17.3%, ~39 min saved), throughput 8,644 -> 10,451 tok/s, final validation loss 1.4218 vs 1.4213 (essentially identical), peak memory 36.2 vs 38.6 GB. On 4x A100 SXM 80GB over NVLink CERAR is 3.65% slower (4.745 h vs 4.578 h).

Caveats the drafter must carry: (i) the authors call their implementation a proof of concept, not optimised to NCCL's level (NCCL uses multiple channels and compute/comm overlap); (ii) encoding/decoding compute is real and grows with c, so on a box whose GPUs are also weak the arithmetic overhead is not free; (iii) the tested PCIe fabric is 31.5 GB/s, roughly 30x the ~0.75 GiB/s NCCL all-reduce measured on the CMP 170HX box, so the communication-bound regime is far deeper here - the direction of the effect should hold, the magnitude is not transferable; (iv) the approximation error is ~2-3% relative on gradients, a training-quality risk the paper argues is benign at 1.4B scale only.
- `2026-10-27` [[github-deepseek-aideepseek-v32-exp-github]] — GitHub - deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V3.2-Exp · GitHub — README for DeepSeek's official DeepSeek-V3.2-Exp release (github.com/deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V3.2-Exp, 18 commits, last Nov 2025). Introduces DSA as an experimental intermediate step toward a next-generation architecture, framed as exploring and validating training and inference efficiency optimizations for long-context scenarios. States that DSA delivers substantial long-context training and inference efficiency gains 'while maintaining virtually identical model output quality', and that the training configuration was deliberately aligned with V3.1-Terminus so the effect of sparse attention could be isolated; the benchmark table shows near-parity (MMLU-Pro 85.0 vs 85.0, GPQA-Diamond 80.7 vs 79.9, AIME 2025 88.4 vs 89.3, SWE Verified 68.4 vs 67.8). The most useful item for a reproduction attempt is the 2025.11.17 update: previous inference demo code had a RoPE implementation discrepancy in the indexer module — the indexer's RoPE input requires a non-interleaved layout while MLA's RoPE expects an interleaved layout — which was fixed. Thin source (770 words); the substantive training recipe lives in the V3.2 technical report, not here.
- `2026-10-27` [[github-rightnow-aistreamindex-memory-bounded-compressed-sparse-attention-via-str]] — GitHub - RightNow-AI/StreamIndex: Memory-bounded compressed sparse attention via streaming top-k. Triton kernels for the DeepSeek-V4 lightning indexer. 32x regime extension on a single H200 | by RightNow https://www.rightnowai.co/ · GitHub — README for the StreamIndex Triton implementation (github.com/RightNow-AI/StreamIndex, 2 commits, May 2026). Confirms the paper's headline table verbatim: at S=32,768 the materialize path takes 317 ms and 129 GB while the chunked path takes 31 ms and 0.40 GB (10.3x); the materialize path OOMs from S=65,536 upward; the chunked path reaches S=1,048,576 in 30,900 ms at 6.21 GB peak HBM. States the mechanism plainly: public CSA implementations (V3.2-Exp reference, TileLang reference) materialize a [B, S, H_I, T] FP32 score tensor before top-k, which is 256 GB at S=65,536 with V4-Flash dimensions (H_I=64, m=4) and exceeds the HBM of any single current GPU. Requirements: PyTorch >= 2.10, Triton >= 3.5, CUDA 12.x or 13.x, Python >= 3.11. Critically for this run: 'Tested on H100 / H200 (Hopper, sm_90a)' — no Ampere validation, so porting to a CMP 170HX (GA100, sm_80) is unverified work. Raw benchmark logs are committed under benchmarks/results/. Thin source (1,209 words) — useful as an adoption-risk and reproducibility check, not as independent evidence.
- `2026-10-27` [[you-only-cache-once]] — You Only Cache Once: — YOCO — You Only Cache Once: Decoder-Decoder Architectures for Language Models (Sun, Dong, Zhu et al., Microsoft Research + Tsinghua, arXiv 2405.05254). The origin of the self-decoder/cross-decoder cross-layer KV sharing that HySparse2 and (in spirit) CSA2's Reuse mode build on. A self-decoder with efficient attention (gated retention) encodes the global KV cache once; a cross-decoder stacked on top reuses that cache via cross-attention. The model behaves like a decoder-only Transformer while caching global KV only once, and the computation flow lets prefill early-exit without changing the final output.

Profiling results: KV cache memory reduced by about 80x for 65B models; for a 3B model, overall inference memory reduced 2x at 32K tokens and more than 9x at 1M tokens; prefill speedup 2.87x at 32K input and 71.8x at 1M context (a 512K context drops from 180 s to under 6 s). This is the cleanest illustration of the pattern that recurs across every source in this batch: cross-layer KV sharing's published wins are long-context wins, and at 32K they shrink to roughly 2x memory and ~2.9x prefill.

Trainability: a 3B YOCO model is scaled to trillions of training tokens with results on par with Transformers, and the context is extended to 1M tokens with near-perfect needle retrieval accuracy — evidence that cross-layer KV sharing is a trainable architecture rather than an inference-only trick. Caveat worth carrying: "only caches once" refers strictly to the global KV cache; the self-decoder still stores a bounded number of local caches. Motivating context: a 512K-token KV cache occupies about 86 GB even with KV quantization, exceeding a single H100-80GB, and optimized prefill of 450K tokens takes about 110 s (380 s for 1M). 8,270 words.
- `2026-10-27` [[native-sparse-attention-hardware-aligned-and-natively]] — Native Sparse Attention: Hardware-Aligned and Natively — NSA — Native Sparse Attention: Hardware-Aligned and Natively Trainable Sparse Attention (Yuan, Gao, Dai et al., DeepSeek-AI, arXiv 2502.11089). The canonical "natively trainable sparse attention" paper and the most directly relevant counterweight to the repo's CSA2-at-32k pessimism. NSA combines three branches — compressed attention, selected block attention, and a sliding window — with blockwise selection as the core hardware-alignment principle: key/value sequences are divided into selection blocks, block importance is scored, and top-n blocks are selected. The paper's argument is that token-granular selection (e.g. HashAttention) forces loading many individual KV tokens, which breaks the contiguous memory access that FlashAttention-style kernels depend on and degrades training efficiency; blockwise selection instead keeps contiguous access and enables full Tensor Core utilization.

Measured kernel results (Triton NSA vs Triton FlashAttention-2, same backend, A100): forward speedup 2.1x at 8k, 3.8x at 16k, 6.3x at 32k, 9.0x at 64k; backward speedup 1.1x at 8k, 2.0x at 16k, 3.4x at 32k, 6.0x at 64k; decode up to 11.6x at 64k. This is the key data point that sparse attention is ALREADY a net training win at 32k when selection is block-granular — which reconciles with LongCat's 0.83x net loss at 32K, measured on token-granular gather.

Training regime: a 27B-parameter backbone trained on 260B tokens; both the full-attention baseline and NSA are pretrained on 270B tokens of 8k-length texts, then continued-trained and SFT'd on 32k-length texts with YaRN. So NSA's own training context tops out at 32k — a direct precedent for a 32k sparse-attention experiment. Selection config: compression block 32, sliding stride 16, selected block size 64, 16 selected blocks (including 1 initial and 2 local blocks), sliding window 512, i.e. roughly 1,024 selected tokens plus a 512-token window at any context length. NSA matches or beats full attention on MMLU/MMLU-PRO/CMMLU/BBH/GSM8K/MATH/DROP/MBPP/HumanEval with a stable pretraining loss curve. Caveat: the speedups are against a Triton FlashAttention-2 baseline, not a tuned production kernel. 9,311 words.
- `2026-10-27` [[deepseek-v32-pushing-the-frontier-of-open]] — DeepSeek-V3.2: Pushing the Frontier of Open — DeepSeek-V3.2 technical report (arXiv 2512.02556) — the origin of DeepSeek Sparse Attention (DSA) and therefore the primary source for the indexer training recipe the repo infers. The lightning indexer computes an index score between each query token and the latent KV entries using a small number of indexer heads and a ReLU activation (chosen for throughput rather than softmax), and a fine-grained token-selection mechanism retrieves only the top-k KV entries, reducing core attention from O(L^2) to O(Lk) while the indexer itself retains a complexity that grows with sequence length.

The two-stage training recipe is stated with exact numbers. Dense warm-up: keep dense attention and freeze all model parameters except the lightning indexer; build a target distribution by summing the main attention scores across all heads and L1-normalizing along the sequence dimension; minimize a KL divergence between that target and the softmax of the indexer scores; LR 1e-3; 1000 steps of 16 sequences of 128K tokens = 2.1B tokens. Sparse training: introduce fine-grained token selection and optimize all parameters; keep the KL alignment but renormalized over only the selected token set S_t; detach the indexer input from the computational graph so the indexer's training signal comes only from its own KL loss while the main model is optimized only on the language-modeling loss; LR 7.3e-6; select 2048 KV tokens per query; 15000 steps of 480 sequences of 128K tokens = 943.7B tokens.

Two facts matter most for the repo's CSA2-at-32k claim: (1) the DSA lineage trains at 128K sequence length, not 32k — both the 2.1B-token indexer warm-up and the 943.7B-token sparse stage use 128K sequences; (2) the indexer and backbone are trained as two separate objectives via a detached graph, which is the concrete mechanism behind the repo's "extra loss term, not a drop-in" claim. The report also covers the 128K long-context extension and the post-training recipe (specialist distillation plus mixed RL). 9,409 words.
- `2026-10-27` [[deepseek-v4]] — DeepSeek-V4: — DeepSeek-V4 technical report (DeepSeek-AI, arXiv 2606.19348) — the CSA/HCA predecessor that CSA2 in V4.1-Flash simplifies. V4-Flash is 43 layers at d=4096 with pure SWA in the first two layers and CSA and HCA interleaved thereafter; CSA uses compression rate m=4, 64 indexer query heads, indexer head dimension 128, and attention top-k of 512 (V4-Flash) or 1024 (V4-Pro); HCA uses m'=128. Under this configuration V4-Flash is 284B total parameters.

The training recipe is the load-bearing part for the CSA2-at-32k question. Training starts at a 4K sequence length and is gradually extended to 16K, 64K and 1M. Critically, V4 warms up with DENSE attention for the first 1T tokens and only introduces sparse attention at a sequence length of 64K — the direct opposite of V4.1-Flash's "no dense attention warmup stages" claim, and in both generations sparse attention is introduced at 64K, never at 32k. When sparsity is introduced, V4 first runs a short stage to warm up the lightning indexer in CSA and then trains with sparse attention for most of the run. Batch size is raised to 75.5M tokens and held there; LR is warmed up over 2000 steps, held at 2.7e-4, then cosine-decayed to 2.7e-5. Sample-level attention masking is used during pretraining.

Precision and determinism machinery: KV entries use a mixed storage format (BF16 for RoPE dimensions, FP8 for the rest) which nearly halves KV cache size versus pure BF16; lightning-indexer attention is computed in FP4 precision; FP4 QAT is applied during post-training to MoE expert weights and the indexer QK path. Sparse-attention backward uses atomicAdd to accumulate KV gradients, which makes training non-deterministic, so V4 allocates per-SM accumulation buffers and performs a deterministic global summation across them. Optimizer is Muon for the majority of parameters with AdamW for the embedding module.

Also relevant: V4 explicitly notes that relative to DeepSeek-V3.2 a SMALLER attention top-k is chosen in the V4 series to improve efficiency on short- and medium-length texts — i.e. the top-k budget was tuned down for shorter contexts, which undercuts the repo's framing that the budget is "tuned for 1M context" and therefore mismatched to 32k. Sparse-attention kernels are co-designed with the KV cache layout (padding blocks to align with hardware), and the visible range of compressed KV entries is precomputed by rules. 26,488 words — long source, flag for source-analyst.
- `2026-10-27` [[over-tokenized-transformer-vocabulary-is-generally-worth-scaling]] — Over-Tokenized Transformer: Vocabulary is Generally Worth Scaling — OverEncoding / Over-Tokenized Transformer (ByteDance Seed, arXiv 2501.16975, ICML 2025) - the direct prior-art baseline Engram compares against. Decouples input and output vocabularies and scales the input vocabulary with hierarchical n-gram tokens; finds a log-linear relationship between input vocabulary size and training loss, so OE-12.8M matches a 2.5x larger baseline at no extra training cost (400M OE vs 1B baseline), with a 5.7x convergence speedup on OLMo2-1B at 1T tokens and 3.2x/3.0x/2.6x downstream accelerations. MOST IMPORTANT PART FOR THIS QUERY is Section 3.3 'Engineering Challenges and Solutions', which is the only source in this batch that reports measured training-time cost of large embedding tables under real sharding strategies: (a) with plain FSDP, the sparse embedding parameters' communication severely degrades training efficiency and FSDP 'easily runs out of memory for vocabulary sizes exceeding m = 5x10^6'; (b) without mitigation FSDP experiences a 25% slowdown; (c) the recommended fix is tensor parallelism specifically for the embedding layer - row-wise sharding across all DP ranks with the token sent to the owning rank and the vector sent back, i.e. two all-to-all in the forward pass and one all-to-all in the backward pass, 'a total communication volume significantly lower than that of FSDP'; (d) with that optimization, training an over-encoded model at m = 10^7 on FSDP loses less than 5% throughput. The paper also proposes, but does not measure, a dedicated embedding-lookup pipeline stage that would overlap embedding communication with the transformer forward of the current micro-batch and allow over-encoding parameters to be offloaded to CPU, citing Fang et al. 2022 and Li et al. 2023. MoE results: OLMoE-1.3B +OE-12.8M goes 1.3B -> 13.1B params with loss -0.082 and downstream +0.014; OLMoE-7B +OE-12.8M goes 7B -> 26.3B with loss -0.076 and downstream +0.007, at 500B tokens.
- `2026-10-27` [[mics-near-linear-scaling-for-training-gigantic-model-on-public]] — MiCS: Near-linear Scaling for Training Gigantic Model on Public — MiCS (JHU / AWS / Peking University): minimizes communication scale - the number of participants in each collective - rather than communication volume, so that collectives stay inside the fastest available group. The motivating asymmetry: DGX-2/DGX-A100 clusters have intra-node bandwidth only about 3x faster than inter-node, so ZeRO's flat all-device partitioning is tolerable; public-cloud V100 instances on 100 Gbps networks have intra-node bandwidth about 24x faster than inter-node, so flat partitioning is not. MiCS adds scale-aware model partitioning and a hierarchical communication strategy. Results on AWS: 2.89x throughput over DeepSpeed on V100/100 Gbps, up to 2.74x on A100-40GB/400 Gbps, 30.1% better than Megatron-LM-3D, 99.4% weak-scaling efficiency, and a 100B model at over 170 TFLOPS per A100 across 512 GPUs. Directly relevant because a 5-GPU PCIe box has no fast tier at all, so 'minimize scale' collapses to 'use all five cards in one flat group'.
- `2026-10-27` [[zero-ofﬂoad-democratizing-billion-scale-model-training]] — ZeRO-Ofﬂoad: Democratizing Billion-Scale Model Training — ZeRO-Offload (Microsoft / UC Merced): offloads optimizer states and gradients to CPU memory to train 13B parameters on a single V100-32GB, a 10x increase over the 1.4B ceiling of plain PyTorch, at 40 TFLOPS/GPU for a 10B model versus 30 TFLOPS for PyTorch on 1.4B. Scales near-linearly to 128 GPUs and reaches 70B parameters on one DGX-2 box when combined with model parallelism (4.5x over MP alone). The load-bearing constraint for this run: CPU memory bandwidth is at least an order of magnitude faster than PCIe bandwidth between CPU and GPU, and GPU memory is another order of magnitude faster again, so the design must minimize CPU-GPU traffic (theoretical minimum 4M for a model-state offload). On a PCIe Gen2 x4 box the PCIe tier is roughly an order of magnitude slower than a modern x16 Gen4 link, so host-offload is even less attractive than in this paper's V100 testbed.
- `2026-10-27` [[arxiv221105953v2-csdc-6-jul-2023]] — Breadth-First Pipeline Parallelism (Lamy-Poirier, arXiv 2211.05953) — Lamy-Poirier (Mila): Breadth-First Pipeline Parallelism, the schedule ZeroPP builds on. It combines pipeline parallelism with fully sharded data parallelism and schedules micro-batches breadth-first so that FSDP unsharding is amortized across many micro-batches instead of being repeated per micro-batch. Reported gain: up to 43% higher training throughput for a 52B-parameter model at a small batch size per GPU compared to Megatron-LM, with lower memory usage. Assumes clusters of hundreds or thousands of GPUs with fast NVLink intra-node and slower InfiniBand inter-node, and targets the regime where batch size per GPU (beta = B/N_GPU) is the binding constraint. Relevant here as the primary source for the PP+FSDP composition that a TP-free, low-bandwidth recipe depends on.
- `2026-10-27` [[ultramemv2-memory-networks-scaling-to-120b]] — UltraMemV2: Memory Networks Scaling to 120B — ByteDance Seed UltraMemV2 (arXiv 2508.18756, 27 Aug 2025) - memory-layer architecture scaled to 120B total parameters with 2.5B activated, reaching parity with 8-expert MoE at matched compute and parameters while requiring far fewer memory accesses. Five changes over UltraMem: memory layers in every transformer block, single-linear-projection value expansion, FFN-based value processing from PEER, principled initialization, and rebalanced memory-to-FFN compute ratios. Configurations: UltraMemV2-2.5B/120B-top256 (256 activated values per memory layer from 120B sparse params) and UltraMemV2-2.5B/60B-top768 (768 activated values from 60B sparse params), with row/column TopM capped at 128 to avoid quadratic intermediate explosion; the open-source variant uses 20 memory layers with 1 head and TopM=32. Training: 1.6T tokens PT then 250B CT, with selected models extended to 3.9T PT plus 500B of 32K-context CT; open-source models at 500B-1T tokens. Gains concentrate on memory-intensive tasks: +1.6 long-context memorization, +6.2 multi-round memorization, +7.9 in-context learning. Central finding: activation density (top-m) matters more than total sparse parameter count, though higher activation raises inference latency. RELEVANT TO THE FROZEN-TABLE QUESTION: the 120B sparse memory parameters are trained from scratch jointly with the backbone, not frozen - so the largest published memory-layer model treats memory as trainable capacity. Caveat: the paper reports no GPU hardware, no per-step latency, and no distributed-training topology.
- `2026-10-27` [[megatron-lm-training-multi-billion-parameter-language-models-using]] — Megatron-LM: Training Multi-Billion Parameter Language Models Using — Shoeybi et al. (NVIDIA), the canonical tensor-parallelism paper and the primary source for TP's communication bill. A transformer layer under Megatron TP needs exactly 2 all-reduces in the forward pass and 2 in the backward pass (4 communication ops per layer), implemented with conjugate f/g autograd operators; the MLP block alone costs 1 all-reduce forward + 1 backward, and the vocabulary-parallel output embedding adds an all-reduce plus an all-gather. Trains an 8.3B GPT-2-like model on 512 V100-32GB GPUs (32 DGX-2H servers) at 15.1 PFLOPs sustained and 76% scaling efficiency against a single-GPU baseline of 39 TFLOPS (30% of peak). Infrastructure: 300 GB/s intra-server NVSwitch, 100 GB/s inter-server over 8 InfiniBand adapters. An 8B model requires 8-way model parallelism. This is the arithmetic behind 'TP is ruled out by the fabric': four all-reduces per layer, each blocking the next GEMM, on a link that is orders of magnitude slower than NVSwitch.
- `2026-10-27` [[powersgd-practical-low-rank]] — PowerSGD: Practical Low-Rank — Vogels, Karimireddy & Jaggi (EPFL): low-rank gradient compression built on power iteration, with error feedback to preserve convergence. The key structural insight is that linear compressors can be aggregated with all-reduce, whereas sign-SGD and coordinate-quantization schemes need a gather, which is why they do not scale on frameworks that rely on all-reduce. On a 16-GPU setting with NCCL over a fast 10 Gbit/s network, PowerSGD compresses gradients more than 120x and cuts communication time by 54% (ResNet18/CIFAR10) and 90% (LSTM/WikiText-2), giving end-to-end wall-clock reductions of 24% and 55% and a 1.5x time-to-accuracy speedup at rank 16. Critical caveat for this run: the authors observe that other compressors' speedups 'vanish with a fast network and highly optimized communication backend' - compression only pays when the network is the bottleneck, which is exactly the PCIe Gen2 x4 case.
- `2026-10-27` [[zero-extremely-efficient-collective-communication-for-giant]] — ZeRO++: Extremely Efficient Collective Communication for Giant — ZeRO++ (Microsoft): three communication-volume reductions aimed at exactly the two regimes this box sits in - low-bandwidth clusters and small per-GPU batches. It restates ZeRO's baseline cost as 3M total volume spread over 2 all-gathers and 1 reduce-scatter, then (1) qwZ quantizes weights FP16 to INT8 for the forward all-gather, halving that collective's volume, with block-wise quantization to protect accuracy; (2) hpZ keeps a full model copy inside each machine and replaces the cross-machine backward all-gather with an intra-machine all-gather, trading memory for bandwidth; (3) qgZ replaces the reduce-scatter with an all-to-all-based quantized gradient averaging that preserves accuracy despite low-precision data.
