Net: 4x lower communication volume than ZeRO, up to 2.16x throughput at 384 GPUs (up to 2.4x over ZeRO in the body). Notes that per-GPU throughput on low-bandwidth clusters is only half that of high-bandwidth clusters, and that cross-node links are often under 100 Gbps Ethernet.
- `2026-10-27` [[scaling-embeddings-outperforms-scaling-experts-in-language]] — Scaling Embeddings Outperforms Scaling Experts in Language — Meituan LongCat team technical report (arXiv 2601.21204) introducing LongCat-Flash-Lite, the production-scale n-gram-memory model that EngramEdit uses as its substrate. CRITICAL FOR THE FROZEN-TABLE QUESTION: LongCat-Flash-Lite allocates 31.4B of its 68.5B parameters (46%) to N-gram Embedding and trains them FROM SCRATCH jointly with the backbone - they are not frozen. Architecture: 14 shortcut layers, MoE with 256 FFN experts plus 128 zero-experts and top-12 routing, 2.9B-4.5B activated parameters per token, 31.4B N-gram Embedding parameters. Training: 11T tokens at 8k sequence length, then 1.5T tokens of mid-training extending to 128k, with YaRN applied during the 32k stage, supporting up to 256k sequences. Scaling findings that bear on the repo's allocation premise: (1) N-gram Embedding should be introduced only once the number of experts exceeds its 'sweet spot' - at low parameter ratios embedding scaling fails to beat simply adding experts, while at high sparsity the benefit is pronounced; (2) allocate no more than 50% of the total parameter budget to N-gram Embedding, with the crossover slightly above a ratio of 20 where embeddings are ~50% of total - consistent with Engram's U-shaped law; (3) N-gram vocabulary size should deviate significantly from integer multiples of the base vocabulary size, since collision counts spike near integer multiples regardless of primality; (4) n-gram order N in 3-5 is near-optimal and K>=2 sub-tables is enough. Systems: an 'N-gram Cache' with custom CUDA kernels manages N-gram IDs on-device, and embedding lookups cost scales with input tokens rather than total embedding parameters, so a larger embedding layer does not penalize decode latency; inference measured on 8xH800-80G at DP1TP8 and DP8TP1 with ISL=4K/OSL=1K.
- `2026-10-27` [[scalable-training-of-mixture-of-experts-models-with]] — Scalable Training of Mixture-of-Experts Models with — Scalable Training of MoE Models with Megatron Core (NVIDIA technical report, arXiv 2603.07685, Mar 2026; 39k words): the production reference for how EP/TP/PP/DP compose for MoE training, and the source of the arithmetic this run needs for the EP question. It frames MoE training as three coupled walls. The Communication Wall: EP's per-GPU send volume in one all-to-all is approximately T*K*h*(EP-1)/EP (T local tokens, K top-k, h hidden dim), and a dispatch+combine cycle doubles it; as EP grows the volume saturates but traffic migrates from NVLink to narrower inter-node links where bandwidth drops by an order of magnitude, and unoptimized all-to-all can consume up to 60% of total training time in DeepSeek-V3-like models. The Memory Wall: all E experts' parameters, gradients and optimizer states must be resident even though only K activate, and offloading to host memory costs PCIe bandwidth. The Compute Efficiency Wall: TP on MoE experts is actively harmful because expert hidden dimensions are already small, so TP shards them into even smaller GEMMs while adding communication; GEMMs are ~70% of execution time in dense Llama-3 405B but under 50% in DeepSeek-V3, with routing/permutation adding ~9%. DeepSeek-V3 illustrates the parameter-compute mismatch: 685B total vs 37B active per token (18x). Dispatcher backends are AllGather, all-to-all (NCCL), and Flex (DeepEP/HybridEP, which assume NVLink-rich topologies). Reference throughput on GB300/GB200: 1,233/1,048 TFLOPS/GPU for DeepSeek-V3-685B, 974/919 for Qwen3-235B. Very long source — flag for source-analyst if the EP arithmetic needs to be reproduced exactly.
- `2026-10-27` [[fire-flyer-ai-hpc-a-cost-effective]] — Fire-Flyer AI-HPC: A Cost-Effective — Fire-Flyer AI-HPC (DeepSeek-AI, arXiv 2408.14158, SC24): the production-cluster counterexample that matters most for this run — a 10,000-GPU cluster built entirely from PCIe A100s (no NVLink in the original design), reaching performance approximating DGX-A100 at half the cost and 40% less energy. Its contribution HFReduce is a CPU-based all-reduce designed for exactly this fabric: intra-node reduce first (async D2H copy of gradients to pinned host memory, reduction with CPU vector instructions), then inter-node all-reduce over RDMA using a double-binary-tree, then H2D back to the GPUs (GDRCopy lets one host read feed four GPUs on the same NUMA node, cutting host reads 3x). The bandwidth argument is the load-bearing part: NCCL's ring topology sends each unit of data through 2n-1 transmissions, consuming (2n-1)/n units of PCIe bidirectional bandwidth — 1.8 units at n=5 — whereas HFReduce consumes exactly 1 unit (one D2H plus one H2D). Measured on Fire-Flyer 2 at 186 MiB: HFReduce reaches 6.3-8.1 GB/s inter-node bandwidth while NCCL reaches only 1.6-4.8 GB/s; adding an NVLink bridge between paired A100s pushes HFReduce past 10 GB/s. HFReduce also uses the GPU copy engine rather than SM kernels, so it does not steal compute. Relevance: it establishes that a PCIe-only fabric is a legitimate production training substrate, and that replacing NCCL's ring all-reduce with a host-reduction scheme is worth roughly 2-4x on the exact bottleneck this box has.
- `2026-10-27` [[github-mcrltccl-thunder-research-groups-collective-communication-library-github]] — GitHub - mcrl/tccl: Thunder Research Group's Collective Communication Library · GitHub — TCCL (Thunder Research Group, SNU; ASPLOS 2024; github.com/mcrl/tccl): a fork of NCCL for 'PCIe-dependent systems' — clusters where GPUs must communicate through the CPU's PCIe host bridge and possibly NUMA interconnects, with no NVLink and no external PCIe switches. Its premise is the key claim for this run: existing collective libraries do NOT achieve the performance their analytical models predict on PCIe-dependent systems, for two reasons that only appear when traffic goes through the CPU — transfers slow down when multiple transfers traverse the CPU in a particular pattern because of NUMA architecture, and bounce buffers are poorly placed when GPUs communicate via CPU memory. Neither congestion pattern shows up on links that bypass the CPU, so NCCL's topology logic never learns to avoid them. TCCL's pathfinder profiles each candidate communication path and merges single-node paths into multi-node paths (brute force is exponential in GPU count), and the runtime is a drop-in libnccl.so replacement selected via NCCL_ALGO=TCCL plus TCCL_XML_FILE. Thin source (README only, ~1.1k words) but ground-truth tier: it is the code artifact behind the ASPLOS paper, last committed Jul 2025.
- `2026-10-27` [[mobius-fine-tuning-large-scale-models-on]] — Mobius: Fine Tuning Large-Scale Models on — Mobius (Feng et al., Tsinghua, ASPLOS 2023, DOI 10.1145/3575693.3575703): the original pipeline-parallel + heterogeneous-memory system for commodity GPU servers, and the cleanest statement of why ZeRO-style data parallelism fails on this class of hardware. Commodity GPUs communicate only over PCIe 3.0 at 16 GB/s versus up to 900 GB/s NVLink, and they lack GPUDirect P2P, so every inter-GPU byte is routed GPU->CPU DRAM->GPU and concurrent transfers contend at the CPU root complex. Mobius profiles DeepSpeed on 4x 3090-Ti and finds communication is over 70% of total training time, with traffic of 7.3x the model size per training step (all-reduce of every parameter's gradient plus all-gather of every layer's parameters), and most transfers reaching only 50% of the root complex's maximum bandwidth. Its fix is PP with stages stored in DRAM and swapped in/out, a mixed-integer-program partition, and 'cross mapping' that deliberately places adjacent stages on GPUs under DIFFERENT root complexes to avoid contention. Results: 3.8-5.1x lower training time than GPipe and DeepSpeed across 3B/8B/15B/51B GPT-like models on 8x 3090-Ti (24 GB, PCIe 3.0 x8, four GPUs per root complex), three topologies (Topo 4 / 2+2 / 1+3), sequence length 512. Relevance: this is the direct ancestor of RoundPipe and the strongest evidence that PP is the right topology for a no-P2P PCIe box, with the caveat that its contention analysis assumes multiple root complexes — a single-root 5-GPU box has less room for cross mapping.
- `2026-10-27` [[engramengram_demo_v1py-at-main-deepseek-aiengram-github]] — Engram/engram_demo_v1.py at main · deepseek-ai/Engram · GitHub — The only implementation file shipped in deepseek-ai/Engram: a single-file forward-pass demo of the Engram module. Its header disclaimer states it 'requires further optimization for actual production use (e.g., custom CUDA kernels, distributed training support)' - confirming there is NO public distributed-training code for Engram, hence no way to verify the paper's All-to-All sharding from source. The memory table is a plain nn.Embedding inside MultiHeadEmbedding with no requires_grad=False and no freezing logic, so in the reference implementation the n-gram tables are ordinary trainable parameters - direct code-level refutation of the premise that Engram tables are frozen. Default demo config: engram_vocab_size = [129280*5, 129280*5] (646,400 rows per n-gram order for n=2 and n=3), max_ngram_size=3, n_embed_per_ngram=512, n_head_per_ngram=8, layer_ids=[1,15], kernel_size=4, backbone hidden_size=1024, hc_mult=4, 30 layers, vocab 129280. That yields 2 x 646,400 x 8 = 10,342,400 table rows at dim 64 per head. The demo also shows the exact tokenizer-compression normalizer chain (NFKC, NFD, StripAccents, Lowercase, whitespace collapse) and the multiplicative-XOR hash with per-layer odd multipliers and prime-sized per-head buckets.
- `2026-10-27` [[demystifying-nccl-an-in-depth-analysis-of-gpu]] — Demystifying NCCL: An In-depth Analysis of GPU — Demystifying NCCL (Hu, Shen et al., ETH Zurich + NVIDIA + Broadcom, arXiv 2507.04786, HOTI 2025): the primary reference for what NCCL actually does on a given fabric, and the mechanism behind the box's measured ~0.75 GiB/s all-reduce. Three protocol variants: Simple (near-peak bandwidth for large messages, ~6 us per-hop latency, memory fences), LL (4 B data + 4 B flag per 8-byte atomic, ~1 us per-hop, but only 25-50% of peak bandwidth because LL FORCES the intermediate buffer into host memory so the CPU can poll the flag, which precludes GPUDirect RDMA), and LL128 (120 B data + 8 B flag, ~95% of peak, ~2 us per-hop, but requires atomic 128-byte writes that are not split or reordered — NCCL disables LL128 on PCIe where that guarantee is absent). Intra-node transports are P2P (p2p.cc, GPUDirect P2P over NVLink or PCIe), SHM (shm.cc, host-staged), and NVLS; NCCL falls back to SHM not only when P2P is unavailable but also when P2P is suboptimal, e.g. inter-socket PCIe P2P. Algorithmically, Ring AllReduce executes 2k-1 steps per loop iteration and is NON-pipelined (each GPU must finish every task in one iteration before starting the next), so it is latency-bound on host-staged links; Tree AllReduce is pipelined but builds its branching structure only across nodes and links the GPUs inside a node in a simple chain, so on a single-node 5-GPU box the tree degenerates to a chain and buys nothing over ring.
- `2026-10-27` [[llmq-efficient-lower-precision-pretraining]] — LLMQ: Efficient Lower-Precision Pretraining — LLMQ (Schultheis & Alistarh, IST Austria, arXiv 2512.15306, Dec 2025; CPAL 2026): an end-to-end CUDA/C++ training framework for 3B-32B models on commodity GPUs, and the strongest primary evidence that the memory half of this box's problem is solvable. It trains or fine-tunes a 7B model on a SINGLE 16 GB card (RTX 5060Ti, 70% MFU) via 8-bit training, selective recomputation, optimizer-state offload and chunked logits/attention workspace, and a 32B model on 4x RTX 4090 at 3,400 tok/s / 51% MFU (vs 29% MFU on 4x L40S); 14B on the same 4x4090 runs at 7,800 tok/s / 54% MFU, and a single 4090 trains up to 14B. Two findings bear directly on the topology question. First, on recent consumer GPUs the cards cannot communicate directly over PCIe, so placing weights in host memory actually REDUCES required communication: weights go GPU->host once after each optimizer step and are then reused from the host cache across the backward pass and all gradient-accumulation forwards; consequently LLMQ enables sharded weights BEFORE sharded gradients, the reverse of classic ZeRO ordering. Second, NCCL all-gather/reduce-scatter on PCIe showed 'quite low' link utilization, while cudaMemcpy transfers driven by the GPU copy engines achieve much better utilization and reserve no SMs for communication kernels; LLMQ implements all-gather and a three-phase memcpy-based reduce-scatter on copy engines, overlapped with the backward pass of the next transformer block. Table 4 anchors the hardware gap: H100 vs RTX 4090 is 989.4 vs 165.2 BF16 TFLOP/s (6x), 80 vs 24 GB, 900 GB/s NVLink vs 64 GB/s PCIe 4.0 (14x).
- `2026-10-27` [[github-deepseek-aiengram-conditional-memory-via-scalable-lookup-a-new-axis-of-sp]] — GitHub - deepseek-ai/Engram: Conditional Memory via Scalable Lookup: A New Axis of Sparsity for Large Language Models · GitHub — Official deepseek-ai/Engram repository (4 commits, latest Jan 14 2026, 4.8k stars). Contains the paper PDF, the architecture drawio, and a single standalone demo script; the README explicitly labels the code a demonstration that 'mocks standard components (like Attention/MoE/mHC) to focus on the Engram module'. CRITICAL NEGATIVE FINDING for training-feasibility work: the shipped implementation has no distributed training support at all, so the paper's All-to-All table sharding and gradient dispatch path cannot be inspected or reproduced from public code. The repo confirms the system-efficiency claim is scoped to inference ('enabling the offloading of massive embedding tables to host memory with minimal inference overhead').
- `2026-10-27` [[pytorch-fsdp-experiences-on-scaling-fully-sharded-data-parallel-pvldb-16]] — PyTorch FSDP: Experiences on Scaling Fully Sharded Data Parallel (PVLDB 16) — Meta's canonical PyTorch FSDP paper (PVLDB 16(12):3848-3860, 2023; open-access copy of doi 10.14778/3611540.3611569). States explicitly that full sharding 'has 1.5x communication overhead and volume over DDP if using bandwidth optimal ring algorithm' - the primary source for the 1.5x FSDP-vs-DDP multiplier. DDP needs parameters, gradients and optimizer states resident on one device: 'when training models with more than one billion parameters using a 40GB GPU device, DDP will likely encounter out-of-memory errors on each device.' Measured on A100-80GB over 2Tb/s RoCE: FSDP matches DDP at 611M (15.18 vs 14.61 TFLOPS/GPU) and 2.28B (27.40 vs 25.76), then DDP OOMs while FSDP trains 11.3B at 148.48 TFLOPS/GPU in BF16; backward prefetch gives ~18% speedup on GPT-175B; 175B reaches 173-186 TFLOPS/GPU against a 312 TFLOPS BF16 peak. Sharding factor F: F=1 degenerates to DDP AllReduce, F=W is full sharding, 1<F<W is hybrid sharding that keeps shard groups inside a node to exploit higher intra-node bandwidth; TP+FSDP 2D mesh keeps TP intra-node and FSDP inter-node. Mixed precision lets all collectives run in low precision, cutting comm volume. Caveats: optimizer math on FlatParameters breaks mathematical equivalence for norm-dependent optimizers; shared parameters must live in the lowest-common-ancestor FSDP unit.
- `2026-10-27` [[zero-memory-optimizations-toward-training-trillion-parameter-models-sc20]] — ZeRO: Memory Optimizations Toward Training Trillion Parameter Models (SC20) — ZeRO (SC20) canonical memory-optimization paper, arXiv copy of doi 10.1109/sc41405.2020.00024. Derives the DP communication-volume arithmetic this run needs: an all-reduce is reduce-scatter plus all-gather, each moving Psi elements, so standard DP costs 2Psi per step; ZeRO-1/2 (Pos+g) stays at exactly 2Psi; ZeRO-3 (Pos+g+p) costs 3Psi, i.e. 1.5x baseline DP. Memory: a 7.5B model needs 120 GB under standard DP, 31.4 GB (Pos, Nd=64), 16.6 GB (Pos+g), 1.88 GB (Pos+g+p); 'Without ZeRO, the largest model DP alone can run has less than 1.5 Billion parameters' on 32 GB V100s; 1T parameters fit on 1024 GPUs. Empirically on 400 V100s (25 DGX-2 nodes, 800 Gbps inter-node): 170B model, 15 PFLOPs sustained (>30% of peak), 38 TFLOPS/GPU on 60B, 8x model size and 10x performance over Megatron-LM. Explicitly rejects CPU offload because PCIe bandwidth is 'severely constrained' - up to 50% of training time can go to GPU-CPU-GPU transfers. Notes that crossing a node boundary drops per-link bandwidth from 300 GB/s (NVSwitch) to 12.5 GB/s (InfiniBand EDR).
- `2026-10-27` [[pytorch-ddp-comm-hooks-default_hookspy-bf16fp16-compress-hooks]] — PyTorch DDP comm hooks: default_hooks.py (bf16/fp16 compress hooks) — PyTorch source for the DDP gradient-compression communication hooks. bf16_compress_hook casts each GradBucket to bfloat16, divides by world size before the collective (to avoid FP16 overflow), all-reduces in bf16, then copies the result back into the original fp32 buffer in place - halving gradient bytes on the wire (4 to 2 bytes per element) at the cost of mantissa precision. fp16_compress_hook is the same with float16; fp16_compress_wrapper and bf16_compress_wrapper wrap an arbitrary hook (e.g. PowerSGD) with the same cast. Both bf16 hooks carry the warning 'This API is experimental, and it requires NCCL version later than 2.9.6.' allreduce_hook is the reference no-op hook. Practical implication for a PCIe Gen2 x4 ring: compression is the cheapest available ~2x cut in DDP communication volume, and it composes with no_sync gradient accumulation.
- `2026-10-27` [[gradient-accumulation-with-ddp-no_sync-interface-distributed-pytorch-forums-2]] — Gradient accumulation with DDP no_sync interface - distributed - PyTorch Forums — PyTorch Forums thread (Jan 2023 - Jun 2024) on using DDP's no_sync() for gradient accumulation. A user accumulates 4 micro-batches of 64 to emulate batch 256 and observes a lower loss than a true 256 batch, suspecting hidden synchronization. Maintainer rvarm1 replies that the no_sync usage 'looks like correct usage' and warns optimizer.step() should run after gradients are allreduced or ranks desynchronize; a later reply (gilg) identifies the real bug - the snippet calls optimizer.zero_grad() and optimizer.step() on the sync iteration in a way that uses only that micro-batch's gradient, and accumulated gradients must be normalized by 1/4. Takeaway for this run: no_sync suppresses the per-micro-batch AllReduce but does not change gradient semantics - loss normalization and step placement stay the user's responsibility.
- `2026-10-27` [[gradient-accumulation-with-ddp-no_sync-interface-distributed-pytorch-forums]] — Gradient accumulation with DDP no_sync interface - distributed - PyTorch Forums — Deprecated: fetch of the GitHub blob URL for torch/distributed/algorithms/ddp_comm_hooks/default_hooks.py returned the PyTorch Forums thread instead of the source file (crawler mis-resolution). Superseded by pytorch-ddp-comm-hooks-default_hookspy-bf16fp16-compress-hooks.
- `2026-10-27` [[on-optimizing-the-communication-of-model-parallelism]] — ON OPTIMIZING THE COMMUNICATION OF MODEL PARALLELISM — Zhuang et al. (CMU / Tsinghua / Berkeley / MBZUAI): formalizes 'cross-mesh resharding', the many-to-many multicast that appears whenever intra-operator parallelism (TP) and inter-operator parallelism (PP) are combined - a sharded tensor must move from a source device mesh to a destination mesh and may change layout. Existing all-gather or P2P implementations are either sub-optimal or hard-coded to one parallelism plan; the paper proposes a broadcast-based communication system plus an overlap-friendly pipeline schedule. Microbenchmarks: up to 10x over existing approaches across tensor and mesh layouts; end-to-end multi-node training gains of 10% (GPT) and 50% (U-Transformer). Stated cluster assumptions: fast intra-node NVLink vs slow inter-node Ethernet/InfiniBand, fully-connected node topology with uniform pairwise bandwidth, host NIC as the bottleneck, full-duplex send/receive. Testbed is AWS p3.8xlarge nodes (4x V100-16GB) with 10 Gbps cross-node bandwidth. Relevant here because it isolates how much model-parallel cost is resharding traffic - exactly the traffic a PCIe-only, no-NVLink, no-P2P fabric makes dominant.
- `2026-10-27` [[zeropp-unleashing-exceptional-parallelism-efficiency]] — ZeroPP: Unleashing Exceptional Parallelism Efficiency — ZeroPP (Shanghai AI Lab / Tsinghua): a TP-free framework that combines pipeline parallelism with ZeRO-3/FSDP, adding a ZeRO-compatible breadth-first PP schedule, decoupled gradient computation for a near-zero pipeline bubble, and recomputation. Theory: TP costs 8Bbsh communication volume per transformer block (four all-reduces across forward and backward); ZeroPP costs 36Bh^2/U per iteration (two all-gathers plus one reduce-scatter per scheduling unit, each three times the ~12h^2 parameter size), so ZeroPP is cheaper whenever 2sUb > 9h - a condition easily met at long sequence lengths and large batches. Microbenchmark on 8 GPUs with a 6.2B GPT: ZeRO-3 communication volume and iteration time beat TP at every global batch size, with the gap widening as batch grows. End-to-end: up to 64 A800-80GB GPUs across 8 InfiniBand nodes; up to 33% faster than Megatron 3D parallelism at comparable memory; a 14.6B model on 32 GPUs with PP=4 and global batch 192 shows the advantage growing as the memory limit tightens. Directly supports 'drop TP, use PP+FSDP' on a low-bandwidth fabric.
- `2026-10-27` [[engram-conditional-memory-via-n-gram-lookups-vllm]] — Engram: conditional memory via n-gram lookups - vLLM — vLLM v0.31.0 Engram feature docs - the ground-truth runtime contract for serving Engram models. Engram layers are declared by the checkpoint (engram_layer_ids for DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash, ple_layer_ids for Qwen4Exp), not chosen at serve time; table sizes, n-gram size and head count are fixed by the checkpoint and not user-configurable. Hashing, lookup and gating run as fused Triton kernels over FP8 rows with ue8m0 block scales. Sizing fact that matters for a 64 GB-card box: DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash carries two Engram layers of roughly 384M table rows each with 256-byte FP8 entries plus block scales - about 200 GB of table weights alone. cpu_offload defaults to true (VLLM_PLE_CPU_OFFLOAD=true): tables live in pinned host memory and are served directly from the Triton kernel through unified virtual addressing, prefetched on a side CUDA stream to overlap with preceding layers; freed GPU memory goes to KV cache. CPU offload requires UVA and vLLM fails fast without it. Topologies: default embedding_across_dp=false keeps one TP-sharded table copy per DP replica (no cross-replica communication, one host copy per replica); embedding_across_dp=true shards hash heads across all TP x DP ranks into a single table copy, gathering hash ids of every co-located DP replica per step and exchanging rows back, trading per-step DP collectives for a much smaller table footprint. dp_shared_memory stores one copy of each TP shard in /dev/shm shared by co-located replicas via cudaHostRegister, dropping host memory from one copy per replica to one per node. Limitations: CUDA-alike only; DeepSeek V4.1 Engram does not support DBO or microbatching (must disable --enable-dbo and set --ubatch-size 0); embedding_across_dp unsupported with elastic expert parallelism.
- `2026-10-27` [[dr-robert-li-engram-a-critical-analysis-of-conditional-memory-as-a-sparsity-axis]] — Dr. Robert Li | Engram: A Critical Analysis of Conditional Memory as a Sparsity Axis - DeepSeek's Adaptation of Classic N-grams for Early Layer Feature Representation — Dr. Robert Li's long adversarial/pedagogical analysis of the Engram paper (19 Jan 2026, ~15.4k words). Mostly a section-by-section walkthrough plus a classical n-gram tutorial, but it contributes three things the primary paper does not state as bluntly. (1) The inference/training asymmetry, spelled out explicitly: prefetch-and-overlap works at inference because indices are known from token IDs before the forward pass, but training needs gradients for every accessed embedding to travel back to host memory, plus optimizer state (Adam momentum and variance) and gradient accumulation across distributed batches - 'training requires bidirectional, latency-sensitive communication that doesn't tolerate PCIe bottlenecks as gracefully'. (2) A memory-accounting worked example: a 100B model at FP16 needs 200 GB HBM (3x H100 80GB tensor-parallel or 2x H200 141GB), whereas an 8B backbone + 92B Engram keeps only ~16 GB on GPU with ~184 GB of tables in host DRAM, runnable on 1x RTX 4090 24GB + 256 GB host RAM - with the caveat that this buys memory capacity, not reasoning depth. (3) A gap table of claims validated vs asserted: the U-shape is tested at only two compute budgets (2e20, 6e20 FLOPs) while V3 uses ~1e25; log-linear Engram scaling is tested only to ~13B; the 100B offload claim is throughput-only with capability NOT tested at 100B; the multi-level Zipfian cache hierarchy is described with no empirical validation; context-extension gains are shown at 32k only, with no 128k or 1M-context testing. Also quantifies hash collision arithmetic (2e15 trigrams into 3e6 slots => ~700M n-grams per slot, ~11 collisions per head per batch, negligible across 8 heads).
- `2026-10-27` [[megablocks-efficient-sparse-training-with-mixture-of-experts]] — MEGABLOCKS: EFFICIENT SPARSE TRAINING WITH MIXTURE-OF-EXPERTS — MegaBlocks (Gale, Narayanan, Young, Zaharia; arXiv 2211.15841, Nov 2022): the canonical dropless MoE training system. Reformulates MoE computation as block-sparse matrix multiplication with variable-sized blocks, so no token is dropped and no expert batch is padded to a fixed capacity factor; reports up to 40% end-to-end speedup over Tutel and 2.4x over Megatron-LM dense at equal validation loss. Two facts matter for this run. First, MoE training does not require expert parallelism to be useful: the small-scale language-modeling study trains MoE-XS/Small/Medium on a SINGLE A100 80GB (batch 512 sequences, seq len 1024, 10B tokens), and only the larger comparison runs use 8x A100 with 8-way expert model parallelism plus data parallelism for non-MoE layers. Second, the padding/dropping tradeoff is quantified: capacity factor 1 loses 0.15 validation-loss reduction versus 0.26 when no tokens are dropped (1.73x larger gain), while avoiding drops more than doubles MoE-layer math; Tutel's padding cut the maximum micro-batch by 2x, 4x and 8x across the three model sizes. Scope: A100 SXM4 80GB, NVLink-class node, 2022 kernels — no PCIe-only or no-P2P measurements.
- `2026-10-27` [[deepseek-sparse-attention-dsa-nvidia-cudnn]] — DeepSeek Sparse Attention (DSA) — NVIDIA cuDNN — NVIDIA cuDNN Frontend "DeepSeek Sparse Attention (DSA)" module (docs.nvidia.com, cuDNN v1.28.0, experimental API subject to change). Ships CuTe-DSL kernels for the DSA TRAINING path: (1) Sparse Attention Backward (FlashMLA-shape, expects out/lse from FlashMLA or the PyTorch reference, takes topk_idxs INT32 and optional per-query topk_length, returns dq/dkv/d_sink); (2) Indexer Forward computing dense scores S[b,q,k] = sum_h ReLU(Q_h . K_h^T) . W_h with a ratio-causal mask and optional q_causal_offsets; (3) Combined Indexer Forward + Top-K (SM100-only compact path); (4) Indexer Top-K radix kernel with per-row seq_lens, top_k <= 2048; (5)-(7) Sparse/Dense Indexer and Attention Score Recompute producing the score tensors for the training loss; (8) Indexer Backward, a three-stage pipeline (in-place score-grad precompute from attn_score (target) and index_score (predict); three warp-specialised GEMMs producing d_index_q, d_weights and a dIndexK_f32 accumulator; dtype cast); (9) Dense Indexer Backward.

This is the primary confirmation of the repo's inferred training structure: the module documents an explicit "training-score loss path" in which attn_score and index_score feed IndexerBackward to produce d_index_q, d_weights, d_index_k — i.e. the indexer is trained by a distillation-style loss against main attention, NOT end-to-end through the non-differentiable top-k. There is also an opt-in SM100 sparse backward v2 backend with a two-term BF16 (hi+lo) expansion claimed ~675x lower gradient-matrix representation error.

Hard feasibility constraint for the CMP 170HX box: "Most kernels target Hopper (SM90) and Blackwell (SM100+) GPUs." Sparse Attention Backward, Score Recompute, Indexer Forward, Indexer Top-K and Indexer Backward all require SM90 or SM100; the combined compressed-logits+Top-K forward is SM100-only. CMP 170HX is GA100 (SM80), so NONE of these kernels run on the box — the only usable path is the pure-PyTorch reference (test/python/fe_api/dsa/dsa_reference.py::ref_sparse_attention_forward). Other constraints: indexer head_dim must be 128; H_kv = 1 (MQA); qhead_per_kv_head in {16,32,64} on SM90 and {32,64} on SM100; FP8/MXFP8 indexer backward unsupported (backward requires BF16 Q/K/W); the production sparse-attention forward is FlashMLA (C++) and is not integrated here. 3,658 words.
- `2026-10-27` [[multi-head-latentmoe-and-head-parallel]] — Multi-Head LatentMoE and Head Parallel: — Multi-Head LatentMoE and Head Parallel (Cui et al., Arizona State University, arXiv 2602.04870): an architecture + parallelism co-design that attacks the property that makes EP expensive on a slow fabric. EP's communication volume and all-to-all latency are O(k) in activated experts, traffic is load-imbalanced, and routing-dependent dispatch needs a metadata exchange to stay deterministic. Multi-Head LatentMoE splits each token into Nh sub-tokens, each with its own router and expert set, and Head Parallel distributes sub-tokens to GPUs BEFORE any routing decision, so each GPU routes and computes entirely locally. Result: O(1) communication volume regardless of k, perfectly balanced traffic, deterministic communication — and still composable with EP. At k=4 the communication volume is 25% of EP's. Trained on a 10B-token FineWeb-EDU subset on H100 80GB (NVLink): 1.11x faster at 2B total params (31.68 vs 35.36 h) and 1.61x faster at 4B (34.41 vs 55.34 h) at comparable quality, with IO-aware routing (online top-k in SRAM) and a pure-PyTorch dropless expert computation to avoid custom kernels. Relevance: this is the strongest architectural counterexample to 'expert parallelism is ruled out by the fabric' — MoE training can be made fabric-insensitive by moving the communication before routing, at the cost of a fixed per-layer all-to-all that is still host-staged on a no-P2P box.
- `2026-10-27` [[factorengram-factorized-n-gram-memory-with-basis-level]] — FactorEngram: Factorized N-gram Memory with Basis-Level — FactorEngram (Baidu, arXiv 2609.35578) - replaces Engram's monolithic per-n-gram embedding with sparsity-regularized coefficients over a dictionary of basis vectors shared across patterns, and reuses the same dictionary for basis-level contextual gating (the hidden state is scored against each basis vector to gate its coefficient before reconstruction). Also covers unigram as well as multi-token n-grams, and sweeps memory-branch insertion locations. Scale is small: 340M and 1B Transformer backbones trained from scratch on 30B and 120B tokens of FineWeb-Edu at 8K context with a 32K vocab; the 340M config carries 1B lookup-table parameters (bigram and trigram capacities 250K each) and the 1B config carries 2B lookup-table parameters (250K bigram / 750K trigram). Gains: at 340M WikiText perplexity 23.19 -> 21.29, LAMBADA 24.90 -> 21.69, +1.76 pp average downstream accuracy over the Transformer, and it beats a reproduction of Engram on all metrics, with the largest gains on 8K Needle-in-a-Haystack. Best insertion point is before attention in middle layers (10 and 12 in the reported config); sparsity coefficient lambda = 1e-3. Relevant as evidence that the Engram memory-table design space is still being actively refactored at sub-1B scale, and that table capacity can be traded against dictionary factorization rather than raw row count.
- `2026-10-27` [[memory-efficient-expert-routing-for-distributed-moe]] — Memory-Efficient Expert Routing for Distributed MoE — RelayMoE (Tarafder et al., William & Mary + BSC, arXiv 2610.07333): memory-efficient expert routing for distributed MoE training. Its diagnostic contribution is the most useful part for a 64 GB-card budget: peak memory in MoE training is set by the MoE block, not attention, because every intermediate buffer in the dispatch pipeline is scaled by top-k. Profiling a Qwen3-style model (top-8, 4 layers) at micro-batch 24 / 16384 tokens on 8x H100 with TP=SP=2, CP=4, EP=8 and full recompute, the MoE input activation is only 336 MiB per GPU but the top-k-expanded dispatch tensors are ~2.6 GiB each (8x expansion) and per-layer peak exceeds 42 GB during backward, with spikes to 51 GB against a 14 GB inter-spike baseline. RelayMoE replaces the single-shot all-to-all dispatcher with a ring that circulates expert weights or tokens, computes locally at each hop, and reconstructs expert intermediates hop-by-hop during backward so they can be freed. Results on 30B-57B production MoE models: 2x average single-layer speedup over Megatron-LM, up to 2.02x full-model throughput at the same memory budget, 3.8x average (7.6x peak) reduction in MoE execution memory, and up to 2.85x longer trainable sequence. Scope caveat: the ring relies on GPU-to-GPU peer-to-peer hops over NVLink 4.0 within a node and NDR InfiniBand across nodes — the exact capability the CMP 170HX box lacks, so the mechanism does not port, but the memory arithmetic does.
- `2026-10-27` [[indexcache-accelerating-sparse-attention-via-cross-layer]] — IndexCache: Accelerating Sparse Attention via Cross-Layer — IndexCache (Bai, Dong, Jiang, Lv, Du, Zeng, Tang, Li — Tsinghua + Z.ai, arXiv 2603.12201) exploits cross-layer redundancy in DeepSeek Sparse Attention: the lightning indexer retains O(L^2) complexity and must run independently at every layer even though the resulting top-k selections are highly similar across consecutive layers. IndexCache partitions layers into a small set of Full layers that run their own indexers and a majority of Shared layers that simply reuse the nearest Full layer's top-k indices. Two variants: training-free IndexCache (greedy search over which layers keep indexers, minimizing LM loss on a calibration set, no weight updates) and training-aware IndexCache (a multi-layer distillation loss training each retained indexer against the averaged attention distributions of all layers it serves; Proposition 1 shows gradient equivalence to distillation against the averaged distribution, so even simple interleaved patterns match full-indexer accuracy).

Results: on a 30B DSA model across nine long-context and reasoning benchmarks, removing 75% of indexer computations gives negligible quality degradation, with up to 1.82x prefill speedup and 1.48x decode speedup at 200K context (decode gains because DSA runs a per-token indexer pass every step). Preliminary results on production-scale GLM-5: 50% indexer removal with comparable long-context/reasoning performance and ~1.2x end-to-end speedup. The paper also documents the standard DSA training recipe: a short dense warm-up trains ONLY the indexer via KL-divergence distillation against the full-attention distribution, after which sparse training proceeds with the indexer receiving distillation gradients on a detached computational graph. 8,771 words.
- `2026-10-27` [[engramedit-decoupled-knowledge-updates]] — EngramEdit: Decoupled Knowledge Updates — EngramEdit (HK PolyU + Hangzhou Diagens, arXiv 2610.10533, 8 Oct 2026) - turns conditional memory into an editable knowledge interface while keeping the Transformer backbone fixed. Computes target memory representations that make the model predict an updated fact across multiple expressions, then jointly solves for updates to the shared n-gram embeddings, penalizing updates to frequently reused embeddings more strongly to preserve unrelated knowledge. Reports near-perfect editing success on CounterFact and ZsRE, generalization to unseen expressions and multi-hop reasoning at nearly 3x the strongest baseline's accuracy, and preserved general capability across 5,000 sequential edits. Experimental substrate is LongCat-Flash-Lite, a 68.5B-parameter MoE with 31.4B parameters in n-gram embeddings (suffix n-grams of length 2, 3, 4; four hashed sub-tables per length; 256-dim per sub-table projected to 3,072-dim token embedding space), run on two NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 GPUs. Editing keeps the pretrained hashed tables FIXED and stores a separate cumulative update vector per edited n-gram indexed by exact token sequence, so storage scales with the number of distinct edited n-grams and hash collisions cannot couple updates. Relevant to the query as evidence that frozen Engram tables are an established, working pattern - but this is an inference-time editing result, not a training-topology result.
- `2026-10-27` [[gpu-initiated-communication-dissecting-down-to-the-bone]] — GPU-Initiated Communication: Dissecting Down to the Bone — GPU-Initiated Communication: Dissecting Down to the Bone (Baydamirli et al., Koc University + fal, arXiv 2610.01380): a mechanism-level dissection of GPU-initiated RDMA at the GPU-NIC boundary, separating hardware cost from library overhead. Builds mini-gda and mini-proxy minimal transports and measures them against NVSHMEM IBGDA, NCCL GIN, DeepEP, UCCL-EP, MSCCL++ and fabric-lib on H100/H200/B200/GB200 with ConnectX-7 InfiniBand. Findings: a minimal GPU path issues an 8 B write in 0.7 us and completes in 4.0 us; libraries add up to 4.6 us of issue time (GDAKI 1.6 us, NVSHMEM public API 5.3 us); ordering scope alone changes issue time 3.7x (system-scope fence 2.62 us vs GPU-scope 0.70 us); a tuned CPU proxy matches the GPU path at idle (5.9 vs 6.9 us median round trip) but needs a dedicated core; sharing a queue with bulk traffic raises latency one to three orders of magnitude; peak 260 M msg/s needs doorbell batching and queue parallelism, and all-to-all traffic loses 59% of its NIC message rate at ~3,000 connections; communication code can cost up to 37% of a kernel's useful throughput even when dormant. Relevance to this run is indirect: it is an InfiniBand/NVLink-class-fabric study, not a PCIe-only consumer-GPU study, and it confirms that the GPU-initiated transports (IBGDA, GDAKI, NCCL GIN, DeepEP) that MoE EP libraries depend on require a NIC plus peer-addressable memory — neither of which a no-P2P CMP 170HX box provides.
- `2026-10-27` [[longcat-sparse-attention-taming-the-lightning-via]] — LongCat Sparse Attention: Taming the Lightning via — LongCat Sparse Attention (LSA; Zan et al., Meituan LongCat Team, arXiv 2608.01662) is a hardware-algorithm co-design of the DSA lineage with three orthogonal components: Streaming-Aware Indexing (SI — converts scattered KV entries into hardware-aligned contiguous layouts for coalesced HBM access), Cross-Layer Indexing (CLI — one layer's indexer result reused across consecutive layers, supported by cross-layer distillation), and Hierarchical Indexing (HI — coarse-to-fine candidate narrowing, training-free and plug-and-play). Scaling experiments span 69B-A3B to 560B-A27B models; LSA supports native training to 1M context and underpins LongCat-2.0 (1.6T-A48B); LongCat-Flash-Lite-Sparse (69B-A3B) is open-sourced.

Measured facts that bear directly on the training-cost question: (a) the sparse-attention operator is severely memory-bound — about 4.5% of peak HBM bandwidth (~1/22) because of discontinuous gather patterns (~6% memory-level parallelism, ~75% cacheline packing efficiency); (b) the backward pass is worse than forward because gradient accumulation allocates a full-sequence-length HBM buffer and executes scatter_add over the selected sparse index set, and overlapping indices across cores serialize writes to shared cachelines; (c) the indexer accounts for up to 90% of per-layer latency at 1024K context, and indexer latency grows 27x from 0.034 ms to 0.930 ms while sparse-attention latency stays ~0.10 ms; (d) at 32K sequence length LSA training is a NET LOSS versus dense MLA — 1.08x forward, 0.79x backward, 0.83x total — with crossover at 64K (1.39x), 2.21x at 128K, 3.39x at 256K, 7.73x at 1024K, and the practical crossover moves to 128K once variable-length packing is accounted for; (e) core-attention training latency at qlen=8192, K=2048, BF16: SFA fwd 23.64 ms / bwd 238.60 ms at 32K KV (backward ~10x forward), HFA 19.70 / 137.77 ms; HFA accelerates core attention up to 1.91x forward and 1.73x backward.

Index-quality and training-recipe facts: adjacent layers share 57.4% of their Top-K budget and reusing an adjacent layer's index set still captures 93.2% of the target layer's attention mass; K=2048 at 128K context gives ~98.4% sparsity with near-lossless quality; sink+SWA regions capture ~83% of attention mass on average across layers. Training is two-stage: a dense warm-up where the indexer is supervised against the full-sequence attention distribution, then sparse training where the distillation target is the attention distribution renormalized over the selected subset; the fixed-to-sparse budget ratio is ~1:1 (Kswa=1024, Ksparse=1024, Ksink=16), and during training the sink/SWA tokens are deliberately included in the distillation target because they carry substantial attention mass. Cross-layer index reuse without training adaptation degrades quality, which is why CLI requires cross-layer distillation in both the dense warm-up and sparse stages. HI is training-free and only enabled beyond a crossover point (net slowdown of 0.79-0.82x below it). 30,152 words — long source, flag for source-analyst.
- `2026-10-27` [[variopath-workload-aware-all-to-all-communication-for-pcie-gpu-clusters]] — VarioPath: Workload-Aware All-to-All Communication for PCIe GPU Clusters — VarioPath (Fei et al., USTC + Tsinghua, arXiv 2609.34340): workload-aware AlltoAllv scheduling for PCIe GPU clusters — the primitive that EP dispatch/combine and sequence parallelism are built from. It documents why PCIe all-to-all is hard: no dedicated scale-up fabric means intra-node GPU traffic and NIC traffic share the same PCIe hierarchy, so concurrent transfers contend on bottleneck links; demand is skewed (top-k routing) and changes every layer (sub-millisecond per layer), defeating static schedules. VarioPath splits an offline topology-aware analyzer (records contention-free transfer patterns as channels, exploits topology symmetry) from an online demand-aware scheduler. On four platforms up to 256 GPUs it averages 5.88x over FAST and 1.72x over DeepEP (1.55-2.00x geomean over DeepEP on random-demand AlltoAllv, 3.73-10.61x over NCCL), reaching 30.91 GB/s algorithmic bandwidth at 256 GPUs vs 28.11 (DeepEP) and 18.85 (NCCL). End-to-end: Qwen3-30B-A3B prefill latency -19.1% to -27.2% at EP16/24/32, Wan2.1 generation -1.1% to -6.6%. Ablation: fixing either peer matching or path choice roughly doubles completion time (2.03x / 2.00x), fixing both 2.84x — peer-path co-design is not optional. Testbeds are Gen4/Gen5 x16 with 32-64 GB/s per GPU, so the absolute GB/s figures do not transfer to a Gen2 x4 box; the structural findings do.
- `2026-10-27` [[lisa-linear-indexed-sparse-attention-for-efficient]] — LISA: Linear-Indexed Sparse Attention for Efficient — LISA (Zhao et al., Alibaba International Digital Commerce + Dalian University of Technology + Amazon, arXiv 2607.19358) is a plug-and-play attention replacement that requires NO pretraining from scratch: a Linear Attention branch (O(n) time, long-range memory) runs in parallel with a Lightning Indexer that selects the top-M tokens for a Sparse Self-Attention, the two branches fused by a gating mechanism, reducing inference complexity from O(n^2) to O(nM) for generating n tokens.

Two-stage training: Stage 1 cold-starts the linear attention plus a sliding-window attention mechanism, optimized by knowledge distillation to approximate the full self-attention distribution of a frozen teacher; Stage 2 introduces the Indexer to replace the static sliding window, trained with a per-head KL divergence loss aligning its selection behaviour with the teacher's attention patterns. All original model parameters are frozen — only the newly added linear-attention and Indexer modules train. Because top-M is argmax, gradients cannot flow back through the selection; early in training they select top-1.2M candidates and randomly mask out 20%, gradually reducing the dropout ratio, and apply z-loss to the Indexer.

Budget analysis: MASS (attention mass captured) reaches 80% at M=256, only 6% below the theoretical oracle upper bound; 70% at M=128 and 88% at M=512, so M=256 is the recommended balance. Results on DeepSeek-distilled Qwen2.5-1.5B/7B: 50% inference speedup at 16K context with +5.6% average reasoning accuracy; on Qwen2.5-7B, 70.6% average accuracy vs 263.3 -> 226.6 relative latency measured on a single A100 (batch 1, BF16, eager mode). Training cost: each of the two stages takes approximately 240 GPU-hours on an NVIDIA H100 (~480 GPU-hours total) for the 7B retrofit, with Stage 1 at LR 2e-5, batch 128, 2 epochs, 50-step warmup. 10,747 words.
- `2026-10-27` [[tf-engram-a-train-free-engram-with-ssd-backed-memory-for]] — TF-Engram: A Train-Free Engram with SSD-Backed Memory for — TF-Engram (CUHK + BIT Zhuhai, arXiv 2607.07388) - a train-free Engram that builds phrase-specific semantic memory offline from an external corpus, stores tables across a GPU-DRAM-SSD hierarchy, and uses Early-Exit Guided Predictive Prefetching to hide external-memory latency during autoregressive decoding. Backbone stays frozen; memory is injected as a hidden-state pathway. Quality: on Qwen3-0.6B the average downstream score rises 57.6 -> 59.4, beating both the frozen backbone and a parameter-matched LoRA baseline. Storage numbers that matter for capacity planning: a 100M-entry table with 1024-dim FP16 vectors is 215.86 GB on disk; a GPU-resident 10M-entry table inflates GPU memory to 24.03 GB, whereas the SSD-backed 100M-entry table uses only 3.06 GB of GPU memory and shifts the burden to CPU DRAM and NVMe. Offline construction cost is modest: 1.612 h for n-gram extraction, 0.231 h embedding generation, 0.157 h index construction. Runtime cost on the testbed (RTX 5090 32 GB, 64 GB host RAM, 516 GB SSD, i9-14900K): baseline 481.2 tok/s and 2.08 ms/token; SSD-backed without prefetch drops to 439.2 tok/s; Top-64 prefetch recovers to 460.3 tok/s, Top-128 to 462.4 tok/s. Decode latency 2.108 ms/token at 1M entries -> 2.175 ms/token at 100M entries (474.6 -> 459.8 tok/s). Best early-exit layer is 8. Note this is a single-consumer-GPU inference result, not a training result.
- `2026-10-27` [[democratizing-moe-inference-on-commodity-gpus]] — Democratizing MoE inference on commodity GPUs — CoMoE (Fan et al., Tsinghua + Alibaba Cloud, arXiv 2610.09424): host-centric MoE inference on commodity GPUs. Its measurement section is the cleanest quantification of how much a no-P2P PCIe fabric costs MoE: on 8x RTX 5090 (PCIe 5.0 x16, 64 GB/s nominal per GPU vs 400 GB/s NVLink = 0.16x), inter-GPU communication accounts for 81.6% of total MoE execution time, and naive deployment reaches only 61% of the throughput of an FLOP-matched datacenter GPU. CoMoE elevates host DRAM from a passive bounce buffer to an active routing hub — host-backed token multicast for dispatch (each shared token written once, receivers pull in parallel) and token-level host-staged aggregation for combine (replacing the global All-to-All barrier with a completion-driven pipeline that absorbs stragglers). Results: 1.24-1.41x throughput per model (up to 1.46x), 85-90% of an 8x A800 + DeepEP configuration at 23.4% of the hardware cost, TTFT -18.8% and TPOT -26.9% on average, combine latency 2.46x geometric-mean faster than NCCL All-to-All. Ablations show the win comes from decomposing All-to-All into one-sided transfers (1.55x) plus NUMA-aware buffers, thread-tile pipelining and batched commit (to 2.46x). Scope: inference, not training; 8 GPUs on Gen5 x16 — a far faster link than Gen2 x4.
- `2026-10-27` [[streamindex-memory-bounded-compressed-sparse]] — StreamIndex: Memory-Bounded Compressed Sparse — StreamIndex (Jaber & Jaber, RightNow AI, arXiv 2605.02568) identifies the memory bottleneck that actually gates CSA-class sparse attention at long context: public CSA implementations materialize a [B, S, HI, T] FP32 indexer score tensor before the top-k reduction. With V4-Flash dimensions (HI=64 indexer heads, compression m=4 so T=S/4), that intermediate is 256 GB at S=65,536 and 4 TB at S=262,144; at S=32,768 the pre-head-sum intermediate alone is 64 GB and measured peak HBM is 129 GB on a 140 GB H200. Every public CSA pipeline the authors know of OOMs on a single H200 for S >= 64K before the top-k has been selected — the indexer step, not the attention kernel, gates maximum sequence length ("Assuming attention is the bottleneck at long context is incorrect for CSA: the pipeline runs out of memory in the indexer step before attention runs").

The fix is a Triton chunked partition-merge top-k driver (151 lines, chunked_indexer.py) exploiting per-key separability of the lightning-indexer score (no softmax coupling across keys), so per-tile top-k can be merged; a per-chunk causal mask keeps mask memory at O(cS*cT) instead of O(S*T). Measured on one H200 (140 GB HBM3e, BF16, CUDA 13, Triton 3.7): materialize OOMs at S=65,536; chunked runs to S=1,048,576 at 6.21 GB peak HBM (32x regime extension). At S=32,768 where both fit, chunked is 10.3x faster (31 ms vs 317 ms) at 0.40 GB vs 129 GB. Set-overlap recall vs the materialize reference is bit-exact at small S and min >= 0.9980 across three 5-point design-space sweeps; FP32 score accumulation is required (FP16 drops perfect-recall rows from 99.99% to 91.82%); chunk-size knee at cS=2048; single-chunk over T optimal when memory allows. Composed with TileLang's pipelined attention at S=262,144, the materialize indexer OOMs while the chunked indexer runs in 1.97 s at 18.56 GB peak (1.83x lower peak HBM at S=131,072). Scope limits: synthetic-but-realistic inputs (no V4 checkpoint fits on one H200 — 270 GB FP8 weights), indexer step only, explicit non-claim of a faster attention kernel or real-checkpoint end-to-end behaviour. Code: github.com/RightNow-AI/StreamIndex.
- `2026-10-27` [[efficient-training-on-multiple-consumer-gpus-with]] — Efficient Training on Multiple Consumer GPUs with — RoundPipe (Luo et al., Tsinghua, SIGOPS ATC 2026, arXiv 2604.27085): pipeline parallelism + CPU offloading for LLM fine-tuning on consumer GPUs. Directly relevant to the CMP 170HX topology question because RoundPipe uses NO GPU peer-to-peer communication at all — it relies entirely on PCIe host-to-device transfers under its Computation Dispatch Paradigm, and still reaches 1.48-2.16x the throughput of the fastest baselines (ZeRO-2, FSDP, ZeRO-Infinity, Megatron-PP, Megatron-TP, Mobius) on 8x RTX 4090 (PCIe 4.0, 32 GB/s) for 1.7B-32B models, and 76%+ of the same frameworks on 8x A800. It quantifies the DDP/ZeRO penalty on this class of fabric: DeepSpeed spends ~70% of training time on communication on consumer GPU servers, and Megatron-TP's throughput 'becomes impractical under PCIe'. Memory anchors: an 8B model needs 128 GB for model states alone (vs 24 GB per 4090), host memory footprint is ~20x parameter size, activation recomputation is 2.37-5.75x faster than reloading activations over PCIe, and max trainable sequence length is independent of GPU count (73K/49K/39K/28K/31K for the five models) because stage-boundary activations live in host DRAM. Bubble ratio falls below 4.5% with asynchronous optimizer updates; near-linear scaling from 1 to 8 GPUs; LoRA fine-tunes Qwen3-235B at 31K sequence length on 24 GB cards.
- `2026-10-27` [[pooling-engram-conditional-memory-in-large]] — Pooling Engram Conditional Memory in Large — CXL memory pooling for Engram tables (EuroMLSys April 2026, Peking Univ + Alibaba Cloud, arXiv 2603.10087). Inference-only study; integrates a CXL-based Engram pool into SGLang and shows near-DRAM end-to-end throughput. Access profile of Engram is the load-bearing part: read-only and immutable during inference, sparse (only ~5 KB fetched per token per layer = 16 discrete embeddings of 320 bytes each for N-gram=2 with 8 hash heads), and latency-tolerant because hash indices depend only on token IDs so retrieval can start before the forward pass. Bandwidth is NOT the bottleneck: at 70,000 tok/s with 2 Engram layers the pool needs only ~0.7 GB/s, easily served by PCIe Gen5 (64 GB/s) or 100GbE (12.5 GB/s). LATENCY is the bottleneck: with t_step~3.6 ms over 64 layers, t_exec~56 us per layer, so an Engram module at layer 2 has a ~56 us prefetch window. RDMA pooling (Mooncake Store) incurs latency orders of magnitude above local DRAM; CXL pooling tracks local DRAM closely (CXL->CPU comparable to DRAM, CXL->GPU higher but acceptable). Configs profiled: Engram-27B (vocab_size 2,262,400; emb_dim 1,280) and Engram-40B (vocab_size 7,239,680; emb_dim 1,280). Testbed: 2 nodes, dual Xeon 6766E (288 cores), 216 MiB L3, 2x NVIDIA L20, 1 TiB DRAM each, XConn XC50256 CXL switch (256 PCIe 5.0 lanes, 512 GB/s, up to 8 servers to a 4 TB pool), 256 GB Samsung CXL card. End-to-end (single L20, DP=1, batch 256, 512-token in/out): Qwen3-4B 6,183.9 baseline -> 5,683.7 +Engram(DRAM) -> 5,614.4 +Engram(CXL); Qwen3-8B 4,185.6 -> 3,909.7 -> 3,895.0 tok/s. Note the paper emulates Engram on Qwen3 models because DeepSeek Engram weights are undisclosed.
- `2026-10-27` [[hysparse2-hybrid-sparse-attention]] — HySparse2: Hybrid Sparse Attention — HySparse2 (Wei, Gao, Zhang et al., Xiaomi LLM-Core, arXiv 2609.26368) is a hybrid sparse attention architecture with two-level KV sharing, evaluated on an 80B-A3B MoE. Outer level: KV Bridging adopts a YOCO-style self-decoder/cross-decoder structure but bridges only full-attention layers — the KV caches for cross-decoder full-attention layers are generated from the hidden states of self-decoder full-attention layers, so prefill can exit after the self-decoder and skip all cross-decoder layers. Inner level: KV Reuse (HySparse's core design) with two refinements — block-level sparsity replaced by token-level sparsity for finer long-context retrieval, and the separate SWA branch removed in favour of forcing a sliding window of recent tokens into the sparse selection. Selection budget: 128 forced local tokens + 1,024 global tokens.

Training recipe (directly relevant as a 32k precedent): the 80B-A3B models are pretrained on approximately 500B tokens at a context length of 32k, then a light post-training stage adds ~100B tokens, introduces agentic data, and extends context to 256k. All three compared models share the same data mixture and schedule; Muon optimizer with a WSD schedule and peak learning rates of 1e-3 (pretraining) / 5e-5 (post-training). After pretraining HySparse2 retains broadly comparable general capabilities and improves long-context retrieval; after light post-training it improves mean MRCR-style retrieval and agentic multi-turn performance.

Cost results: at 1M tokens HySparse2 reduces prefill FLOPs by 2.92x relative to HySparse and 5.02x relative to Hybrid SWA, with KV cache of only 2.69 GB versus 6.72 GB (HySparse) and 12.09 GB (Hybrid SWA) under FP8 KV storage. Ablations show token-level selection beats block-level. 6,151 words.
- `2026-10-27` [[amsp-reducing-communication-overhead-of]] — AMSP: Reducing Communication Overhead of — AMSP (NTU / Shanghai AI Lab / SenseTime): a ZeRO/FSDP sharding-strategy optimizer. Each model-state component (parameters, gradients, optimizer states) independently picks Full-Replica, Full-Sharding or Partial-Sharding over a chosen device mesh; AMSP formulates a communication-cost minimization and overlaps collectives with compute. Motivation numbers: LLaMA-7B model states need 112 GB, more than an 80 GB A100; ZeRO-1 MFU is 63% at 8 GPUs but 36% at 1024 GPUs at the same batch; LLaMA-13B ZeRO-3 MFU falls from 47% to 4% going from 8 to 1024 GPUs. Three stated causes: (1) intra/inter-node bandwidth gap - DGX-A100 gives 600 GB/s intra-node bidirectional per GPU vs 400 GB/s inter-node per node, ratio 2 in their testbed; (2) collective latency grows with scale, measured as NCCL effective bandwidth from 8x1 to 8x64 GPU meshes over 4x800 Gbps InfiniBand HDR; (3) a fixed global batch (4M tokens held constant from 8 to 512 GPUs) shrinks per-GPU micro-batches and destroys the compute-to-communication ratio. Result: up to 52% MFU on 1024 GPUs and 1.56x throughput vs MiCS and ZeRO++.
- `2026-10-27` [[vescale-fsdp-flexible-and-high-performance-fsdp-at]] — veScale-FSDP: Flexible and High-Performance FSDP at — ByteDance Seed (arXiv 2602.22437, Apr 2026): FSDP reimplementation built on RaggedShard (block-aligned sharding) plus a structure-aware planning algorithm, giving zero-copy collectives and native support for block-wise quantization and non-element-wise optimizers (Shampoo, Muon). Claims 5-66% higher throughput and 16-30% lower memory than existing FSDP systems while scaling to 10K GPUs. The load-bearing statement for this run: 'the communication cost of FSDP and the computation cost per GPU remain constant with respect to the number of GPUs, depending only on the model and input sizes' - so at a fixed global batch, adding GPUs shrinks per-GPU tokens and lets parameter AllGather plus gradient ReduceScatter dominate runtime. Linear strong scaling holds at a 120M-token global batch up to 10K GPUs, and 3.4x from 1K to 8K GPUs at a 16M-token batch; 2.4T parameters train on 1K GPUs with no MFU degradation. Testbed: 8x H800 per node (979 BF16 TFLOPS, 80 GB HBM, 400 GB/s NVLink). Practical guidance: avoid oversized FSDP groups, prefer HSDP; FSDP1 has slow ReduceScatter, FSDP2 has tensor-copy overhead, Megatron-FSDP pays padding cost.
- `2026-10-27` [[kv-kaizen-learning-context-adaptive]] — KV-Kaizen: Learning Context-Adaptive — KV-Kaizen (Monteiro, Bethune, Filippova, Laguna, Grangier, Cuturi — Apple, arXiv 2609.37988) learns a context-adaptive per-layer KV cache configuration along three axes — depth (sharing one cache across layers, i.e. inheriting an earlier layer's cache), precision (fewer bits, {2,4,8,16}), and rank (truncating the MLA latent representation) — under a single bits-per-token budget. A small selector reads the prompt once before prefill and picks one of 17 actions per layer; the configuration then holds for every generated token, and selector and model are trained together during supervised fine-tuning so the model adapts to the cache it will be served under. The budget can itself be an input, so one selector serves several compression targets.

Findings: applying any single axis uniformly across all layers limits achievable compression because it degrades accuracy, whereas composing mild settings locally and adaptively preserves accuracy at much higher compression. A 4x cache reduction incurs no accuracy degradation from 7B parameters up; on Qwen2.5-14B at 16k, 4x cache compression composed with eviction to one position in eight yields a 32x smaller decode-time cache while preserving accuracy (the two ratios multiply). Compressed models are more accurate than smaller uncompressed models with the same cache size, and selectors reach the Pareto frontier of accuracy vs cache size against learning-free and post-hoc baselines. Theoretical maximal compression for a 48-layer model such as Qwen2.5-14B is 4108x (8x precision x 10.7 lowest rank x 48 depth), though all experiments target far lower factors.

Relevance to the CSA2 question: this is the inference-side counterpoint — substantial KV compression (4x with no loss from 7B up, 32x composed with eviction) is achievable post hoc, without architectural sparse attention and without from-scratch sparse pretraining. The paper's own framing is "these findings support pre-training large models and compressing them only afterwards." 16,061 words.
- `2026-10-27` [[memory-grafting-scaling-language-model]] — Memory Grafting: Scaling Language Model — Memory Grafting (Tsinghua + Microsoft Research Asia, arXiv 2605.20948v1, 20 May 2026) - the paper that actually introduces FROZEN Engram memory tables. Instead of learning the n-gram table from scratch, it runs a donor 'grafting model' (Qwen3.5-35B-A3B, DeepSeek-V2-Lite, or GLM-4.7-Flash) offline over the top-1M most frequent n-grams per order (2-, 3-, 4-gram => 3M entries per grafted layer), stores the donor's final-token hidden state in bf16 as a frozen table W_mem, and retrieves it by exact longest-match suffix lookup; the table is explicitly NOT updated during recipient training - only the recipient-side projections and gates are learned. A hash-based Engram table is retained as a TRAINABLE fallback for miss positions, so the memory subsystem is only partially frozen. Scale of frozen memory: ~12 GB per grafted layer for Qwen3.5-35B-A3B, and the 0.92B-trainable recipient carries approximately 12B frozen grafting memory, offloaded. Results: 2.8B-total/0.55B-activated recipient, 100B tokens - average benchmark 51.95 (MoE) / 52.43 (vanilla Engram) -> 53.86 (MG-Qwen3.5), with LAMBADA 42.98->48.19, BoolQ 57.92->62.54, WinoGrande 58.98->60.93. 0.92B-trainable/0.29B-activated recipient, 50B tokens: MoE 45.62, vanilla Engram 45.03 (underperforms at this scale), MG-DeepSeek 46.42, MG-Qwen3.5 46.98, MG-GLM 45.83. Efficiency: 0.92x training throughput, 0.95x inference throughput, 0.97x inference memory versus the MoE baseline. Motivation stated explicitly: Engram training efficiency declines once the memory module exceeds ~70% of total parameters, so frozen grafted memory decouples capacity growth from recipient training cost.
- `2026-10-27` [[efficient-expert-parallel-communication]] — Efficient Expert-Parallel Communication — ThunderEP (Lee et al., Seoul National University, arXiv 2609.40093): an EP communication design for MoE inference on PCIe consumer GPUs with no P2P. Establishes the fabric mechanics the CMP 170HX box shares: without P2P every inter-GPU byte is host-staged and crosses PCIe twice (sender writes to pinned host buffer, receiver reads it back), so NCCL's ring All-Gather moves 2(N-1)S bytes per GPU versus N*S for a single-step host broadcast — a ratio of 2(N-1)/N, i.e. 1.6x at N=5. Measured PCIe contention on 6x RTX 5090 (Gen5 x16): one GPU alone reaches 56.5 GB/s, but two GPUs under the same host bridge communicating concurrently drop to 35.5 GB/s each, so per-GPU bandwidth falls as more GPUs join a collective. ThunderEP (single-step host broadcast, DMA-engine transfers, per-sender completion flags) beats NCCL by 2.00x on dispatch and 1.53x on combine, 1.66x end-to-end prefill and 1.26x decode; ablation TTFT 2115.9 ms -> 1436.4 ms. Scope limits: inference only (no gradient collectives), and every existing EP library (DeepEP, pplx-kernels, NCCL EP, UCCL-EP, NIXL) assumes NVLink, GPUDirect P2P or RDMA and therefore cannot run on this fabric at all.
- `2026-10-27` [[deepseek-v41-flash]] — DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash: — DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash technical report (DeepSeek-AI, arXiv 2609.19969v1, 17 Sep 2026): a 552B-backbone multimodal MoE with a Causal Encoder-Decoder (CED) split of 20 encoder + 20 decoder layers, activating 8B params/token in prefill and 16B in decode, pretrained on 45T tokens at a fixed 100.6M-token batch. CSA2 is the paper's central KV-compression mechanism and differs materially from the repo's description of it: it is NOT a per-layer indexer running in parallel everywhere. Each CSA2 layer is statically assigned one of three modes — Full (own main KV, own indexer Q, fresh Top-K), Reindex (borrows a preceding layer's main KV + indexer K, rescores with its own indexer Q), Reuse (borrows both KV and the latest Top-K indices, no indexer at all). In the actual 40-layer configuration only 4 layers run a Full-mode indexer (3 encoder + 1 decoder), 4 run Reindex indexers, 27 are Reuse (zero indexer work), and the first 2 layers are pure SWA — 67.5% of layers execute no indexer at all. Hyperparameters: attention top-k = 512 KV entries per query; indexer 32 query heads, head dim 128; main attention 64 heads, head dim 512, query compression dim 1280; SWA window 128; compression ratio m=2 in encoder layers and m=1 in decoder layers; Hierarchical Sparse Indexer selects at most 2,048 blocks of 8 positions = 16,384 candidate positions. The hierarchical indexer is "used only in the decoder of CED to reduce this repeated scoring during decode" and is "introduced in post-training", with the candidate restriction applied identically during training and inference so deeper indexers are optimized under the same search domain they use at inference; the first Full-mode layer still scans the entire causally visible range. Result: 890 bytes/token global KV cache (~1/4 of V4-Flash) and nearly flat decode FLOPs (256-fold context growth 4K→1M raises decode FLOPs only 1/4).

Training-side facts that bear directly on the repo's CSA2-at-32k claim: (1) sparse attention is trained FROM SCRATCH at sequence length 64K with NO dense-attention warm-up stage, then extended to 1M at 34T tokens — so 32k is below the paper's trained regime, not a natural operating point; (2) FP4 global KV caches are used DURING TRAINING with only marginal performance degradation (QAT extended from the FP4 indexer Q/K to the main KV cache, MXFP4/NVFP4-style block scaling); (3) distributed CSA2 training needs extra machinery the repo does not budget for — "shadow indexers" (a lightweight executable replica per pipeline stage with a single logical owner, kept consistent by parameter sync and gradient aggregation), pipeline payload extensions carrying intermediate representations and sparse routing information across stage boundaries, and micro-batch-level shared-state management coordinating state lifetimes across forward, activation recomputation and backward; (4) Engram tables are partitioned by row across engram-parallel process groups, optimizer state is sharded across replicas, embeddings are stored/fetched in FP8, prefetch is initiated for the whole local batch before each pipeline stage, and embedding gradients are buffered during backward and returned to owning ranks after the backbone backward — Engram LR is scaled 5x. Optimizer mix: Muon for linear transforms, AdamW for RMSNorm/non-matrix params, Sinkhorn-balanced update for embeddings and prediction head. Reuse-mode layers execute with only 15 kernels during prefill and 11 during decode. 24,845 words — long source, flag for source-analyst.
- `2026-10-27` [[conditional-memory-via-scalable-lookup]] — Conditional Memory via Scalable Lookup: — DeepSeek-AI/PKU canonical Engram paper (arXiv 2601.07372v2, 12 Jul 2026). Introduces conditional memory as a second sparsity axis: hashed suffix n-gram lookups into large static embedding tables, gated by the backbone hidden state and fused residually, inserted only at selected layers (layers 2 and 15 in the 27B model). Sparsity-allocation law: with P_tot and P_act fixed, validation loss is U-shaped in the MoE/Engram split, optimum at rho~75-80% MoE (loss 1.7248 -> 1.7109 at C=6e20 FLOPs), i.e. 20-25% of the inactive-parameter budget should go to Engram. Engram-27B = 26.7B total / 3.8B activated / 5.7B Engram params, 55 routed + 2 shared experts, 30 blocks, hidden 2560, MLA 32 heads, mHC M=4, Muon, DeepSeek-V3 128k tokenizer, 262B tokens; beats iso-param iso-FLOPs MoE-27B on MMLU +3.4, CMMLU +4.0, BBH +5.0, ARC-C +3.7, HumanEval +3.0, MATH +2.4, GSM8K +2.2. Long-context: YaRN extension to 32768 tokens for 5,000 steps (30B tokens); Multi-Query NIAH 97.0 vs 84.2, Variable Tracking 89.0 vs 77.0. CRITICAL FOR THIS QUERY: Engram tables are TRAINED, not frozen - the paper shards them across GPUs with model parallelism and uses All-to-All to gather active rows in forward and dispatch gradients in backward, and optimizes the embedding tables with Adam at 5x LR and no weight decay. Host-DRAM offload with prefetch-and-overlap is an INFERENCE-only mechanism (Fig. 2b): a 100B-parameter Engram table resident in host DRAM costs <3% throughput on H800 (4B-dense 9,031.62 -> 8,858.28 tok/s; 8B-dense 6,315.52 -> 6,140.02 tok/s, 2.8%), because effective transfer volume scales with activated slots, not table size. The multi-level Zipfian cache hierarchy (HBM/DRAM/NVMe) is described but not benchmarked.
